Positive Logic: An Introduction for Model Theorists
Mark Kamsma

Abstract




Abstract

Positive logic is a generalisation of full first-order logic that does not have negation built in. Still, many model-theoretic ideas, tools and techniques work perfectly fine in positive logic. Importantly, there is a compactness theorem. With some care, many classical results hold in the generality of positive logic without giving up any strength.

In these self-contained notes we give an introduction to model theory in positive logic. We give a complete treatment of the basics of positive model theory and then we move on to deeper model-theoretic concepts. First, we discuss countable categoricity, where we work towards a theorem that characterises countably categorical positive theories. After that, we briefly discuss how the convenient formalism of monster models goes through in positive logic as usual. This is helpful in the remainder of the notes, where we discuss simple and stable theories. The main aim in those chapters is to develop dividing independence and prove Kim-Pillay style theorems. For a smoother treatment we assume thickness, which is the relatively mild assumption that being an indiscernible sequence is type-definable. We finish by discussing two big applications of positive logic: hyperimaginaries and continuous logic. For the former we define an
(-)^{\textup{heq}}
construction, analogous to the
(-)^{\textup{eq}}
construction for imaginaries in full first-order logic. Where the
(-)^{\textup{heq}}
construction is problematic in full first-order logic, it does stay within the framework in positive logic and it preserves many nice properties. For the latter we explain how continuous logic can be studied as a special case of positive logic, making it so that all abstract model-theoretic results in positive logic apply to continuous theories.

In the appendix we provide a quick guide to the material covered in these notes, including very brief proof sketches.

In memoriam

Jaap

Chapter 1
Introduction

Positive logic is a generalisation of full first-order logic that does not have negation built in. That is, the allowed connectives are
\top
,
\bot
,
\wedge
and
\vee
, and only existential quantification
\exists
is allowed. This is a generalisation, because any desired amount of negation can be added back in through a process called Morleyisation.

Many model-theoretic ideas, tools and techniques still work perfectly fine in positive logic. Importantly, there is a compactness theorem. With some care, many classical results hold in the generality of positive logic without giving up any strength.

A notable example of the use of positive logic is that hyperimaginaries can be added to the monster model, similar to how we can add imaginaries in full first-order logic without any issue. Another important example is that positive logic also subsumes continuous logic, in the sense that any monster model of a continuous theory can be viewed as the monster model of a positive theory, allowing abstract results in positive logic to be applied in continuous logic. Both these (classes of) examples are discussed in these notes.

Only basic knowledge of model theory is assumed. Some remarks are aimed at a more advanced audience that is familiar with the full first-order version of whatever is discussed. The later chapters (simplicity and stability, Chapters
5
and
6
) technically require no prerequisite knowledge, but might be lacking in motivation if the reader is not familiar with simplicity and stability in full first-order logic.

1.1
The purpose of these notes

The main purpose of these notes is to provide an introduction to positive logic, and to present an overview of some of the deep model theory that can be done, and has been done, in positive logic. This is not a survey of all the work in positive logic to date. We chose to treat a positive version of what is usually referred to as the Ryll-Nardzewski theorem (a characterisation of countably categorical theories) and a positive treatment of simplicity and stability.

The main results that we present are not original. The purpose is not to present new results, but to present a self contained introduction to and overview of positive model theory. Even though these results are already present in literature, they are spread out over multiple papers, sometimes using different terminology and notation. By consolidating this existing body of work, we can give a simpler and smoother presentation. With less technical statements the hope is that this makes positive model theory accessible to a larger audience.

1.2
Overview

We give a brief summary of each chapter.

2.

In this chapter we treat the basics of positive logic. We start by discussing the syntax and fundamental notions such as homomorphisms, immersions, positively closed models (p.c. models) and, importantly, compactness. We then discuss familiar constructions from full first-order logic, such as types, amalgamation and the downward Löwenheim-Skolem theorem. We finish the chapter with a hierarchy of tameness properties that positive theories can enjoy, namely: being Boolean, Hausdorff, semi-Hausdorff or thick.

3.

The goal of this chapter is to prove a positive version of what is usually referred to as the Ryll-Nardzewski theorem, a characterisation of countably categorical theories. We prove an omitting types theorem along the way and introduce positively saturated, atomic and prime models.

4.

As is common in model theory, we will wish to work in a monster model. In this chapter we discuss how such monster models can be constructed, similarly to how it is done in full first-order logic. Being able to now work in a monster model, we give a positive version of some common model-theoretic tools, such as the construction of indiscernible sequences, and a treatment of Lascar strong types.

5.

The goal of this chapter is to prove a Kim-Pillay style theorem: a characterisation of simple theories in terms of the existence of an independence relation, that must then be given by non-dividing and is thus unique. Throughout we will assume the positive theory we work with is thick, as this allows us to give a much simpler and smoother treatment of simplicity that is very close to the usual treatment in full first-order logic.

6.

Continuing the previous chapter, we link stability to simplicity. We approach stability through independence relations, with the goal of this chapter being a Kim-Pillay style theorem for stable theories.

7.

In this final chapter we give two classes of examples of positive theories. First we discuss how one can add hyperimaginaries as elements to the monster model without leaving the framework of positive logic. So we get an
(-)^{\textup{heq}}
construction, similar to the
(-)^{\textup{eq}}
construction from full first-order logic. After that we discuss how continuous logic can be studied through positive logic by giving an explicit description of how to turn a continuous monster model into a monster model of a positive theory. This allows us to apply all the abstract theory that has been developed for positive logic (e.g., simplicity and stability) to continuous logic. We give a brief example of how this translation can be used to get a Kim-Pillay style theorem for simple theories in continuous logic.

Finally, in appendix
A
we give an as-brief-as-possible summary of the necessities for positive logic. This can be viewed as a cheat sheet and a quick start guide for readers that wish to use positive logic.

1.3
Bibliographic remarks

To make these notes self contained, all proofs and details are worked out. We thus refer as little as possible to other sources in the text. As mentioned before, the main results are not original, so at the end of each chapter there is a section with bibliographic remarks. In these sections we briefly discuss original sources and further reading.

Chapter 2
Basics

We cover the basics of positive logic, assuming a basic background in full first-order logic. The main objects of study in positive logic are positively closed models (p.c. models, see ?THM?
). Such models work well with the positive fragment of full first-order logic, but they do not work well with negations. Importantly, we still have a compactness theorem for positive formulas (?THM?
).

We discuss types (Section
2.2
) and properties of the category of (p.c.) models (Section
2.3
), which is all very similar to full first-order logic after making some natural adjustments.

An important difference between positive logic and full first-order logic is that behaviour that is always type-definable in full first-order logic is generally no longer type-definable in positive logic. In practice it is often the case that enough of such behaviour is still type-definable in a positive theory. It is thus useful to have a hierarchy of how ‘nice’ a positive theory is in this regard, yielding the notions of Boolean, Hausdorff, semi-Hausdorff and thick theory, which are discussed in Section
2.4
.

2.1
Formulas, homomorphisms, immersions and p.c. models

We assume the reader is familiar with the following definitions, which are exactly the same as in the classical treatment for full first-order logic:

•

signature
or
language
, consisting of
constant symbols
,
function symbols
and
relation symbols
, possibly multi-sorted;

•

a
structure
in a given language (we allow empty structures, which are essentially propositional structures);

•

the recursive definition of a formula in full first-order logic, and satisfaction of such a formula in a structure.

We also adapt some conventions that are standard in model theory:

•

in general treatments we often leave the signature implicit and just assume to work in some fixed signature
\mathcal{L}
(that is often left out of the notation);

•

the theories we consider are assumed to be consistent;

•

we will not distinguish tuples from single elements, so the notation
a\in M
means that
a
is some tuple in
M
;

•

usually lowercase letters
a,b,c,\ldots
will denote tuples of elements in a structure while
x,y,z,\ldots
will denote (tuples of) variables, uppercase letters
A,B,C,\ldots
will denote sets where we use
M
and
N
for models;

•

unions are written in juxtaposition, so
AB
just means
A\cup B
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
positive formula
is one that is built from atomic formulas using the connectives
\top,\bot,\wedge,\vee
and the existential quantifier
\exists
. Our signature will always include the symbol for equality
=
, but not necessarily the symbol for inequality.

An
h-inductive sentence
is one of the form
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
, where
\varphi(x)
and
\psi(x)
are positive formulas. An
h-universal sentence
is an h-inductive sentence of the form
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\bot)
.

A
positive theory
is a set of h-inductive sentences.

We stress that inequality is generally not a positive formula, which is relevant in certain applications of positive logic. For example, when treating hyperimagarinies (Section
7.1
) or continuous logic (Section
7.2
).

Semantically h-inductive sentences are precisely the sentences expressing that one positively definable set is included in another. The ‘h’ in the name refers to “homomorphism” and comes from their interaction with homomorphisms when taking directed unions (see also the discussion before ?THM?
).

The h-universal sentences are precisely the negations of positive sentences, up to equivalence. So they can always be written in the form
\neg\exists x\varphi(x)
, where
\varphi(x)
is positive quantifier-free. Such a formula is equivalent to
\forall x\neg\varphi(x)
, hence the name.

In categorical logic, and mainly in topos theory, positive logic is also studied under the name of
coherent logic
. Note that our h-inductive sentences are then exactly
coherent sequents
.

Convention 2.1.2

(Stay positive!)
.

As these notes are about positive logic, we will drop the “positive” from terms from now on. That is, we will just say “formula” and “theory” instead of “positive formula” and “positive theory” respectively. If we ever need to refer to full first-order logic, we will explicitly say so.

Remark 2.1.3
.

One easily verifies by induction that every formula
\varphi(x)
is equivalent to a formula of the form
\exists y\psi(x,y)
, where
\psi(x,y)
is positive quantifier-free. Another useful normal form is that
\varphi(x)
is always equivalent to a formula of the form
\varphi_{1}(x)\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n}(x)
, such that for each
1\leq i\leq n
the formula
\varphi_{i}(x)
is of the form
\exists y\psi_{i}(x,y)
, where
\psi_{i}(x,y)
is a conjunction of atomic formulas.

Definition 2.1.4
.

A
homomorphism
is a function
f:M\to N
between structures that preserves relation symbols and commutes with function symbols and constant symbols. That is:

1.

f(c_{M})=c_{N}
for every constant symbol
c
;

2.

f(g_{M}(a))=g_{N}(f(a))
for every function symbol
g
and every
a\in M
;

3.

for any
a\in M
and any relation symbol
R
we have that
M\models R(a)
implies
N\models R(a)
.

In this situation we also call
N
a
continuation
of
M
.

Proposition 2.1.5
.

A function
f:M\to N
is a homomorphism iff for every formula
\varphi(x)
and every
a\in M
we have

M\models\varphi(a)\implies N\models\varphi(f(a)).

Proof.

The right to left direction is trivial, and the other direction follows by induction on the complexity of the formula.
∎

Remark 2.1.6
.

?THM?
says in particular that for any sentence
\varphi
we have
M\models\varphi
implies
N\models\varphi
. This holds even if
M
is empty (the quantification “every
a\in M
” in ?THM?
is ignored for sentences).

The contrapositive of this statement says precisely that for any h-universal sentence
\chi
we have that
N\models\chi
implies
M\models\chi
.

Definition 2.1.7
.

Let
M
be an
\mathcal{L}
-structure. We write
\mathcal{L}(M)
for the language that is
\mathcal{L}
together with a new constant symbol for each element of
M
. We view
M
as an
\mathcal{L}(M)
-structure by interpreting each constant symbol as the corresponding element.

The
positive diagram
of an
\mathcal{L}
-structure
M
is the set
\Diag(M)
of all positive quantifier-free
\mathcal{L}(M)
-sentences that are true in
M
.

Remark 2.1.8
.

We will often implicitly use the fact that a model
N
of
\Diag(M)
is essentially the same thing as a homomorphism
f:M\to N
.

Definition 2.1.9
.

A homomorphism
f:M\to N
is called an
immersion
if for every formula
\varphi(x)
and every
a\in M
we have

M\models\varphi(a)\Longleftrightarrow N\models\varphi(f(a)).

Just like in ?THM?
we note that nothing in the definition of an immersion excludes the case where
M
is empty. It just means that we have
M\models\varphi
if and only if
N\models\varphi
for every sentence
\varphi
.

Definition 2.1.10
.

We call a model
M
of a theory
T
a
positively closed model
, or
p.c. model
, if the following equivalent conditions hold:

(i)

every homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
is an immersion;

(ii)

for every
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
, if there is a homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
and
N\models\varphi(f(a))
then already
M\models\varphi(a)
;

(iii)

for every
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
such that
M\not\models\varphi(a)
there is
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
and
M\models\psi(a)
.

Lemma 2.1.11
.

The conditions in ?THM?
are indeed equivalent.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
By definition of being an immersion.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Let
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
be such that
M\not\models\varphi(a)
. The theory
T\cup\Diag(M)\cup\{\varphi(a)\}
is inconsistent. This is because a model
N
of this theory would be a model of
T
, admit a homomorphism
f:M\to N
and be such that
N\models\varphi(f(a))
from which
M\models\varphi(a)
would follow by our assumption. Hence there is some
\chi(a,b)\in\Diag(M)
such that
T
is inconsistent with
\{\varphi(a),\chi(a,b)\}
. As
a
and
b
do not appear in
T
this means that
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\exists y\chi(x,y))
. Taking
\psi(x)
to be
\exists y\chi(x,y)
then completes the proof.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Let
f:M\to N
be a homomorphism with
N\models T
. Let
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
be such that
N\models\varphi(f(a))
. Suppose for a contradiction that
M\not\models\varphi(a)
. Then by assumption there is
\psi(x)
with
M\models\psi(a)
and
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
. As
f
is a homomorphism we must have
N\models\psi(f(a))
, but then
N\models\varphi(f(a))\wedge\psi(f(a))
while being a model of
T
. So we arrive at a contradiction and conclude
M\models\varphi(a)
, as required.
∎

Remark 2.1.12
.

Some authors also use the name “existentially closed model” or “e.c. model” for what we call a p.c. model. This terminology stems from the approach where we consider embeddings between models instead of homomorphisms (see also ?THM?
(i)). In that case, a model
M
is an e.c. model precisely when every quantifier-free formula (potentially using negations) that has a solution in some bigger model
N\supseteq M
already has a solution in
M
. So this is really about the
existence
of solutions.

Being positively closed is similar, but more is going on: p.c. models make as much true as possible. For example, the only p.c. models of the empty theory (in the empty signature) are singletons. It is instructive to see why this happens, as it emphasises two points. Namely that being p.c. is not just about finding ‘new’ solutions to equations, but also that as many things as possible have to be true about existing elements. The second point is that equality is one of these things that can be true about elements, meaning in particular that when moving from an arbitrary model to a p.c. model some elements might have to be identified.

So let
M
be a p.c. model of the empty theory. Then
N=M\cup\{*\}
is another model and
M\subseteq N
is a homomorphism of models. As
N\models\exists x(x=x)
we must have
M\models\exists x(x=x)
, so
M
is inhabited. To see that
M
must be a singleton, let
a,b\in M
and consider the homomorphism of models
f:M\to N^{\prime}
, where
N^{\prime}=\{*\}
. Applying ?THM?
(ii) to the formula
x=y
we find
N^{\prime}\models f(a)=f(b)
, and so
M\models a=b
, as required.

We also note that some authors use the name “positively existentially closed model” or “pec model” for what we call a p.c. model.

Definition 2.1.13
.

Let
T
be a theory and
\varphi(x)
be a formula. A formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
is called an
obstruction of
\varphi(x)
.

Using the above terminology we can rephrase ?THM?
(iii) as follows: for every
a
and
\varphi(x)
such that
M\not\models\varphi(a)
there is an obstruction
\psi(x)
of
\varphi(x)
such that
M\models\psi(a)
.

Remark 2.1.14
.

In some literature the term “a negation” is used instead of “an obstruction”. This can be confused with
the
negation of a formula, hence the choice to use the current terminology. The term “a denial” has also appeared.

Geometric logic
allows infinite disjunctions. That is, a geometric formula is one that is built from atomic formulas using
\top
,
\bot
,
\wedge
,
\exists
and infinite disjunctions, such that it has only finitely many free variables.

Corollary 2.1.15
.

The class of p.c. models of a theory
T
can always be axiomatised using geometric logic. More explicitly, writing
\Obs(\varphi)
for the set of obstructions of
\varphi
(with respect to
T
) we have that the following geometric theory axiomatises the class of p.c. models of
T
:

T\cup\left\{\forall x\left(\varphi(x)\vee\bigvee\Obs(\varphi)\right):\varphi(x)\text{ is a formula}\right\}.

Proof.

Being a model of the specified geometric theory is clearly equivalent to ?THM?
(iii).
∎

Definition 2.1.16
.

Let
T
be a theory. The
h-universal theory
of
T
is defined as

T^{\textup{u}}=\{\chi\text{ an h-universal sentence}:T\models\chi\}.

The
Kaiser hull
of
T
is defined as

T^{\textup{pc}}=\{\chi\text{ an h-inductive sentence}:M\models\chi\text{ for every p.c.\ model }M\text{ of }T\}.

It turns out that
T^{\textup{u}}
and
T^{\textup{pc}}
have the same p.c. models as
T
, and are respectively the minimal and maximal such theories in a precise sense. This is shown later in ?THM?
.

Lemma 2.1.17
.

Let
T
be a theory in full first-order logic. The models of
T^{\textup{u}}
are precisely those
M_{0}
such that admit a homomorphism
f:M_{0}\to M
into some model
M
of
T
.

Proof.

One direction is clear: if
f:M_{0}\to M
is a homomorphism then
M_{0}\models T^{\textup{u}}
because
M\models T^{\textup{u}}
. We prove the other direction, so let
M_{0}\models T^{\textup{u}}
. We will show that
T\cup\Diag(M_{0})
is consistent. If it would be inconsistent there would be
\varphi(a)\in\Diag(M_{0})
, where
a\in M_{0}
, such that
T\models\neg\varphi(a)
. As
a
does not appear in
T
this just means that
T\models\neg\exists x\varphi(x)
. Hence
\neg\exists x\varphi(x)\in T^{\textup{u}}
, contradicting
M_{0}\models T^{\textup{u}}
.
∎

Definition 2.1.18
.

Recall that a
directed poset
is a poset
I
such that for any
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in I
there is an upper bound
j\in I
. A
directed system
of
\mathcal{L}
-structures is a functor from a directed poset
I
into the category of
\mathcal{L}
-structures and homomorphisms. More precisely, we have
\mathcal{L}
-structures
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
together with a homomorphism
f_{ij}:M_{i}\to M_{j}
for every
i\leq j
such that:

(i)

f_{ii}
is the identity function,

(ii)

f_{jk}f_{ij}=f_{ik}
for all
i\leq j\leq k
.

The
union of a directed system

(M_{i})_{i\in I}
or
directed union

of

(M_{i})_{i\in I}
is defined as follows. For the underlying set we take
M=\coprod_{i\in I}M_{i}/{\sim}
where
\sim
is the equivalence relation defined as follows: for
a\in M_{i}
and
b\in M_{j}
we have
a\sim b
if and only if there is
k\geq i,j
such that
f_{ik}(a)=f_{ik}(b)
. For
a\in M_{i}
we write
[a]
for the equivalence class of
a
(so
[a]\in M
). Then we make
M
into an
\mathcal{L}
-structure as follows:

•

for every constant symbol
c
we set
c_{M}=[c_{M_{i}}]
for any
i\in I
;

•

for every relation symbol
R(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
we set
M\models R([a_{1}],\ldots,[a_{n}])
if there is some
i\in I
and
a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime}\in M_{i}
representing
[a_{1}],\ldots,[a_{n}]
with
M_{i}\models R(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime})
;

•

for every
n
-ary function symbol
f
we do the following, given
[a_{1}],\ldots,[a_{n}]\in M
we let
i
be such that there are
a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime}\in M_{i}
representing
[a_{1}],\ldots,[a_{n}]
and we set
f^{M}([a_{1}],\ldots,[a_{n}])=[f^{M_{i}}(a_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,a_{n}^{\prime})]
.

In the special case where
I
is a linear order we call
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
a
chain
. If
I
is an ordinal and
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
is such that for every limit ordinal
\ell\in I
the structure
M_{\ell}
is the directed union of
(M_{i})_{i<\ell}
we call
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
a
continuous chain
.

Remark 2.1.19
.

Some remarks about directed systems and unions of them, using the notation from ?THM?
.

(i)

The structure on
M
is well-defined because the maps in the system are homomorphisms and because the system is directed.

(ii)

If all the homomorphisms in the directed system
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
are inclusions then the underlying set of
M
is just the set-theoretic union of
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
. We often think of homomorphisms as inclusions, so by abuse of notation we will often disregard the equivalence relation. That is, given
a\in M_{i}
we will also write
a\in M
instead of
[a]\in M
. Conversely, given
a\in M
(which now denotes
[a]
) we will write
a\in M_{i}
if there is
a^{\prime}\in M_{i}
such that
a^{\prime}\sim a
.

(iii)

Following the previous point, we will write
\bigcup_{i\in I}M_{i}
for the union of the system
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
, viewed as an
\mathcal{L}
-structure.

(iv)

The obvious inclusions (technically just maps) from a directed system
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
into its union
M=\bigcup_{i\in I}M_{i}
are homomorphisms. This follows immediately from how we defined the structure on
M
.

Proposition 2.1.20
.

Let
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a directed system and let
M=\bigcup_{i\in I}M_{i}
be its union. Then for any formula
\varphi(x)
and any
a\in M
we have
M\models\varphi(a)
if and only if there is some
i\in I
such that
a\in M_{i}
and
M_{i}\models\varphi(a)
.

Proof.

This is straightforward induction on the complexity of
\varphi
. For atomic formulas the statement holds by definition. Then each induction step is straightforward using the fact that the system is directed and that all the maps in the system are homomorphisms.
∎

The following theorem explains the name “h-inductive” sentence. The ‘h’ stands for “homomorphism” and directed unions are sometimes also called
inductive limits
.

Theorem 2.1.21
.

Let
T
be a theory in full first-order logic. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

T
can be axiomatised using h-inductive sentences, that is: there is an equivalent theory
T^{\prime}
that contains only h-inductive sentences;

(ii)

the union of a directed system of models of
T
is again a model of
T
;

(iii)

the union of a chain of models of
T
is again a model of
T
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Let
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
be an h-inductive sentence in
T^{\prime}
and let
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a directed system of models of
T
. It is enough to show that
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
holds in
M=\bigcup_{i\in I}M_{i}
. So let
a\in M
be such that
M\models\varphi(a)
. By ?THM?
there is
i\in I
such that
a\in M_{i}
and
M_{i}\models\varphi(a)
. As
M_{i}
is a model of
T
, and hence of
T^{\prime}
, we have
M_{i}\models\psi(a)
from which
M\models\psi(a)
follows.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Trivial.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Let
T^{\prime}
be the set of h-inductive consequences of
T
. Let
M_{0}\models T^{\prime}
, we will show that
M_{0}\models T
. Write
\Pi
for the set of all h-universal
\mathcal{L}(M_{0})
-sentences that are true in
M_{0}
. We claim that
T\cup\Diag(M_{0})\cup\Pi
is consistent. If not then there are
\varphi(a)\in\Diag(M_{0})
and
\psi(a)\in\Pi
such that
T\models\neg(\varphi(a)\wedge\psi(a))
. Note that
\psi(x)
is of the form
\neg\chi(x)
for some positive formula
\chi(x)
. So because
a
does not appear in
T
we have
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\chi(x))
. This last sentence is h-inductive, hence
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\chi(x))\in T^{\prime}
. Then
M_{0}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\chi(x))
, so, as
M_{0}\models\varphi(a)
, we have
M_{0}\models\chi(a)
. However, this contradicts
M_{0}\models\psi(a)
. So we conclude that
T\cup\Diag(M_{0})\cup\Pi
is indeed consistent.

We then find a model
N_{0}
of
T\cup\Diag(M_{0})\cup\Pi
. That is,
N_{0}
is a model of
T
and there is a homomorphism
f_{0}:M_{0}\to N_{0}
. Now take
\Delta
to be the elementary diagram of
M_{0}
(i.e. all
\mathcal{L}(M_{0})
-sentences in full first-order logic that are true in
M_{0}
). Then by construction
\Delta^{\textup{u}}=\Pi
, so by ?THM?
and the fact that
N_{0}\models\Pi
there is a model
M_{1}\models\Delta
with a homomorphism
g_{0}:N_{0}\to M_{1}
. In particular
g_{0}f_{0}:M_{0}\to M_{1}
is an elementary embedding because
M_{1}\models\Delta
and thus
M_{1}\models T^{\prime}
.

Repeating this construction, we find an infinite chain of homomorphisms

M_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}N_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{0}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}N_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}M_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\ldots

such that
M_{i}\models T^{\prime}
,
N_{i}\models T
and
g_{i}f_{i}
is an elementary embedding for all
i<\omega
. Set
U=\bigcup_{i<\omega}M_{i}=\bigcup_{i<\omega}N_{i}
. By assumption
U
is then a model of
T
. It is also the union of a chain of elementary embeddings, so the inclusion
M_{0}\to U
is an elementary embedding. We conclude that
M_{0}\models T
, as required.
∎

Proposition 2.1.22
.

Let
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a directed system of p.c. models of some theory
T
. Then
M=\bigcup_{i\in I}M_{i}
is again a p.c. model of
T
.

Proof.

By ?THM?

M
is a model of
T
, so it remains to verify that it is p.c. We check ?THM?
(ii). Let
a\in M
,
\varphi(x)
and
f:M\to N
be such that
N\models\varphi(f(a))
and
N\models T
. By directedness there is
i\in I
such that
a\in M_{i}
. Write
g:M_{i}\to N
for the composition
M_{i}\to M\xrightarrow{f}N
. So we have
N\models\varphi(g(a))
and hence
M_{i}\models\varphi(a)
because
M_{i}
is p.c. We conclude that indeed
M\models\varphi(a)
.
∎

Definition 2.1.23
.

For a theory
T
we let
|T|
be the cardinality of the set of formulas, up to logical equivalence. We call
T
a
countable theory
if
|T|
is countable.

In some approaches
|T|
is defined as the maximum between
\aleph_{0}
and the cardinality of the set of formulas in the language of
T
. However, all we really care about is how many formulas there are up to equivalence modulo
T
. For example, a signature could have many constant symbols, but if the theory declares them to be all equal then there is effectively only one constant symbol. At the same time, we typically have infinitely many distinct formulas, even up to equivalence (e.g.,
x_{1}=x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{n}=x_{n}
for all
n<\omega
).

Theorem 2.1.24

(Continue to p.c. model)
.

Every model
M
of a theory
T
can be continued to a p.c. model of
T
. That is, there is some p.c. model
N
of
T
with a homomorphism
f:M\to N
.

Proof.

We will construct a chain
(M_{n})_{n<\omega}
of models of
T
, with
M_{0}=M
, such that the following holds: for any
a\in M_{n}
and
\varphi(x)
, if there is a homomorphism
f:M_{n+1}\to N
for some
N\models T
with
N\models\varphi(f(a))
then
M_{n+1}\models\varphi(a)
.

Assume that
M_{n}
has been constructed. Let
\Phi
be the set of all formulas with parameters in
M_{n}
. Enumerate
\Phi
as
(\varphi_{i}(a_{i}))_{i<|T|+|M_{n}|}
. We construct a chain
(M_{n}^{i})_{i<|T|+|M_{n}|}
of models of
T
as follows. We set
M_{n}^{0}=M_{n}
and at limit stages we take the union. For the successor step we assume to have constructed
M_{n}^{i}
. If there is a homomorphism
f:M_{n}^{i}\to N
with
N\models T
and
N\models\varphi_{i}(f(a_{i}))
we set
M_{n}^{i+1}=N
and take
f
to be the next link in the chain. If no such homomorphism exists we set
M_{n}^{i+1}=M_{n}^{i}
. Having constructed this chain, we set
M_{n+1}=\bigcup_{i<|T|+|M_{n}|}M_{n}^{i}
.

We verify the induction hypothesis for
M_{n+1}
. Let
a\in M_{n}
and
\varphi(x)
be any formula. Let
i<|T|+|M_{n}|
be such that
\varphi=\varphi_{i}
and
a=a_{i}
. Assume there is a homomorphism
f:M_{n+1}\to N
for some
N\models T
with
N\models\varphi(f(a))
. Composing
f
with the obvious inclusion
M_{n}^{i}\to M_{n+1}
we obtain a homomorphism
g:M_{n}^{i}\to N
with
N\models\varphi(g(a))
. By how we constructed
M_{n}^{i+1}
this means that
M_{n}^{i+1}\models\varphi(a)
and thus
M_{n+1}\models\varphi(a)
, as required.

Having constructed
(M_{n})_{n<\omega}
we set
N=\bigcup_{n<\omega}M_{n}
. To check that
N
is p.c. we let
a\in N
and
\varphi(x)
be any formula. Assume there is some homomorphism
f:N\to N^{\prime}
with
N^{\prime}\models T
and
N^{\prime}\models\varphi(f(a))
. Let
n<\omega
be such that
a\in M_{n}
. Composing
f
with the obvious inclusion
M_{n+1}\to N
we find a homomorphism
g:M_{n+1}\to N^{\prime}
. Then
N^{\prime}\models\varphi(g(a))
, so by the induction hypothesis we have
M_{n+1}\models\varphi(a)
and thus
N\models\varphi(a)
, as required.
∎

Lemma 2.1.25
.

For any theory
T
we have
(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}=T^{\textup{u}}
.

Proof.

As
T^{\textup{pc}}\models T
we have
T^{\textup{pc}}\models T^{\textup{u}}
, so
T^{\textup{u}}\subseteq(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}
. Now let
M
be any model of
T
and continue
M
to a p.c. model
N
. Then
N\models T^{\textup{pc}}
and hence
N\models(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}
. By definition
(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}
contains only h-universal sentences, so
M\models(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}
because
N
is a continuation of
M
. As
M
was an arbitrary model of
T
we have
T\models(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}
. We conclude that
(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}\subseteq T^{\textup{u}}
, and the result follows.
∎

Theorem 2.1.26
.

Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be theories in the same language. Then
T
and
T^{\prime}
have the same p.c. models if and only if
T^{\textup{pc}}\models T^{\prime}
and
T^{\prime}\models T^{\textup{u}}
. In particular,
T^{\textup{u}}
and
T^{\textup{pc}}
have the same p.c. models as
T
and are respectively the minimal and maximal such theories.

Proof.

We first prove the left to right direction. As
T
and
T^{\prime}
have the same p.c. models, every p.c. model of
T
is a model of
T^{\prime}
. So by the definition of
T^{\textup{pc}}
we have
T^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\textup{pc}}
and thus
T^{\textup{pc}}\models T^{\prime}
. To prove
T^{\prime}\models T^{\textup{u}}
we let
M
be any model of
T^{\prime}
and
\chi\in T^{\textup{u}}
. We can continue
M
to a p.c. model
N
of
T^{\prime}
. So
N
is also a p.c. model of
T
and in particular a model of
T^{\textup{u}}
, thus
N\models\chi
. As
\chi
is h-universal and
N
is a continuation of
M
we must have
M\models\chi
. Since
M
was an arbitrary model of
T^{\prime}
we get
T^{\prime}\models\chi
and we conclude
T^{\prime}\models T^{\textup{u}}
.

Now we prove the right to left direction. Let
M
be a p.c. model of
T
. Then
M\models T^{\textup{pc}}
and hence
M\models T^{\prime}
because
T^{\textup{pc}}\models T^{\prime}
. To see that
M
is p.c. we verify property ?THM?
(iii). So let
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
be such that
M\not\models\varphi(a)
. Then, because
M
is p.c. for
T
, there is
\psi(x)
with
M\models\psi(a)
and
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
. We thus have
\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))\in T^{\textup{u}}
, as this is an h-universal sentence. Since
T^{\prime}\models T^{\textup{u}}
we thus have
T^{\prime}\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
, and we conclude that
M
is also p.c. for
T^{\prime}
.

Now let
M
be a p.c. model of
T^{\prime}
. We have that
(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}\models(T^{\prime})^{\textup{u}}
and
(T^{\prime})^{\textup{u}}\models T^{\textup{u}}
. So by ?THM?
we have
(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}=(T^{\prime})^{\textup{u}}=T^{\textup{u}}
. We first show that any homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
must be an immersion. Assume for a contradiction that there is
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
such that
N\models\varphi(f(a))
while
M\not\models\varphi(a)
. As
M
is a p.c. model of
T^{\prime}
there must be
\psi(x)
such that
M\models\psi(a)
and
T^{\prime}\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
. However, that means that
N\models\varphi(f(a))\wedge\psi(f(a))
, contradicting
\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))\in(T^{\prime})^{\textup{u}}=T^{\textup{u}}
. We are left to show that
M
is indeed a model of
T
. Using that
M\models T^{\textup{u}}
we find a homomorphism
f:M\to N
where
N\models T
, by ?THM?
. By the above discussion
f
is in fact an immersion. Let
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))\in T
be an h-inductive sentence and let
a\in M
be such that
M\models\varphi(a)
. Then
N\models\varphi(f(a))
and so
N\models\psi(f(a))
. Using the fact that
f
is an immersion we have
M\models\psi(a)
. So
M\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
and we conclude that
M
is indeed a model of
T
, which completes the proof.
∎

When doing model theory in positive logic we are interested in the p.c. models. When we restrict our attention to p.c. models we do still have compactness, but only for positive formulas.

Theorem 2.1.27

(Compactness for positive formulas)
.

Let
T
be a theory and let
\Sigma(x)
be a set of positive formulas. Suppose that for every finite
\Sigma_{0}(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)
there is
M\models T
with
a\in M
such that
M\models\Sigma_{0}(a)
. Then there is a p.c. model
N
of
T
with
a\in N
such that
N\models\Sigma(a)
.

Proof.

By the compactness theorem for full first-order logic we find a model
M^{\prime}
of
T
and
a^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\models\Sigma(a^{\prime})
. Continue
M^{\prime}
to a p.c. model
N
of
T
. Then because
\Sigma(x)
only contains positive formulas we have
N\models\Sigma(f(a^{\prime}))
. So we set
a=f(a^{\prime})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

To illustrate that we can generally not get more compactness, we consider the following two examples.

Example 2.1.28
.

Consider the theory
T
with a symbol for inequality and
\omega
many disjoint unary predicates
P_{n}(x)
. Then p.c. models of
T
are precisely those which consist of
\omega
-many disjoint infinite sets, one for each predicate. If we had full compactness then the set

\Sigma(x)=\{\neg P_{n}(x):n<\omega\}

would have a realisation in some p.c. model, which is impossible.

Example 2.1.29
.

It could happen that there is a definable set that is infinite and bounded. This does not contradict compactness: it just means that inequality is not positively definable on that set. Such situations might arise when adding hyperimaginaries as parameters, which can be done in positive logic (see ?THM?
), but we give a simpler example here.

The signature consists of
\omega
many constant symbols
\{c_{i}\}_{i<\omega}
. The theory
T
then asserts that all of these constant symbols are distinct, i.e.
c_{i}\neq c_{j}
for all
i\neq j
. There is precisely one p.c. model of
T
(up to isomorphism), which consists of just the interpretations of the constant symbols. So the trivial definable set
x=x
is bounded (i.e., it is countable in every p.c. model), but infinite. Again, with full compactness we would run into trouble because

\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\omega_{1}})=\{x_{i}\neq x_{j}:i<j<\omega_{1}\}

would then yield a realisation with uncountably many elements.

Definition 2.1.30
.

We say that a theory
T
has
positive quantifier elimination
if every formula is equivalent to a positive quantifier-free formula modulo
T
.

Proposition 2.1.31
.

A theory
T
has positive quantifier elimination if for every formula
\exists y\varphi(x,y)
, where
y
is a single variable and
\varphi(x,y)
is a conjunction of atomic formulas, there is a quantifier-free formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\exists y\varphi(x,y)\leftrightarrow\psi(x))
.

Proof.

We claim that for every formula of the form
\exists y_{1}\ldots y_{k}\varphi(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
, with
\varphi(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
a conjunction of atomic formulas and
y_{i}
a single variable for all
1\leq i\leq n
, is equivalent to a quantifier-free formula modulo
T
. Every formula is equivalent to a disjunction of such formulas, so it is enough to prove the claim.

The proof of the claim comes down to repeatedly applying the assumption. We work out the details. We will show by induction on
1\leq i\leq n
that there are quantifier-free formulas
\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
such that
\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
is equivalent to
\exists y_{n-i+1}\ldots y_{n}\varphi(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
modulo
T
. Then
\psi_{n}(x)
is equivalent to
\exists y_{1}\ldots y_{n}\varphi(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
modulo
T
, as required.

For the base case we simply take
\psi_{0}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
to be
\varphi(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
. Now assume we have constructed
\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
. We may assume that
\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
is of the form

\alpha_{1}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})\vee\ldots\vee\alpha_{k}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i}),

where
\alpha_{j}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
is a conjunction of atomic formulas for all
1\leq j\leq k
. So
\exists y_{n-i}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
is equivalent to

\exists y_{n-i}\alpha_{1}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})\vee\ldots\vee\exists y_{n-i}\alpha_{k}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i}).

By our assumption each of the disjuncts is equivalent to a quantifier-free formula modulo
T
, so
\exists y_{n-i}\psi_{i}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i})
is equivalent to a quantifier-free formula modulo
T
, which will be our
\psi_{i+1}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n-i-1})
. The equivalence (modulo
T
) to
\exists y_{n-i}\ldots y_{n}\varphi(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
follows immediately from the construction.
∎

2.2
Types and type spaces

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of some theory
T
and let
a\in M
. The
type
of
a
in
M
is given by:

\tp(a;M)=\{\varphi(x):M\models\varphi(a)\}.

If the elements of
a
are indexed by
I
, we call this an
I
-type
, in particular if
a
is finite with
|a|=n
(in a single-sorted language) we call this an
n
-type
.

The notation
\tp(a;M)
makes sense for any structure
M
, not just p.c. models, as we see in the proposition below. We wish to reserve the terminology “type” for the situation in ?THM?
, see also ?THM?
.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

Let
M
be a model of some theory
T
. Then
M
is a p.c. model if and only if for every
a\in M
we have that
\tp(a;M)
is a maximal consistent set of formulas (modulo
T
).

Proof.

We first prove the left to right direction. Let
a\in M
and set
p(x)=\tp(a;M)
and let
\varphi(x)
be any formula that is not in
p(x)
. Then
M\not\models\varphi(a)
, so because
M
is p.c. there must be an obstruction
\psi(x)
of
\varphi(x)
such that
M\models\psi(a)
. We thus have
\psi(x)\in p(x)
which means that
p(x)\cup\{\varphi(x)\}
is inconsistent with
T
. We conclude that
p(x)
is indeed maximal consistent, modulo
T
.

For the converse we let
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
be arbitrary. Let
f:M\to N
be a homomorphism with
N\models T
and
N\models\varphi(f(a))
. Write
p(x)=\tp(a;M)
and
q(x)=\tp(f(a);N)
. As
f
is a homomorphism we have
p(x)\subseteq q(x)
, and because
q(x)
is realised in a model of
T
it is consistent modulo
T
. By maximality of
p(x)
we must thus have
p(x)=q(x)
. So
\varphi(x)\in q(x)=p(x)
and we see that
M\models\varphi(a)
, as required.
∎

By compactness we have that types are precisely the maximally consistent sets of formulas. Some authors use “type” also for any consistent set of formulas (or for those of the form
\tp(a;M)
, where
M
is not necessarily p.c.). For us a “type” will always be a maximal consistent set of formulas, while we make the following definition for the other case.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
T
be a theory. A
partial type
is any consistent set of formulas (modulo
T
). So an alternative definition for
type
is: a maximal partial type.

Definition 2.2.4
.

Let
T
be a theory. For an index set
I
we define the
type space of
I
-types of
T

\operatorname{S}_{I}(T)
as follows. The underlying set is the set of
I
-types of
T
. For a set of formulas
\Sigma(x)
we write

[\Sigma(x)]=\{p(x)\in\operatorname{S}_{I}(T):\Sigma(x)\subseteq p(x)\},

and for formulas we simplify the notation
[\{\varphi(x)\}]
to
[\varphi(x)]
. We topologise
\operatorname{S}_{I}(T)
by taking the sets of the form
[\Sigma(x)]
as closed sets. As we often do not care so much about the index set
I
we may drop it from the notation and just write
\operatorname{S}(T)
.

The topology in the above definition is well-defined. It is easy to see that the proposed closed sets are closed under arbitrary intersections and we have
[\bot]=\emptyset
and
[\top]=\operatorname{S}(T)
. To see that things are closed under finite unions we note that

[\Sigma(x)]\cup[\Sigma^{\prime}(x)]=[\{\varphi(x)\vee\varphi^{\prime}(x):\varphi(x)\in\Sigma(x)\text{ and }\varphi^{\prime}(x)\in\Sigma^{\prime}(x)\}].

Proposition 2.2.5
.

The space
\operatorname{S}(T)
is a compact
T_{1}
space. The latter means that given any two points
p,q\in\operatorname{S}(T)
there is a closed set
A\subseteq\operatorname{S}(T)
with
p\in A
and
q\not\in A
.

Proof.

Compactness of
\operatorname{S}(T)
follows from the compactness theorem, using the following formulation of topological compactness: given a family
\mathcal{F}
of closed sets with the finite intersection property (i.e.
\bigcap\mathcal{F}_{0}\neq\emptyset
for any finite
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
) we have that
\bigcap\mathcal{F}\neq\emptyset
. The
T_{1}
property follows from the maximality of the types: if
p(x)
and
q(x)
are types then there is some
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
with
\varphi(x)\not\in q(x)
, so
A=[\varphi(x)]
is the required closed set.
∎

2.3
Properties of the category of (p.c.) models

In this section we consider various basic properties for the category of (p.c.) models of a fixed theory, such as the amalgamation property (?THM?
), downward Löwenheim-Skolem (?THM?
) and the joint continuation property (?THM?
). We have already seen that these categories have directed unions (i.e., directed colimits, see ?THM?s
and
).

Lemma 2.3.1

(Amalgamation lemma)
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be two
\mathcal{L}
-structures and let
a\in M
and
b\in M^{\prime}
be (possibly infinite) tuples of matching length. If
\tp(b;M^{\prime})\subseteq\tp(a;M)
then there is an
\mathcal{L}
-structure
N
with an elementary embedding
f:M\to N
and a homomorphism
g:M^{\prime}\to N
such that
f(a)=g(b)
.

Proof.

Introduce a tuple of new constants
c
matching
a
and
b
to form
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. We extend
M
and
M^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-structures by interpreting
c
as
a
and
b
respectively. Let
\Delta
be the
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-elementary diagram of
M
. That is, all
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(M)
-sentences in full first-order logic that are true in
M
. Let
\Sigma
be the positive diagram
\Diag(M^{\prime})
of
M^{\prime}
, considered as an
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
-structure. It suffices to show that
\Delta\cup\Sigma
is consistent.

Let
\varphi(c,d)\in\Sigma
where
d
is some tuple of elements from
M^{\prime}
, distinct from
c
. Then
\exists y\varphi(x,y)\in\tp(b;M^{\prime})
and thus
M\models\exists y\varphi(a,y)
because
\tp(b;M^{\prime})\subseteq\tp(a;M)
. So there is
d^{\prime}\in M
with
M\models\varphi(a,d^{\prime})
and we thus see that
M
is a model of
\Delta\cup\{\varphi(c,d)\}
. We conclude that
\Delta\cup\Sigma
is finitely consistent and hence consistent.
∎

Corollary 2.3.2
.

Let
M\xleftarrow{f}M_{0}\xrightarrow{g}M^{\prime}
be a span of
\mathcal{L}
-structures, where
f
is a homomorphism and
g
is an immersion. Then there exists an amalgamation
M\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}N\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
, that is
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
, where
f^{\prime}
is an elementary embedding and
g^{\prime}
is a homomorphism.

Proof.

By the assumptions on
f
and
g
we have
\tp(g(M_{0});M^{\prime})=\tp(M_{0};M_{0})\subseteq\tp(f(M_{0});M)
, so we can apply ?THM?
with
a=f(M_{0})
and
b=g(M_{0})
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.3
.

Some facts:

(i)

if
f:M_{1}\to M_{2}
and
g:M_{2}\to M_{3}
are homomorphisms such that
gf
is an immersion then
f
is an immersion;

(ii)

if
f:M\to N
is an immersion and
N
is a p.c. model of
T
then
M
is a p.c. model of
T
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
a\in M_{1}
and
\varphi(x)
be some formula such that
M_{2}\models\varphi(f(a))
. Then we have
M_{3}\models\varphi(gf(a))
and hence
M_{1}\models\varphi(a)
, as required.

(ii)
We first prove that
M
is a model of
T
. Let
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))\in T
and let
a\in M
such that
M\models\varphi(a)
. Then
N\models\varphi(f(a))
and thus
N\models\psi(f(a))
because
N
is a model of
T
. As
f
is an immersion we have
M\models\psi(a)
and we conclude that
M
is a model of
T
.

To show that
M
is a p.c. model we let
g:M\to N^{\prime}
be any homomorphism. By ?THM?
there are
N\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}U\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}N^{\prime}
where
f^{\prime}
is a homomorphism and
g^{\prime}
is an elementary embedding with
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
. As
N
is p.c. we have in fact that
f^{\prime}
is an immersion and so
f^{\prime}f
is an immersion. By (i) we conclude that
g
is an immersion, as required.
∎

Theorem 2.3.4

(Downward Löwenheim-Skolem for p.c. models)
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of
T
and let
A\subseteq M
. Then there is a p.c. model
M_{0}\subseteq M
with
A\subseteq M_{0}
and
|M_{0}|\leq|A|+|T|
, such that the inclusion is an elementary embedding.

Proof.

By the usual Löwenheim-Skolem theorem we find
M_{0}\subseteq M
with
A\subseteq M_{0}
and
|M_{0}|\leq|A|+|T|
, such that the inclusion is an elementary embedding. In particular the inclusion is an immersion, so
M_{0}
is a p.c. model by ?THM?
.
∎

In full first-order logic there are many equivalent definitions of a complete theory. The important ones are that all models satisfy the same set of full first-order sentences and that any two models can be jointly elementarily embedded into a third model (which allows us to build monster models, see Section
4.3
). The latter is easily generalised to positive logic, and gives rise to the definition below. The positive equivalent of the former—i.e., all p.c. models satisfy the same set of h-inductive sentences—is equivalent condition (iv) below.

Definition 2.3.5
.

A theory
T
is said to have the
joint continuation property
, or
JCP
for short, if the following equivalent conditions hold.

(i)

For any two models
M
and
M^{\prime}
of
T
there is a model
N
of
T
with homomorphisms
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
.

(ii)

For any two p.c. models
M
and
M^{\prime}
of
T
there is a model
N
of
T
with homomorphisms
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
.

(iii)

For any two h-universal sentences
\varphi
and
\psi
we have that
T\models\varphi\vee\psi
implies
T\models\varphi
or
T\models\psi
.

(iv)

For every p.c. model
M
of
T
,
T^{\textup{pc}}
is precisely the set of all h-inductive sentences that are true in
M
.

(v)

For some p.c. model
M
of
T
,
T^{\textup{pc}}
is precisely the set of all h-inductive sentences that are true in
M
.

(vi)

For every p.c. model
M
of
T
,
T^{\textup{u}}
is precisely the set of all h-universal sentences that are true in
M
.

(vii)

For some p.c. model
M
of
T
,
T^{\textup{u}}
is precisely the set of all h-universal sentences that are true in
M
.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

The conditions in ?THM?
are indeed equivalent.

Proof.

We prove the following implications.

(iv)
(ii)
(vi)
(v)
(i)
(vii)
(iii)

(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii)
The right to left direction is trivial. The other follows after continuing each of
M
and
M^{\prime}
into a p.c. model.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
Suppose for a contradiction that there is some h-inductive sentence
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
that is true in some p.c. model
M
but is not in
T^{\textup{pc}}
. By definition of
T^{\textup{pc}}
this means that there must be a p.c. model
M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\not\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
. So there is
a\in M^{\prime}
with
M^{\prime}\models\varphi(a)
and
M^{\prime}\not\models\psi(a)
. As
M^{\prime}
is p.c. there is an obstruction
\psi^{\prime}(x)
of
\psi(x)
such that
M^{\prime}\models\psi^{\prime}(a)
. That is, we have
M^{\prime}\models\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi^{\prime}(x))
. Using (i) we find a model
N
of
T
with homomorphisms
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
. Each of these homomorphisms is an immersion, because
M
and
M^{\prime}
are p.c. We thus have
N\models\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi^{\prime}(x))
and so
M\models\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi^{\prime}(x))
. However, the latter contradicts
M\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(v)
Trivial.

(v)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Immediate, using the fact that
(T^{\textup{pc}})^{\textup{u}}=T^{\textup{u}}
(?THM?
).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(vi)
Suppose for a contradiction that there is some h-universal sentence
\neg\varphi
that is true in some p.c. model
M
but is not in
T^{\textup{u}}
. Then there is a model
M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\models\varphi
. Using (i) we find a model
N
of
T
with homomorphisms
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
. We thus have
N\models\varphi
, but then
M\models\varphi
, because
M
is a p.c. model so
M\to N
is an immersion, a contradiction.

(vi)
\Rightarrow
(vii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Trivial.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be models of
T
. We prove that
\Diag(M)\cup\Diag(M^{\prime})
is consistent. If not, then there would be
\varphi(a)\in\Diag(M)
and
\psi(b)\in\Diag(M^{\prime})
such that
T\models\neg(\varphi(a)\vee\psi(b))
. We may assume
a
and
b
to be disjoint, so we get
T\models\neg\exists x\varphi(x)\vee\neg\exists y\psi(y)
. So by (iii) we must have either
T\models\neg\exists x\varphi(x)
contradicting
\varphi(a)\in\Diag(M)
, or
T\models\neg\exists y\psi(y)
contradicting
\psi(b)\in\Diag(M^{\prime})
.
∎

Definition 2.3.7
.

We call a p.c. model
M
of a theory
T
a
maximal p.c. model
if any model
N
of
T
admits a homomorphism
N\to M
into
M
.

An example of a theory with a maximal model is the empty theory considered in ?THM?
, where we established that the singletons are the p.c. models. So any singleton is a maximal model of that theory. It can also happen that the maximal p.c. model is infinite, see ?THM?
. This is in contrast to full first-order logic, where having a maximal model means that all models are finite.

2.4
Boolean, (semi-)Hausdorff and thick

Even though positive logic does not have negation built in, we can add back as much as we desire. This is done through a process called Morleyisation, as described below.

Definition 2.4.1
.

A
positive fragment

\Delta
of a language
\mathcal{L}
is a set of formulas in full first-order logic that contains all atomic formulas and is closed under sub-formulas, change of variables, conjunction and disjunction. Given such a positive fragment
\Delta
we define the (
\Delta
-)
Morleyisation

\Mor(\Delta)
to be the following positive theory. We extend the language to include a relation symbol
R_{\varphi}(x)
for every
\varphi(x)\in\Delta
. Then we inductively add h-inductive sentences to
\Mor(\Delta)
so that it expresses that
R_{\varphi}(x)
and
\varphi(x)
are equivalent for every
\varphi(x)\in\Delta
(see below for details).

Remark 2.4.2
.

Fix some language
\mathcal{L}
and let
\Delta
be a positive fragment. Given any
\mathcal{L}
-theory
T
whose axioms are of the form
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
with
\varphi,\psi\in\Delta
we can, and will, naturally view
T
as a positive theory by considering
\Mor(\Delta)\cup T^{\prime}
. Here
T^{\prime}
is obtained from
T
by replacing each
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))\in T
by
\forall x(R_{\varphi}(x)\to R_{\psi}(x))
.

There are two particularly interesting cases of the above situation. Write
\Delta_{\textup{qf}}
for the set of all quantifier-free full first-order formulas, and
\Delta_{\textup{fo}}
for the set of all full first-order formulas. We consider Morleyisation in each case.

(i)

Working with a
\Delta_{\textup{qf}}
-Morleyised theory
T
is equivalent to saying that the negation of every relation symbol (including equality) is positive definable. In particular, all homomorphisms between models of such a theory
T
are embeddings. We call such a theory a
Pillay theory
. If the theory furthermore has the property that every span of homomorphisms between models
M\xleftarrow{f}M_{0}\xrightarrow{g}M^{\prime}
can be amalgamated (i.e., there are homomorphisms of models
M\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}N\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
) then we call it a
Robinson theory
.

The p.c. models of a Pillay theory are what are classically called existentially closed models or e.c. models (see also ?THM?
). Often one would prove that such a theory is model complete, or at least has a model companion, so that it can be studied as a full first-order theory. Using positive logic we no longer need to worry about such things. We can just study the theory as a positive theory, even when the theory is not companionable.

(ii)

We can study full first-order logic as a special case of positive logic by working with
\Delta_{\textup{fo}}
-Morleyised theories. In this case every formula in full first-order logic will be equivalent to a positive formula (in fact, to an atomic one). So the notions of homomorphism, immersion and elementary embedding all coincide. This also means that every model is a p.c. model. See also ?THM?
. Having JCP is now equivalent to the theory being complete (see also the discussion before ?THM?
).

Convention 2.4.3
.

Whenever we say we that we consider a theory
T
in full first-order logic as a positive theory, we mean its Morleyised version as described in ?THM?
(ii). In this light we will view positive logic as a proper generalisation of full first-order logic.

Lemma 2.4.4
.

The positive theory
\Mor(\Delta)
described in ?THM?
does indeed exist.

Proof.

We add h-inductive sentences to
\Mor(\Delta)
based on the complexity of a formula
\varphi(x)\in\Delta
. We may assume that
\varphi(x)
is built using the connectives
\vee,\wedge,\neg
and
\exists
, as any other full first-order connectives can be treated as abbreviations for these connectives. So we split into the following cases, based on the outermost connective in
\varphi(x)
.

Atomic
\varphi(x)
.
We can simply add the sentences
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to R_{\varphi}(x))
and
\forall x(R_{\varphi}(x)\to\varphi(x))
, which are h-inductive because
\varphi(x)
is atomic.

Connectives
\vee
and
\wedge
.
Write
\bigcirc
for the relevant connective (i.e. either
\vee
or
\wedge
) so that
\varphi(x)
is
\varphi_{1}(x)\bigcirc\varphi_{2}(x)
. We then add the sentences
\forall x(R_{\varphi}(x)\to R_{\varphi_{1}}(x)\bigcirc R_{\varphi_{2}}(x))
and
\forall x(R_{\varphi_{1}}(x)\bigcirc R_{\varphi_{2}}(x)\to R_{\varphi}(x))
.

Connective
\neg
.
So
\varphi(x)
is of the form
\neg\psi(x)
. We then add the sentences
\forall x(R_{\varphi}(x)\vee R_{\psi}(x))
and
\forall x\neg(R_{\varphi}(x)\wedge R_{\psi}(x))
.

Connective
\exists
.
So
\varphi(x)
is of the form
\exists y\psi(x,y)
. We add the sentences
\forall x(R_{\varphi}(x)\to\exists yR_{\psi}(x,y))
and
\forall x(\exists yR_{\psi}(x,y)\to R_{\varphi}(x))
.

One now easily proves by induction on the complexity of the formula
\varphi(x)
that
\Mor(\Delta)\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow R_{\varphi}(x))
.
∎

Definition 2.4.5
.

We call a theory
T

Boolean
if the following equivalent conditions hold.

(i)

Every model of
T
is a p.c. model.

(ii)

Every homomorphism between models of
T
is an immersion.

(iii)

For every positive formula
\varphi(x)
there is a positive formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow\psi(x))
.

(iv)

For every full first-order formula
\varphi(x)
there is a positive formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow\psi(x))
.

(v)

Every homomorphism between models of
T
is an elementary embedding.

Clearly any Morleyised full first-order theory is Boolean, so following ?THM?
we use these terms interchangeably.

The term Boolean in ?THM?
refers to the fact that for such theories the distributive lattice of positively definable sets is in fact a Boolean algebra. Some sources use the term
positively model complete
, but Boolean seems more descriptive.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

Let
T
be a theory. Suppose that
\varphi(x)
is a full first-order formula such that for every homomorphism
f:M\to N
of models of
T
, and any
a\in M
we have
M\models\varphi(a)
implies
N\models\varphi(f(a))
. Then
\varphi(x)
is equivalent to a positive formula modulo
T
.

Proof.

We first prove the following claim. Let
M
be any model of
T
and
a\in M
such that
M\models\varphi(a)
. Then there is positive
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\psi(x)\to\varphi(x))
and
M\models\psi(a)
.

To prove the claim we consider the set of formulas
T\cup\Diag(M)\cup\{\neg\varphi(a)\}
. This cannot be consistent, as that would give us a homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
and
N\not\models\varphi(f(a))
. There is thus some
\chi(a,b)\in\Diag(M)
such that
T\models\chi(a,b)\to\varphi(a)
. As
a
and
b
do not appear in
T
this means that
T\models\forall x(\exists y\chi(x,y)\to\varphi(x))
, and taking
\psi(x)
to be
\exists y\chi(x,y)
proves the claim.

Let
\Psi(x)
be the set of all formulas
\neg\psi(x)
such that
\psi(x)
is positive and implies
\varphi(x)
, modulo
T
. We will show that
T\cup\Psi(x)\cup\{\varphi(x)\}
is inconsistent. If it were consistent then we find a model
M
of
T
and
a\in M
such that
M\models\Psi(a)
and
M\models\varphi(a)
. Using the claim there must be a positive
\psi(x)
that implies
\varphi(x)
modulo
T
such that
M\models\psi(a)
. However, by definition
\neg\psi(x)\in\Psi(x)
, so this contradicts
M\models\Psi(a)
.

Let
\{\neg\psi_{1}(x),\ldots,\neg\psi_{n}(x)\}\subseteq\Psi(x)
such that
T\cup\{\neg\psi_{1}(x),\ldots,\neg\psi_{n}(x),\varphi(x)\}
is inconsistent. Then
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi_{1}(x)\vee\ldots\vee\psi_{n}(x))
, and we conclude that
\varphi(x)
is equivalent to the positive formula
\psi_{1}(x)\vee\ldots\vee\psi_{n}(x)
modulo
T
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.7
.

The conditions in ?THM?
are indeed equivalent.

Proof.

We prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
\Rightarrow
(v)
\Rightarrow
(i). The first and last implication are trivial, so we prove the remaining three.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Let
\varphi(x)
be a positive formula. As every homomorphism between models of
T
is an immersion, truth of
\neg\varphi(x)
is also preserved upwards by such homomorphisms. By ?THM?
we conclude that
\neg\varphi(x)
is equivalent to a positive formula, modulo
T
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
This easily follows by induction on the complexity of the full first-order formula
\varphi(x)
, where we use (iii) for the induction step with
\neg
. We treat the remaining connectives such as
\to
and
\forall
as abbreviations using the connectives for positive formulas together with
\neg
.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(v)
To verify that a homomorphism
f:M\to N
is an elementary embedding it suffices to check that for every full first-order formula
\varphi(x)
and every
a\in M
we have that
M\models\varphi(a)
implies
N\models\varphi(f(a))
. This immediately follows from (iv) because
\varphi(x)
will be equivalent to a positive formula, modulo
T
, and hence its truth is preserved upwards by homomorphisms.
∎

The following definitions, except for being Boolean, are taken from
[
BY03c
]
. These assumptions are very useful for developing (neo)stability theory for positive logic, while the weaker ones—thickness, and even being semi-Hausdorff—are relatively mild. Before we define them, we first need to recall the notion of an indiscernible sequence.

Definition 2.4.8
.

Fix a theory
T
. An
indiscernible sequence
is an infinite sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in some p.c. model
M
such that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}
in
I
we have

\tp(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n}};M)=\tp(a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{n}};M).

So a sequence is indiscernible precisely when any two subsequences of the same order-type have the same type.

Definition 2.4.9
.

Let
T
be a theory. We call
T
:

•

Boolean
if every formula in full first-order logic is equivalent to some positive formula, modulo
T
(or any of the equivalent statements from ?THM?
);

•

Hausdorff
if for any two distinct types
p(x)
and
q(x)
there are
\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)
and
\psi(x)\not\in q(x)
such that
T^{\textup{pc}}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
;

•

semi-Hausdorff
if equality of types is type-definable, so there is a partial type
\Omega(x,y)
such that for any
a,b
in any p.c. model
M
we have
\tp(a;M)=\tp(b;M)
if and only if
M\models\Omega(a,b)
;

•

thick
if being an indiscernible sequence is type-definable, so there is a partial type
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
such that for any sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
in any p.c. model
M
we have that
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is indiscernible if and only if
M\models\Theta((a_{i})_{i<\omega})
.

The reason for the name Hausdorff is that this corresponds to the type spaces being Hausdorff, see ?THM?
.

We mentioned Boolean theories in ?THM?
again because they fit very well in the hierarchy mentioned there, as is apparent from the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4.10
.

Boolean implies Hausdorff implies semi-Hausdorff implies thick.

Proof.

Boolean implies Hausdorff.
Let
p(x)
and
q(x)
be distinct types. Pick any
\varphi(x)\in q(x)
such that
\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)
. Because the theory is Boolean there must be
\psi(x)
that is equivalent to
\neg\varphi(x)
, modulo the theory. So we have
\psi(x)\not\in q(x)
while also
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
, so in particular
T^{\textup{pc}}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
, as required.

Hausdorff implies semi-Hausdorff.
Define

\displaystyle\Omega(x,y)=\{\varphi(x,y):\;

\displaystyle\text{for all }a,b\text{ in any p.c.\ model }M\text{ with }\tp(a)=\tp(b)

\displaystyle\text{we have }M\models\varphi(a,b)\}.

Let
a,b
be arbitrary in some arbitrary p.c. model
M
. By construction we have that
\tp(a)=\tp(b)
implies
M\models\Omega(a,b)
. For the other direction we prove the contrapositive. So suppose that
\tp(a)\neq\tp(b)
. Because the theory is Hausdorff there are
\varphi(x)\not\in\tp(a)
and
\psi(x)\not\in\tp(b)
such that
T^{\textup{pc}}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
. The latter means that by definition of
\Omega(x,y)
we then have
(\varphi(x)\wedge\varphi(y))\vee(\psi(x)\wedge\psi(y))\in\Omega(x,y)
. The former means that
M\not\models(\varphi(a)\wedge\varphi(b))\vee(\psi(a)\wedge\psi(b))
, hence
M\not\models\Omega(a,b)
, as required.

Semi-Hausdorff implies thick.
Define the partial type
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
as:

\bigcup\{\Omega(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}};x_{j_{1}},\ldots,x_{j_{n}}):n<\omega,i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}<\omega,j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}<\omega\}.

Here
\Omega(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}};x_{j_{1}},\ldots,x_{j_{n}})
is the partial type expressing that
x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}
and
x_{j_{1}},\ldots,x_{j_{n}}
have the same type, which exists by the semi-Hausdorff assumption. So
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
expresses that any two finite subsequences of
(x_{i})_{i<\omega}
of the same length have the same type, and hence it expresses indiscernibility.
∎

The following characterisations of Hausdorff theories (?THM?
) are useful in practice.

Definition 2.4.11
.

We say that a theory
T
has the
h-amalgamation property
or
APh
if any span
M\xleftarrow{f}M_{0}\xrightarrow{g}M^{\prime}
of homomorphisms between models of
T
can be amalgamated (i.e., there are homomorphisms
M\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}N\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
with
N\models T
such that
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
).

Definition 2.4.12
.

We call types
p(x)
and
q(x)

separable
for a theory
T
if there are
\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)
and
\psi(x)\not\in q(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
. We say that a theory
T

separates types
if any two distinct types are separable for
T
.

So a theory
T
is Hausdorff iff
T^{\textup{pc}}
separates types.

Lemma 2.4.13
.

Let
M
be a model of some theory
T
and let
a\in M
. Write
\pi(x)=\tp(a;M)
and let
p(x)\in\operatorname{S}(T)
be a maximal type with
\pi(x)\subseteq p(x)
. Then there is a homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
and
N\models p(f(a))
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\Diag(M)\cup p(a)
is consistent. So let
\varphi(a,b)\in\Diag(M)
where
b
is a tuple of parameters from
M
disjoint from
a
. Then
\exists y\varphi(a,y)\in\pi(a)\subseteq p(a)
. As
p(a)
is consistent, we conclude by compactness that
\Diag(M)\cup p(a)
is consistent.
∎

Proposition 2.4.14
.

The following are equivalent for a theory
T
:

(i)

T
separates types;

(ii)

for any
M\models T
and
a\in M
there is some type
p(x)
such that for any homomorphism
f:M\to N
where
N
is a p.c. model of
T
we have that
N\models p(f(a))
;

(iii)

T
has APh.

Proof.

We prove (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Suppose for a contradiction that there are homomorphisms
f:M\to N
and
g:M\to N^{\prime}
, where
N
and
N^{\prime}
are p.c. models, such that
\tp(f(a);N)\neq\tp(g(a);N^{\prime})
. As
T
separates types we find
\varphi(x)\not\in\tp(f(a);N)
and
\psi(x)\not\in\tp(g(a);N^{\prime})
such that
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
. Then we must either have
M\models\varphi(a)
or
M\models\psi(a)
. In the first case we get
N\models\varphi(f(a))
, contradicting
\varphi(x)\not\in\tp(f(a);N)
, and in the second case we get
N^{\prime}\models\psi(g(a))
, contradicting
\psi(x)\not\in\tp(g(a);N^{\prime})
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Let
p(x)
and
q(x)
be two distinct types and consider the set of formulas

\Sigma(x)=\{\neg\varphi(x):\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)\text{ or }\varphi(x)\not\in q(x)\}.

We claim that
\Sigma(x)
is inconsistent with
T
. If it were consistent then there would be a model
M
of
T
and some
a\in M
with
M\models\Sigma(a)
. By construction
\tp(a;M)\subseteq p(x)\cap q(x)
. So by ?THM?
there are homomorphisms
f:M\to N
and
g:M\to N
into models of
T
, which we may assume to be p.c. models by ?THM?
, such that
\tp(f(a);N)=p(x)
and
\tp(g(a);N^{\prime})=q(x)
. This contradicts (ii), and so
\Sigma(x)
must be inconsistent with
T
.

There are thus
\neg\varphi_{1}(x),\ldots,\neg\varphi_{n}(x)\in\Sigma(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\varphi_{1}(x)\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n}(x))
. Let
I=\{i:\varphi_{i}(x)\not\in p(x)\}
and
J=\{j:\varphi_{j}(x)\not\in q(x)\}
. By definition of
\Sigma(x)
we have that
I\cup J=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Let
\psi_{I}(x)=\bigvee_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}(x)
and
\psi_{J}(x)=\bigvee_{i\in J}\varphi_{i}(x)
. Then
T\models\forall x(\psi_{I}(x)\vee\psi_{J}(x))
while
\psi_{I}(x)\not\in p(x)
and
\psi_{J}(x)\not\in q(x)
, so
T
separates
p(x)
and
q(x)
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Let
M\xleftarrow{f}M_{0}\xrightarrow{g}M^{\prime}
be a span of homomorphisms. We may assume
M
and
M^{\prime}
to be p.c. models. Let
a
be a tuple that enumerates
M_{0}
. By assumption
\tp(f(a);M)=\tp(g(a);M^{\prime})
. So we find the required amalgamation by ?THM?
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Let
M\models T
and
a\in M
. Continue
M
into some p.c. model
N
by a homomorphism
f:M\to N
. We claim that
p(x)=\tp(f(a);N)
is as described in (ii). Let
g:M\to N^{\prime}
be any other homomorphism with
N^{\prime}
a p.c. model. By APh we find
N\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}N^{*}\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}N^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
. We thus see that
\tp(g(a);N^{\prime})=\tp(g^{\prime}g(a);N^{*})=\tp(f^{\prime}f(a);N^{*})=\tp(f(a);N)=p(x)
, where we used that
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
are immersions because
N
and
N^{\prime}
are p.c. models.
∎

Proposition 2.4.15
.

The following are equivalent for a theory
T
:

(i)

T
is Hausdorff;

(ii)

\operatorname{S}_{I}(T)
is a Hausdorff space for all index sets
I
;

(iii)

T^{\textup{pc}}
separates types;

(iv)

T^{\textup{pc}}
has APh.

Proof.

The equivalence (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) is just a reformulation and (iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv) is ?THM?
. It remains to show (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii).

Being a Hausdorff space can be formulated as follows: for any distinct points
p
and
q
there are closed sets
A
and
B
such that
p\not\in A
and
q\not\in B
while
A\cup B
is the entire space. With this in mind (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follows easily: for distinct types
p(x)
and
q(x)
we
\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)
and
\psi(x)\not\in q(x)
such that
T^{\textup{pc}}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
and consider the closed sets
[\varphi(x)]
and
[\psi(x)]
.

We prove (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Let
p(x)
and
q(x)
be distinct types and let
[\pi(x)]
and
[\rho(x)]
be such that
p(x)\not\in[\pi(x)]
and
q(x)\not\in[\rho(x)]
while
[\pi(x)]\vee[\rho(x)]
is the entire space. Here we used that any closed set in
\operatorname{S}_{I}(T)
is of the form
[\sigma(x)]
for some set of formulas
\sigma(x)
. As
p(x)\not\in[\pi(x)]
by definition means that
\pi(x)\not\subseteq p(x)
, we find
\varphi(x)\in\pi(x)
such that
\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)
. Similarly we find
\psi(x)\in\rho(x)
such that
\psi(x)\not\in q(x)
. We have
[\pi(x)]\subseteq[\varphi(x)]
and
[\rho(x)]\subseteq[\psi(x)]
so
[\varphi(x)]\cup[\psi(x)]=\operatorname{S}_{I}(T)
which means that
T^{\textup{pc}}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
, as required.
∎

We now consider some examples to show that none of the implications in ?THM?
are reversible and that non-thick theories exist.

Example 2.4.16
.

We give an example of a Hausdorff non-Boolean theory. Write
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:0<q<1\}
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be the language that has a constant symbol for each element of
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}
and an order symbol
\leq
. Considering the obvious
\mathcal{L}
-structure on
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}
we let
T
be the set of h-inductive sentences that are true in
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}
.

Let
[0,1]
be the real unit interval. We claim that any model
M
of
T
admits a unique homomorphism into
[0,1]
. For a singleton
a\in M
we let
L_{a}=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}:M\models q\leq a\}
and
R_{a}=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}:M\models a\leq q\}
. As
T
specifies that
\leq
is a linear order, this determines a Dedekind cut in
[0,1]
and so there is a unique
r_{a}\in[0,1]
such that
q_{1}\leq r_{a}\leq q_{2}
for all
q_{1}\in L_{a}
and
q_{2}\in R_{a}
. We can thus define a homomorphism by
M\to[0,1]
be sending
a
to
r_{a}
, and clearly every homomorphism must send
a
to
r_{a}
.

There are two consequences of the above claim. Firstly, it means that the real unit interval
[0,1]
is a maximal p.c. model for this theory. This means that
T
is not Boolean because there is an infinite maximal p.c. model (cf. ?THM?
).

Secondly, it means that
T
, and hence
T^{\textup{pc}}
, has APh. So
T
is Hausdorff by ?THM?
. Indeed, any span of homomorphisms
M\leftarrow M_{0}\to M^{\prime}
between models of
T
can be completed to a square by composing with the homomorphisms
M\to[0,1]\leftarrow M^{\prime}
. This square then commutes by uniqueness of the homomorphism
M_{0}\to[0,1]
.

Example 2.4.17
.

We give an example of a semi-Hausdorff non-Hausdorff theory. For this we use the theory
T
from ?THM?
, which has
\omega
many constants
c_{n}
and declares them to be distinct. There is then only one p.c. model (up to isomorphism), namely
\omega
with
c_{n}
interpreted as
n
. Then for any tuples
a
and
b
we have
a\equiv b
if and only if
a
and
b
are equal to the same tuple of constants if and only if
a=b
. So
T
is semi-Hausdorff. To show that
T
is not Hausdorff we show that APh fails for
T^{\textup{pc}}
. Using ?THM?
one quickly checks that
T
, and hence
T^{\textup{pc}}
, has positive quantifier elimination, from which it quickly follows that
T^{\textup{pc}}
does not specify anything more than
T
does. That is,
T
and
T^{\textup{pc}}
are logically equivalent. Let
M
be
\omega
together with one extra point
*
, which is then a model of
T^{\textup{pc}}
. We define a homomorphism
f_{1}:M\to\omega
by taking the identity on
\omega
and setting
f_{1}(*)=1
. Similarly we define
f_{2}:M\to\omega
with
f_{2}(*)=2
. The span
\omega\xleftarrow{f_{1}}M\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\omega
cannot be amalgamated.

Example 2.4.18
.

We give an example of a thick theory that is not semi-Hausdorff. Consider the signature
\mathcal{L}
with unary relation symbols
P_{n}
and
P^{\prime}_{n}
for all
n<\omega
, and a binary relation symbol
R
. We define the
\mathcal{L}
-structure
M=\{a_{n},b_{n}:n<\omega\}
as follows. The interpretation of
P_{n}
is
\{a_{n},b_{n}\}
and
P^{\prime}_{n}
is the complement of
P_{n}
. We take
R
to be the symmetric anti-reflexive relation
\{(a_{n},b_{n}),(b_{n},a_{n}):n<\omega\}
, so
R
is the inequality relation on each
P_{n}
. Let
T
be the h-inductive theory of
M
. Then
M
is a p.c. model of
T
. There is a maximal p.c. model
N
of
T
given by
N=M\cup\{a_{\omega},b_{\omega}\}
, where
N\models P^{\prime}_{n}(a_{\omega})\wedge P^{\prime}_{n}(b_{\omega})
for all
n<\omega
and also
N\models R(a_{\omega},b_{\omega})\wedge R(b_{\omega},a_{\omega})
.

Since
N
is maximal, the only indiscernible sequences are the constant ones. So
T
is a thick theory. However,
T
is not semi-Hausdorff. To see this, suppose for a contradiction that
\Omega(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
is a partial type such that for any
c_{1},c_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\in N
we have
N\models\Omega(c_{1},c_{2},d_{1},d_{2})
if and only if
\tp(c_{1},c_{2};N)=\tp(d_{1},d_{2};N)
. For
n<\omega
define the set of formulas

\Sigma_{n}(x,z_{1},z_{2})=\Omega(x,z_{1},x,z_{2})\cup\{R(z_{1},z_{2})\}\cup\{P^{\prime}_{k}(x)\wedge P^{\prime}_{k}(z_{1})\wedge P^{\prime}_{k}(z_{2}):k<n\},

and set
\Sigma(x,z_{1},z_{2})=\bigcup_{n<\omega}\Sigma_{n}(x,z_{1},z_{2})
. For any
n<\omega
we have that
N\models\Sigma_{n}(a_{\omega},a_{n},b_{n})
, so by compactness there is a p.c. model of
T
that contains a realisation of
\Sigma(x,z_{1},z_{2})
. By maximality of
N
such a realisation must exist in
N
. That is, there are
c,d,e\in N
such that
N\models\Sigma(c,d,e)
. By construction of
\Sigma(x,z_{1},z_{2})
we have
\{d,e\}=\{a_{\omega},b_{\omega}\}
and
c
is one of
a_{\omega}
or
b_{\omega}
. Without loss of generality we can thus assume
N\models\Sigma(a_{\omega},a_{\omega},b_{\omega})
. Writing
p(x,y)=\tp(a_{\omega},a_{\omega};N)
and
q(x,y)=\tp(a_{\omega},b_{\omega};N)
we must then have
p(x,y)=q(x,y)
. This is a contradiction, because
p(x,y)
contains the formula
x=y
, while
q(x,y)
contains an obstruction of this formula, namely
R(x,y)
. We conclude that
T
is not semi-Hausdorff.

Example 2.4.19
.

We show that the theory from ?THM?
is not thick. Recall that our signature contains unary relation symbols
P_{n}
for all
n<\omega
and an inequality symbol
\neq
. The theory
T
expresses that
P_{n}
and
P_{k}
are disjoint for all
n\neq k
and that the inequality symbol is indeed inequality. The p.c. models of
T
then consist of an infinite set for each
P_{n}
.

We claim that
T
has positive quantifier elimination. We will use ?THM?
, so let
\exists y\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y)
be a positive formula where
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y)
is a conjunction of atomic formulas and
y
is a single variable. We distinguish two cases.

•

One of the atomic formulas in
\varphi
is of the form
x_{i}=y
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Then we may replace all occurrences of
y
by
x_{i}
and thus eliminate the quantifier.

•

The variable
y
does not appear in any equality in
\varphi
. If the atomic formulas involving
y
contain a contradiction (e.g.,
y\neq y
or
P_{n}(y)\wedge P_{k}(y)
for
n\neq k
) then the entire formula is equivalent to
\bot
. Otherwise we can safely remove all atomic formulas involving
y
from
\varphi
and thus eliminate the quantifier.

Now suppose for a contradiction that
T
is thick. Let
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
express (in p.c. models) that
(x_{i})_{i<\omega}
is an indiscernible sequence. Then
\{P_{0}(x_{0})\wedge P_{1}(x_{1})\}\cup\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
cannot be realised in a p.c. model, so by compactness there is some
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
that is a finite conjunction of formulas in
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
, such that
\{P_{0}(x_{0})\wedge P_{1}(x_{1}),\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\}
is inconsistent with
T
. That is,
\exists(x_{i})_{2\leq i\leq N}\varphi(x_{0},\ldots,x_{N})
is an obstruction of
P_{0}(x_{0})\wedge P_{1}(x_{1})
. By positive quantifier elimination, this obstruction is equivalent to a formula

\bigvee_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}(x_{0},x_{1}),

where each
\varphi_{i}(x_{0},x_{1})
is a conjunction of atomic formulas. As every
\varphi_{i}(x_{0},x_{1})
must be an obstruction of
P_{0}(x_{0})\wedge P_{1}(x_{1})
, it must contain at least one of the following atomic formulas:

•

P_{n}(x_{0})
for some
n<\omega
,

•

P_{n}(x_{1})
for some
n<\omega
,

•

x_{0}=x_{1}
.

Let
k<\omega
be such that
P_{k}
is not mentioned in any
\varphi_{i}(x_{0},x_{1})
. Let
M
be some p.c. model of
T
and let
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
be distinct elements in
P_{k}(M)
. Then
M\models\Theta((a_{i})_{i<\omega})
. However, by choice of
k
we have
M\not\models\varphi_{i}(a_{0},a_{1})
for all
i\in I
and so
M\not\models\varphi(a_{0},\ldots,a_{N})
, contradicting that this is a finite conjunction of formulas in
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
.

2.5
Bibliographic remarks

The basics of positive logic (i.e., the contents of Sections
2.1
and
2.3
) appear in, for example,
[
BY03a
,
BYP07
,
PY18
]
. There is also
[
Hod93
, Chapter 8]
, which treats what we called Pillay theories (see ?THM?
). So the set up there is slightly less general, but the proofs are really the same and are easily adapted to our more general setting. A lot of the current terminology is based on this less general setting (e.g., “joint continuation property” versus “joint embedding property” and “p.c. model” versus “e.c. model”). Some literature actually uses the older terminology, even in the more general setting for positive logic (see also ?THM?
). By working in a
\Delta_{\textup{qf}}
-Morleyised theory the older notions are all obtained as a special case of the newer notions. For example: homomorphisms are precisely embeddings of structures, h-inductive and h-universal sentences are precisely inductive and universal sentences respectively (also called
\forall\exists
-sentences and
\forall
-sentences respectively) and a continuation of structures is just an extension. Finally, because homomorphisms can be viewed as just inclusions of structures, directed unions are genuine unions.

Our treatment of type spaces follows
[
BY03a
]
. Another approach is possible, which we discuss in the remark below.

Remark 2.5.1
.

In
[
Hay19
]
