a different kind of type for positive logic is considered. There all sets of formulas of the form
\tp(a;M)
are taken, where
M
is just some model of a fixed theory
T
. So
M
is not necessarily p.c. This corresponds to taking prime filters on the distributive lattice of definable sets of
T
. We will call these
prime types
.

This way another topological space can be defined, for which we will write
\operatorname{S}_{n}^{\prime}(T)
(to distinguish it from the space
\operatorname{S}_{n}(T)
of maximal types). The points are prime types, and a basis of open sets is given by the subsets of the form

[\varphi(x)]=\{p(x)\in\operatorname{S}_{n}^{\prime}(T):\varphi(x)\in p(x)\}.

This will yield a
spectral space
. This is precisely the same approach as is standard in full first-order logic. The latter can be viewed as an instance of the duality Boolean algebras and Stone spaces, namely with definable sets in
n
variables on the Boolean algebra side and the Stone space of types on the topological side. This duality generalises to one between distributive lattices and spectral spaces, of which the distributive lattice of positively definable sets together with the space
\operatorname{S}_{n}^{\prime}(T)
forms an instance.

There is a general philosophy in model theory that a theory is ‘the same’ as the collection of its type spaces. For both
\operatorname{S}(T)
and
\operatorname{S}^{\prime}(T)
this can be made precise.

•

In
[
BY03a
, Theorem 2.38]
it is made precise how a theory can be recovered from the collection of type spaces ‘like’ ones of the form
\operatorname{S}_{n}(T)
. If we start with a theory
T
, consider its type spaces and then recover a theory
T^{\prime}
from that we can never hope that
T
and
T^{\prime}
are exactly the same. This is because
\operatorname{S}_{n}(T)
only detects types in p.c. models, and there can be many different theories with the same p.c. models (see e.g. ?THM?
). Another problem is that all that the type space
\operatorname{S}_{n}(T)
sees are type-definable sets, because these correspond to the closed sets in the type space and there is no way to distinguish between formulas and sets of formulas. However, non of these things are relevant for the model-theoretic properties of the theories involved. For example,
T
and
T^{\prime}
will have monster models with the same automorphism groups.

•

In
[
Kam23b
, Theorem 1.1]
a duality is described between positive theories and collections of types spaces ‘like’ ones of the form
\operatorname{S}_{n}^{\prime}(T)
. It turns out that this approach does offer enough detail to recover a theory up to logical equivalence (if the language is fixed, otherwise up to some appropriate isomorphism).

In ?THM?
(i) we defined Pillay and Robinson theories. This is a very common setup in model theory and is thus deserving of its own name (see also
[
Hod93
, Chapter 8]
). In 1998, Hrushovski introduced the name Robinson theory
[
Hru98
]
, based on the substantial amount of work that Robinson had done in this setup. Later, Pillay developed simplicity theory in a similar setting
[
Pil00
]
, but an important difference was that the amalgamation property was no longer required (something that we have seen implies quite good behaviour, namely that the theory is Hausdorff, see ?THM?
). Therefore, it seemed fitting to use the name Pillay theories for Robinson theories without the amalgamation property.

The properties Hausdorff and semi-Hausdorff were defined in
[
BY03a
, Definition 1.41]
. They were defined in topological terms, hence the terminology, and a topological proof is given that Hausdorff implies semi-Hausdorff. Our presentation focuses on the logical aspect. Thickness is introduced in
[
BY03c
]
. The fact that being Hausdorff is equivalent to APh (?THM?
) is
[
PY18
, Theorem 8]
.

The examples of a non-Boolean Hausdorff theory (?THM?
) and a thick theory that is not semi-Hausdorff (?THM?
) are taken from
[
DGK23
, Example 3.12]
and
[
Poi10
, Section 4]
respectively. Although, in both cases they served a completely different purpose. The example of a semi-Hausdorff theory that is not Hausdorff (?THM?
) is essentially
[
PY18
, Example 4]
. These examples, and the other examples we gave in Section
2.4
, are elementary but rather artificial. More natural examples are known.

•

Existentially closed exponential fields are Hausdorff and non-Boolean. These are studied in
[
HK21
]
, with not being Boolean being established in
[
HK21
, Corollary 3.8]
. The fact that this example is Hausdorff is established in
[
DK22
, Proposition 10.4]
, see also the discussion after that proposition.

•

Bilinear spaces over a fixed field can be studied as a positive theory. This is done in
[
Kam23a
]
. If the field is finite then the theory is Boolean, and we are in the well-known setting of studying bilinear spaces over a finite field in full first-order logic. However, if the field is infinite then the theory is semi-Hausdorff and not Hausdorff
[
Kam23a
, Proposition 4.14]
.

•

Ultrametric spaces with distances in
\mathbb{N}
are known to be not thick. These are first discussed in
[
BY03b
, Example 4.3]
, and non-thickness is established as a consequence of the general theory of simplicity in positive logic. That is (referring forward to material from later on in these notes),
[
BY03b
, Example 4.3]
establishes that the type of a single element over the empty set has no non-dividing extensions. So dividing independence does not satisfy
full existence
. At the same time, the theory is shown to be stable and thus in particular simple (?THM?
), but in thick simple theories dividing independence satisfies
full existence
(?THM?
).

For more information about geometric logic, as mentioned in ?THM?
, see for example
[
Joh02
, Chapter D1]
. There one can also find more on the topos-theoretic approach to positive logic, under the name coherent logic, as briefly discussed after ?THM?
.

Another use for positive logic in the context categorical logic is in accessible categories. For example, accessible categories can be characterised as the categories of models of infinitary positive theories. More can be found in
[
AR94
, Chapter 5]
, where positive theories are called basic theories.

Chapter 3
Countable categoricity

We provide a characterisation of theories that have one countable p.c. model (up to isomorphism), i.e.
countably categorical theories
, see ?THM?
. In full first-order logic this is usually referred to as the Ryll-Nardzewski theorem, and all the usual characterisations go through except for having finite type spaces (see ?THM?
).

Along the way we establish a positive version of multiple model-theoretically important notions and results, such as omitting types (?THM?
), back-and-forth (?THM?
), saturation (Section
3.3
), atomic models (Section
3.4
) and prime models (Section
3.5
).

3.1
Omitting types

We give a criterion for when a subset of an arbitrary structure is in fact a p.c. model of some given theory. This reminds of Tarski’s test for full first-order logic and the positive logic version is due to Haykazyan, hence the name.

Lemma 3.1.1

(Haykazyan’s test)
.

Let
T
be a theory and
M\models T
. Suppose that
A\subseteq M
is a subset such that for every
a\in A
and all quantifier-free
\varphi(x,y)
one of the following holds:

•

there is
b\in A
such that
M\models\varphi(a,b)
,

•

there is quantifier-free
\psi(x,z)
and
c\in A
such that
M\models\psi(a,c)
and
T\models\neg\exists xyz(\varphi(x,y)\wedge\psi(x,z))
.

Then
A
is a p.c. model of
T
.

Proof.

We claim that for every quantifier-free formula
\varphi(x,y)
and all
a\in A
we have that if
M\models\exists y\varphi(a,y)
then there is
b\in A
such that
M\models\varphi(a,b)
. Suppose for a contradiction that this is not the case. Then there is quantifier-free
\psi(x,z)
and
c\in A
such that
M\models\psi(a,c)
and
T\models\neg\exists xyz(\varphi(x,y)\wedge\psi(x,z))
. As
M\models T
this in particular implies that
M\not\models\exists xy\varphi(x,y)
, contradicting that
M\models\exists y\varphi(a,y)
.

Using the claim, we can now easily check that
A
is a substructure. For that we need to check that it is closed under constant symbols and function symbols
1
1

1

If one insists that structures are non-empty then
M
is non-empty, so
M\models\exists y(y=y)
and hence by the claim there is
b\in A
(with
M\models b=b
). If we allow empty structures then
M
can be empty, and so
A=M
will be empty. This will still be a p.c. model, and this case does actually not require special treatment, but we still explain what happens. The assumption implies there will be some
\psi
, which now has to be propositional, such that
M\models\psi
and
T\models\psi\to\neg\exists x(x=x)
. So any continuation of
M
will be empty.
. So let
c
be a constant symbol. Then
M\models\exists y(y=c)
, and so there is
b\in A
with
M\models b=c
, thus
c\in A
. Similarly, if
f
is an
n
-ary function symbol and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
then
y=f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
is a formula with parameters in
A
and because
M\models\exists y(y=f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))
, there is
b\in A
with
M\models b=f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
and hence
f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in A
.

So by construction
A\subseteq M
is an embedding of structures. By ?THM?
we have that
A\models T^{u}
. We will show that
A
is a p.c. model of
T^{\textup{u}}
, which by ?THM?
implies that
A
is a p.c. model of
T
. For this, we verify ?THM?
(iii). Let
\chi(x)
be any formula and write it as
\exists y\varphi(x,y)
, where
\varphi(x,y)
is quantifier-free. Let
a\in A
be such that
A\not\models\chi(a)
. Then there is no
b\in A
such that
M\models\varphi(a,b)
, as that would imply
A\models\varphi(a,b)
. There must thus be quantifier-free
\psi(x,z)
and some
c\in A
such that
M\models\psi(a,c)
and
T\models\neg\exists xyz(\varphi(x,y)\wedge\psi(x,z))
. Therefore
A\models\psi(a,c)
and
T^{\textup{u}}\models\neg\exists xyz(\varphi(x,y)\wedge\psi(x,z))
. So
\exists z\psi(x,z)
is an obstruction of
\chi(x)
modulo
T^{\textup{u}}
and
A\models\exists z\psi(a,z)
, as required.
∎

In full first-order logic we call a type
isolated
if there is a formula implying the entire type. The term comes from the fact that this corresponds to the type being an isolated point in the type space. In positive logic we can use the same idea, but such types are no longer necessarily isolated points, so we change the terminology.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
T
be a theory. We call a partial type
\Sigma(x)
in finitely many variables
supported
if there is
\varphi(x)
such that
T\cup\{\exists x\varphi(x)\}
is consistent and for all
\chi(x)\in\Sigma(x)
we have

T^{\textup{pc}}\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\chi(x)).

In this case we call
\varphi(x)
the
support
of
\Sigma(x)
.

Lemma 3.1.3
.

Let
T
be a theory. Let
p(x)
be a type. Then
p(x)
is supported by
\varphi(x)
if and only if
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
and for every
\psi(x)\not\in p(x)
we have
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
.

Proof.

We first prove the left to right direction. Let
\psi(x)\not\in p(x)
and assume for a contradiction that there is a model
M
of
T
with
a\in M
such that
M\models\varphi(a)\wedge\psi(a)
. We may assume that
M
is a p.c. model. Write
q(x)=\tp(a;M)
. As
\varphi(x)
implies every formula in
p(x)
modulo
T^{\textup{pc}}
we have
p(x)\subseteq q(x)
, and hence
p(x)=q(x)
by maximality. We arrive at a contradiction, as then
\psi(x)\in p(x)
. To see that
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
we again assume for a contradiction that this is not the case. By what we have just established we must then have
T\models\neg\exists x\varphi(x)
, contradicting that
T\cup\{\exists x\varphi(x)\}
is consistent.

We now prove the converse. Firstly,
T\cup\{\exists x\varphi(x)\}
is consistent as
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
and
p(x)
is realised in some model of
T
. Now let
\chi(x)\in p(x)
. Suppose for a contradiction that
T^{\textup{pc}}\not\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\chi(x))
. Then there is a p.c. model
M
of
T
and some
a\in M
with
M\models\varphi(a)
and
M\not\models\chi(a)
. There is thus an obstruction
\psi(x)
of
\chi(x)
such that
M\models\psi(a)
. As
\chi(x)\in p(x)
, we must have
\psi(x)\not\in p(x)
. So by our assumption on
\varphi(x)
we must have
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
, contradicting
M\models\varphi(a)\wedge\psi(a)
.
∎

Proposition 3.1.4
.

If a theory
T
has JCP then every p.c. model of
T
realises all supported types.

Proof.

Let
M
be any p.c. model and let
p(x)
be a type that is supported by some
\varphi(x)
. By ?THM?
we have
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
. Let
M^{\prime}
be a p.c. model of
T
that realises
p(x)
, so in particular
M^{\prime}\models\exists x\varphi(x)
. As
T
has JCP there is a model
N
of
T
with immersions
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
. So
N\models\exists x\varphi(x)
and hence
M\models\exists x\varphi(x)
. Let
a\in M
be such that
M\models\varphi(a)
. As
M\models T^{\textup{pc}}
and because
\varphi(x)
supports
p(x)
we have
M\models p(a)
, and so
p(x)
is realised in
M
.
∎

By the above proposition, supported types have to be realised (if the theory has JCP). As is the case with isolated types in full first-order logic, these are the only types that are necessarily realised by p.c. models (if
T
is countable), as the following theorem shows.

Theorem 3.1.5

(Omitting types)
.

Let
T
be a countable theory. If
\Sigma(x)
is a partial type in finitely many variables that is not supported then there is a p.c. model
M
of
T
that omits
\Sigma(x)
. That is, for all
a\in M
we have
M\not\models\Sigma(a)
.

Proof.

Write
\Sigma(x)=\Sigma(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are all single variables.

Let
C=\{c_{i}\}_{i<\omega}
be a set of new constant symbols. We will construct a set of sentences
\Gamma
in this extended language that is consistent with
T
and satisfies the following properties.

(1)

For every quantifier-free formula
\varphi(y,z)
in the original language and any tuple
a\in C
one of the following holds:

(i)

there is a tuple
b\in C
for which we have
\varphi(a,b)\in\Gamma
,

(ii)

there is a quantifier-free
\psi(y,w)
in the original language and a tuple
c\in C
such that
\psi(a,c)\in\Gamma
and
T\models\neg\exists yzw(\varphi(y,z)\wedge\psi(y,w))
.

(2)

For all
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}\subseteq\omega
there is an obstruction
\psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of
\Sigma(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
such that
\psi(c_{i_{1}},\ldots,c_{i_{n}})\in\Gamma
.

We only defined what it means to be “an obstruction” of a formula, but this straightforwardly extends to sets of formulas:
\psi(x)
is an obstruction of
\Sigma(x)
if
\{\psi(x)\}\cup\Sigma(x)
is inconsistent with
T
. Equivalently, using compactness, if there are
\varphi_{1}(x),\ldots,\varphi_{k}(x)\in\Sigma(x)
such that
\psi(x)
is an obstruction of
\varphi_{1}(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{k}(x)
modulo
T
.

We will construct
\Gamma
as the union of a countable chain
\emptyset=\Gamma_{0}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\subseteq\ldots
, such that each
\Gamma_{i}
is finite. We alternate constructions between even and odd stages. For this we let
(\varphi_{i}(a_{i},z))_{i<\omega}
be an enumeration of all quantifier-free formulas in the language extended by the constant symbols in
C
. We also let
(\bar{c}_{i})_{i<\omega}
be an enumeration of
C^{n}
.

Having constructed
\Gamma_{2i}
, we let
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
be the finite subset of constants that appear in
\Gamma_{2i}
. Let
M
be a p.c. model of
T
that realises
\Gamma_{2i}
, and interpret any constants from
a_{i}
that do not appear in
\Gamma_{2i}
arbitrarily in
M
. To construct
\Gamma_{2i+1}
we distinguish two cases.

(i)

If
M\models\exists z\varphi_{i}(a_{i},z)
then let
b\in M
be such that
M\models\varphi_{i}(a_{i},b)
. For each element in
b
that does not correspond to the interpretation of a constant symbol from
C
, we pick a constant symbol from
C\setminus C^{\prime}a_{i}
so that
b
becomes the interpretation of constants from
C
. Set
\Gamma_{2i+1}=\Gamma_{2i}\cup\{\varphi_{i}(a_{i},b)\}
, which is consistent with
T
as
M
is a model.

(ii)

If
M\not\models\exists z\varphi_{i}(a_{i},z)
then, because
M
is a p.c. model, there is an obstruction
\theta(y)
of
\exists z\varphi_{i}(y,z)
such that
M\models\theta(a_{i})
. Write
\theta(y)
as
\exists w\psi(y,w)
with
\psi(y,w)
quantifier-free, so
T\models\neg\exists yzw(\varphi(y,z)\wedge\psi(y,w))
. Let
c\in M
be such that
M\models\psi(a_{i},c)
. For each element in
c
that does not correspond to the interpretation of a constant symbol from
C
, we pick a constant symbol from
C\setminus C^{\prime}a_{i}
so that
c
becomes the interpretation of constants from
C
. Set
\Gamma_{2i+1}=\Gamma_{2i}\cup\{\psi(a_{i},c)\}
, which is consistent with
T
as
M
is a model.

This completes the construction of
\Gamma_{2i+1}
.

Now assume that we have constructed
\Gamma_{2i+1}
. Then there is a formula
\theta(x,y)
such that
\Gamma_{2i+1}
is equivalent to
\theta(\bar{c}_{i},d)
, where
d
is a tuple of constants from
C
that is disjoint from
\bar{c}_{i}
. As
\exists y\theta(x,y)
does not support
\Sigma(x)
, there must be
\chi(x)\in\Sigma(x)
and some p.c. model
M
of
T
with
\bar{a}\in M
such that
M\models\exists y\theta(\bar{a},y)
and
M\not\models\chi(\bar{a})
. As
M
is a p.c. model, there is an obstruction
\psi(x)
of
\chi(x)
(and hence of
\Sigma(x)
) such that
M\models\psi(\bar{a})
. Let
b\in M
be such that
M\models\theta(\bar{a},b)
. Interpret the constant symbols in
\bar{c}_{i}
and
d
as
\bar{a}
and
b
respectively. Setting
\Gamma_{2i+2}=\Gamma_{2i+1}\cup\{\psi(\bar{c}_{i})\}
we then have
M\models\Gamma_{2i+2}
, and so
\Gamma_{2i+2}
is consistent with
T
.

This completes the construction of
\Gamma
. Now let
N
be a model of
T\cup\Gamma
. Let
M\subseteq N
be the set enumerated by constant symbols in
C
. Property (1) of
\Gamma
tells us that ?THM?
applies, and so we have that
M
is a p.c. model of
T
. At the same time, property (2) of
\Gamma
tells us that no tuple in
M
can realise
\Sigma(x)
. So
M
is indeed the required p.c. model that omits
\Sigma(x)
.
∎

3.2
Back-and-forth

It often happens in model theory that we build isomorphisms using a back-and-forth argument. Such arguments work just as well in positive logic. In this section we give a technical setup and work out the details so that we can directly apply it in many situations.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
M
and
N
be two structures in the same signature. A partial function
f:M\to N
is called a
partial immersion
if for all
a\in\dom(f)
and for every formula
\varphi(x)
we have

M\models\varphi(a)\Longleftrightarrow N\models\varphi(f(a)).

Note that if
f
is the empty function then
f
is a partial immersion precisely when
M\models\varphi
if and only if
N\models\varphi
for every sentence
\varphi
(see also the discussion after ?THM?
).

Definition 3.2.2
.

Let
M
and
N
be two structures in the same signature, and let
\kappa
be a cardinal. We say that
M
and
N
are in
\kappa
-
back-and-forth correspondence
if:

(i)

M\models\varphi
if and only if
N\models\varphi
for every sentence
\varphi
,

(ii)

for every partial immersion
f:M\to N
with
|\dom(f)|<\kappa
and any singleton
a\in M
there is
b\in N
such that
f
can be extended to a partial immersion that sends
a
to
b
,

(iii)

for every partial immersion
f:M\to N
with
|\dom(f)|<\kappa
and any singleton
b\in N
there is
a\in M
such that
f
can be extended to a partial immersion that sends
a
to
b
.

The difference between (ii) and (iii) in the above definition is that that in (ii) we start with
a\in M
and find
b\in N
, and in (iii) this is the other way around.

Remark 3.2.3
.

It is often the case that two structures
M
and
N
, which we want to prove are in
\kappa
-back-and-forth correspondence, are in fact p.c. models of some theory
T
with JCP. In this case, (i) in ?THM?
is automatic. Indeed, the characterisation in ?THM?
tells us that
T^{\textup{pc}}
is precisely the set of h-inductive sentences true in
M
, and the same for
N
. The claim then follows because any sentence can in particular be viewed as an h-inductive sentence.

Theorem 3.2.4

(Back-and-forth)
.

Suppose that
M
and
N
are two structures of cardinality at most
\kappa
that are in
\kappa
-back-and-forth correspondence. Then
M
and
N
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Choose enumerations
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
and
(b_{i})_{i<\kappa}
of
M
and
N
respectively (possibly repeating elements if their cardinalities are less than
\kappa
). We will inductively construct bijections
f_{i}:A_{i}\to B_{i}
, such that for all
i\leq\kappa
:

(1)

f_{i}
extends
f_{j}
for all
j<i
;

(2)

(a_{j})_{j<i}\subseteq A_{i}\subseteq M
;

(3)

(b_{j})_{j<i}\subseteq B_{i}\subseteq N
;

(4)

f_{i}
is a partial immersion
M\to N
;

(5)

|A_{i}|=|B_{i}|<\kappa
(except for
i=\kappa
).

Clearly,
f_{\kappa}
would then be the desired isomorphism. At limit stages we take unions. For the base case we take the empty function, which trivially satisfies (1)–(3) and satisfies (4) because
M
and
N
are in
\kappa
-back-and-forth correspondence (see ?THM?
(i)).

That leaves the successor case. Let
f_{i}
be constructed. Then
A_{i}=\dom(f_{i})
has cardinality less than
\kappa
by the induction hypothesis. So using the
\kappa
-back-and-forth correspondence of
M
and
N
there is
b\in N
such that we can extend
f_{i}
to a partial immersion
g:A_{i}\cup\{a_{i}\}\to B_{i}\cup\{b\}
by setting
g(a_{i})=b
. It follows immediately that
g
is surjective. Injectivity follows from being a partial immersion, and so
g
is a bijection. Again using
\kappa
-back-and-forth correspondence of
M
and
N
, this time applied to
g
, we find
a\in M
such that we can extend
g
to a partial immersion
f_{i+1}:A_{i}\cup\{a_{i},a\}\to B_{i}\cup\{b,b_{i}\}
by setting
f_{i+1}(a)=b_{i}
, which is again a bijection. We set
A_{i+1}=A_{i}\cup\{a_{i},a\}
and
B_{i+1}=B_{i}\cup\{b,b_{i}\}
. This finishes the construction and hence the proof.
∎

3.3
Positive saturation

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
M
be a structure and
A\subseteq M
be any subset. A
formula over
A
is a formula
\varphi(x,a)
, where
a\in A
. A set
\Sigma(x)
of formulas over
A
is called
finitely satisfiable
in
M
if for any finite subset of formulas
\{\varphi_{1}(x,a_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{n}(x,a_{n})\}\subseteq\Sigma(x)
we have that
M\models\exists x(\varphi_{1}(x,a_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n}(x,a_{n}))
. We say that
\Sigma(x)
is
satisfiable in
M
if there is
b\in M
such that
M\models\Sigma(b)
.

Technically what is happening with the set
A
is that we temporarily add constant symbols to our signature for the elements of
A
, and expand
M
by interpreting these constant symbols as their corresponding elements.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of some theory
T
and let
\Sigma(x)
be a set of formulas over
M
in any number of variables. Then
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
if and only if there is a continuation of
M
to a p.c. model
N
of
T
in which
\Sigma(x)
is satisfiable.

Proof.

For the left to right direction we consider the set of formulas
\Diag(M)\cup\Sigma(x)
. By assumption this is finitely satisfiable in a model of
T
, namely in
M
. By compactness (?THM?
) there is a p.c. model
N
of
T
and
a\in N
such that
N\models\Diag(M)\cup\Sigma(a)
. As
N\models\Diag(M)
there is a homomorphism
M\to N
and so
N
is the required continuation of
M
.

Conversely, let
a\in N
be such that
N\models\Sigma(x)
and let
\Sigma_{0}(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)
be a finite subset. Then
N\models\exists x\bigwedge\Sigma_{0}(x)
. So as
M
is p.c. we have
M\models\exists x\bigwedge\Sigma_{0}(x)
, and we conclude that
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
.
∎

In practice we usually care about satisfiability and realisations of sets of formulas, and so we may as well assume them to be closed under finite conjunctions. Similar to how we used
\bigwedge\Sigma_{0}(x)
in the above proof. This simplifies
\Sigma(x)
being finitely satisfiable in
M
to: for every
\varphi(x,a)\in\Sigma(x)
we have
M\models\exists x\varphi(x,a)
. Throughout, we will implicitly use this notational convenience.

Definition 3.3.3
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal. A structure
M
is called
positively
\kappa
-saturated
if, for every
A\subseteq M
with
|A|<\kappa
, every set
\Sigma(x)
of formulas over
A
in a single variable
x
that is finitely satisfiable in
M
is satisfiable in
M
.

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal. A structure
M
is positively
\kappa
-saturated if and only if for every
A\subseteq M
with
|A|<\kappa
, every set
\Sigma(x)
of formulas over
A
and with
|x|\leq\kappa
that is finitely satisfiable in
M
is satisfiable in
M
.

The difference between ?THM?
and ?THM?
is that in the latter we allow
x
to be of length
\kappa
.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq M
with
|A|<\kappa
and let
\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\kappa})
be a set of formulas over
A
in
\kappa
many variables, where each
x_{i}
is a single variable. We may assume that for any
\varphi(y,z)\in\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\kappa})
, where
y
is a single variable and
z
potentially a tuple of variables, we also have
\exists y\varphi(y,z)\in\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\kappa})
. Indeed, this does not change satisfiability (or finite satisfiability) of
\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\kappa})
.

For
\delta<\kappa
we let
\Sigma_{\delta}((x_{i})_{i<\delta})
be the restriction of
\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\kappa})
to the variables
(x_{i})_{i<\delta}
. That is, we take only those formulas that mention those variables. We inductively build a sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\kappa}
such that for all
\delta<\kappa
we have
M\models\Sigma_{\delta}((b_{i})_{i<\delta})
. The base case and the limit stages are trivial. So now assume we have constructed
(b_{i})_{i<\delta}
. Note that
\Sigma_{\delta+1}(x_{\delta},(b_{i})_{i<\delta})
is a set of formulas in a single variable over
A(b_{i})_{i<\delta}
, and this set of parameters has cardinality
<\kappa
. Therefore, we only need to check that
\Sigma_{\delta+1}(x_{\delta},(b_{i})_{i<\delta})
is finitely satisfiable in
M
, as saturation then gives us a realisation, which is exactly the required
b_{\delta}
.

So let
\varphi(x_{\delta},(b_{i})_{i<\delta})\in\Sigma_{\delta+1}(x_{\delta},(b_{i})_{i<\delta})
, where
\varphi(x_{\delta},(x_{i})_{i<\delta})
is some formula over
A
(only really using finitely many variables from
(x_{i})_{i<\delta}
). Then
\exists x_{\delta}\varphi(x_{\delta},(x_{i})_{i<\delta})\in\Sigma((x_{i})_{i<\kappa})
by our earlier assumption. We conclude that
\exists x_{\delta}\varphi(x_{\delta},(b_{i})_{i<\delta})\in\Sigma_{\delta}((b_{i})_{i<\delta})
and so
M\models\exists x_{\delta}\varphi(x_{\delta},(b_{i})_{i<\delta})
, as required.
∎

Example 3.3.5
.

Positive saturation does not imply being p.c. For example, take the empty theory in the language of pure equality. So the p.c. models are the singletons (see also ?THM?
). Let
M=\{a,b\}
be a two element set. Then any set
\Sigma(x)
of formulas with parameters in
M
can only say that
x
is equal to one of
a
and
b
or to both. The latter is not finitely satisfiable in
M
, and the other options are clearly satisfiable in
M
. So
M
is positively
\kappa
-saturated for all
\kappa
, but
M
is not p.c.

We will generally only be interested in positive saturatedness in p.c. models. In Section
4.1
we will see that we can always construct such p.c. models. However, the lemma below already gives us a criterion for when every p.c. model of a theory is positively
\omega
-saturated, and so in particular establishing their existence.

Lemma 3.3.6
.

Let
T
be a theory and suppose that every type in finitely many variables is supported. Then every p.c. model of
T
is positively
\omega
-saturated.

Proof.

Let
M
be a p.c. model and let
\Sigma(x,b)
be finitely satisfiable in
M
, where
x
and
b\in M
are finite. Then by ?THM?
there is a realisation
a
in some p.c. model
N
that is a continuation of
M
. Set
p(x,y)=\tp(a,b;M)
and let
\varphi(x,y)
be the support of
p(x,y)
. Then
N\models\exists x\varphi(x,b)
so because
M
is p.c. we find
a^{\prime}\in M
with
M\models\varphi(a^{\prime},b)
. As
\varphi
supports
p
we have that
M\models p(a^{\prime},b)
and hence
M\models\Sigma(a^{\prime},b)
because
\Sigma(x,b)\subseteq p(x,b)
by construction.
∎

As usual, we get that positively saturated structures are isomorphic.

Theorem 3.3.7
.

Let
T
be a theory with JCP. Suppose that
M
and
N
are positively
\kappa
-saturated p.c. models of
T
with
|M|,|N|\leq\kappa
. Then
M
and
N
are isomorphic.

Proof.

We will prove that
M
and
N
are in
\kappa
-back-and-forth correspondence. Then the result follows from ?THM?
. Following ?THM?
, (i) from ?THM?
is automatic, so we prove (ii), and (iii) follows by symmetry.

Let
f:M\to N
be a partial immersion with
|\dom(f)|<\kappa
and let
a\in M
be a singleton. Write
C=\dom(f)
, and set
p(x)=\tp(a/C;M)=\{\varphi(x,c):c\in C\text{ and }M\models\varphi(a,c)\}
. Consider the type

f(p)(x)=\{\varphi(x,f(c)):\varphi(x,c)\in p(x)\}.

As
p(x)
is satisfiable in
M
, we have that
f(p)(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
N
because
f
is a partial immersion. By positive
\kappa
-saturatedness of
N
there is a realisation
b\in N
of
f(p)(x)
. Let
g
extend
f
by setting
g(a)=b
. We prove that
g
is a partial immersion. For this, we let
\varphi(x,y)
be any formula (
x
may not appear in
\varphi
) and let
c\in C
match the length of
y
. By construction we have that
M\models\varphi(a,c)
implies
N\models\varphi(b,f(c))
. It is the converse that requires an argument. We prove the contrapositive. Suppose that
M\not\models\varphi(a,c)
. As
M
is a p.c. model, there is an obstruction
\psi(x,y)
of
\varphi(x,y)
modulo
T
such that
M\models\psi(a,c)
. By the already established direction, we have
N\models\psi(b,f(c))
and hence
N\not\models\varphi(b,f(c))
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Example 3.3.8
.

The analogous version of the above theorem in full first-order logic is often stated as: any two
\kappa
-saturated elementary equivalent structures of cardinality
\kappa
are isomorphic. The assumption that
M
and
N
are p.c. models of the same theory with JCP is then the analogue of being elementary equivalent, but it is slightly more subtle. The argument does not go through if we let
M
and
N
be two positively
\kappa
-saturated structures of cardinality at most
\kappa
that satisfy the same h-inductive sentences. The issue is that they may not be p.c. models, and the
g
constructed in the proof of ?THM?
will generally only be a partial homomorphism, but not a partial immersion.

As a concrete example we consider the theory from ?THM?
, which has
\omega
many constant symbols
\{c_{i}\}_{i<\omega}
and declares them all to be distinct. Then the model
M
that consists only of interpretations for the constant symbols is the unique p.c. model. By the usual compactness theorem and downward Löwenheim-Skolem, there is a countable model
N
that is elementary equivalent to
M
and which contains an element
a\in N
that is not the interpretation of any of the constant symbols. One quickly checks that both
M
and
N
are positively
\omega
-saturated. However, they are clearly not isomorphic.

3.4
Atomic models

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
T
be a theory. We call
M
an
atomic model
of
T
if it is a p.c. model of
T
in which only supported types are realised.

Theorem 3.4.2
.

Let
T
be a theory with JCP. Then any two countable atomic models of
T
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Let
M
and
N
be countable atomic models of
T
. We show that
M
and
N
are in
\omega
-back-and-forth correspondence, so the isomorphism follows from ?THM?
. Following ?THM?
, (i) from ?THM?
is automatic, so we prove (ii), and (iii) follows by symmetry.

Let
f:M\to N
be a partial immersion with
|\dom(f)|
finite and let
a\in M
be a singleton. Enumerate
\dom(f)
as a finite tuple
a^{\prime}
. By assumption,
\tp(a,a^{\prime};M)
is supported. So let
\varphi(x,y)
be the support of
\tp(a,a^{\prime};M)
. Then
M\models\exists x\varphi(x,a^{\prime})
and so
N\models\exists x\varphi(x,f(a^{\prime}))
. Let
b
be such that
N\models\varphi(b,f(a^{\prime}))
. Then because
N
is a p.c. model we have by definition of being a support that
\tp(a,a^{\prime};M)\subseteq\tp(b,f(a^{\prime});N)
. By maximality of types in p.c. models (?THM?
) we have
\tp(a,a^{\prime};M)=\tp(b,f(a^{\prime});N)
, and so
f
can be extended to a partial immersion that sends
a
to
b
, as required.
∎

3.5
Prime models

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
T
be a theory. We call
M
a
prime model
if it is a p.c. model of
T
and every p.c. model of
T
is a continuation of
M
.

Proposition 3.5.2
.

If a theory
T
has a prime model then
T
has JCP.

Proof.

Let
M
be a prime model and let
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
be two p.c. models. Then there are immersions
N_{1}\leftarrow M\to N_{2}
. By ?THM?
we can amalgamate this span to obtain
N_{1}\to N\leftarrow N_{2}
, where
N
is a model of
T
.
∎

Proposition 3.5.3
.

If a theory
T
has a prime model
M
then
|M|\leq|T|
.

Proof.

By ?THM?
there is a p.c. model
N
of
T
with
|N|\leq|T|
. Since
M
is prime, there is a homomorphism
M\to N
. As
M
is a p.c. model, this is an immersion and so in particular it is an injection. Hence
|M|\leq|N|\leq|T|
.
∎

Theorem 3.5.4
.

Let
T
be a countable theory with JCP, and let
M
be a p.c. model of
T
. Then
M
is prime if and only if it is countable and atomic.

Proof.

Countability follow from ?THM?
. Next we show that
M
only realises supported types. Let
p(x)
be a type that is not supported. Then by ?THM?
there is a p.c. model
N
of
T
that omits
p(x)
. As
M
is prime there is a homomorphism
f:M\to N
. So if
p(x)
were to be realised in
M
, say by
a\in M
, then
f(a)
would realise
p(x)
in
N
. Therefore,
p(x)
cannot be realised in
M
.

For the right to left direction we let
N
be any p.c. model of
T
. Enumerate
M
as
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
. We will inductively construct an increasing chain of functions
f_{i}:\{a_{j}\}_{j<i}\to N
such that for all tuples
a
in
\{a_{j}\}_{j<i}
and all
\varphi(x)
we have

M\models\varphi(a)\quad\implies\quad N\models\varphi(f(a)).

Then
f=\bigcup_{i<\omega}f_{i}
will be the desired homomorphism
M\to N
. For the base case we take the empty function, for which we need to check that every sentence satisfied by
M
is also satisfied by
N
(see also ?THM?
). This is indeed true, because both
M
and
N
are p.c. models of the same theory with JCP. So, by ?THM?
,
N
is a model of
T^{\textup{pc}}
, which can be computed as the set of h-inductive sentences satisfied by
M
(and any positive sentence can be viewed as an h-inductive sentence).

Having constructed
f_{i}
, we let
p(x,y)=\tp(a_{i},(a_{j})_{j<i};M)
. As
M
is atomic, there is a support
\varphi(x,y)
of
p(x,y)
. By ?THM?
we have
\varphi(x,y)\in p(x,y)
and so
M\models\exists x\varphi(x,(a_{j})_{j<i})
. By the induction hypothesis we then have
N\models\exists x\varphi(x,f((a_{j})_{j<i}))
. Let
b\in N
be such that
N\models\varphi(b,f((a_{j})_{j<i}))
. As
\varphi(x,y)
supports
p(x,y)
and
N
is a p.c. model, we have
N\models p(b,f((a_{j})_{j<i}))
. We can thus set
f_{i+1}(a_{i})=b
. This completes the construction and thus the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.5.5
.

Let
T
be a countable theory. Any two prime models of
T
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Let
M
and
N
be prime models of
T
. By ?THM?
,
T
has JCP, so ?THM?
applies. Hence
M
and
N
are both countable and atomic. The result now follows from ?THM?
.
∎

3.6
Characterising countably categorical theories

Definition 3.6.1
.

Let
\kappa
be a cardinal. A theory
T
is called
\kappa
-categorical
if it has only one p.c. model of cardinality
\kappa
, up to isomorphism.

Theorem 3.6.2
.

Let
T
be a countable theory with JCP. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

T
is
\omega
-categorical,

(ii)

every type in finitely many variables is supported,

(iii)

all p.c. models are atomic,

(iv)

all countable p.c. models are atomic,

(v)

every p.c. model is positively
\omega
-saturated,

(vi)

there is a positively
\omega
-saturated prime model,

(vii)

there is a positively
\omega
-saturated atomic model.

Proof.

We prove the following implications.

(v)
(i)
(ii)
(i)-(v)
(iv)
(vi)
(iii)
(vii)

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Suppose for a contradiction that there is a type
p(x)
in finitely many variables that is not supported. Let
M
be a p.c. model in which
p(x)
is realised, say by
a\in M
. By ?THM?
there is a countable p.c. model
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
with
a\in M^{\prime}
. In particular,
p(x)
is realised in
M^{\prime}
by
a
. By ?THM?
there is also a p.c. model
N
that omits
p(x)
. Again, using ?THM?
, there is a countable p.c. model
N^{\prime}\subseteq N
. In particular,
N^{\prime}
omits
p(x)
. However, by assumption
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
must be isomorphic, which is our desired contradiction.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
By definition.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
Trivial.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(i)
By ?THM?
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(v)
By ?THM?
.

(v)
\Rightarrow
(i)
By ?THM?
.

Now that we have established the equivalence of (i)–(v), we prove the equivalence with the final two properties.

(i)–(v)
\Rightarrow
(vi)
Let
M
be the unique countable p.c. model. By (iii)
M
is atomic and, so
M
is prime by ?THM?
. Finally,
M
is positively
\omega
-saturated by (v).

(vi)
\Rightarrow
(vii)
Let
M
be prime and positively
\omega
-saturated. By ?THM?
we have that
M
is countable. Therefore,
M
is atomic by ?THM?
.

(vii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Let
M
be a positively
\omega
-saturated atomic model. Let
p(x)
be a type in finitely many variables and let
M^{\prime}
be a p.c. model in which
p(x)
is realised. Using JCP of
T
we find immersions
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
into some model
N
of
T
. Then
N
realises
p(x)
. So for any
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
we have
N\models\exists x\varphi(x)
, and hence
M\models\exists x\varphi(x)
. We thus see that
p(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
. By positive
\omega
-saturation we thus have that
p(x)
is realised in
M
, and since
M
is atomic this means that
p(x)
is supported.
∎

Remark 3.6.3
.

?THM?
provides several equivalent characterisations of being
\omega
-categorical for positive theories. However, compared to the analogous theorem for full first-order logic one important characterisation is missing: namely that every space of types in finitely many variables is finite. This is no longer an equivalent condition in positive logic. In fact, one easily sees that having finite type spaces is equivalent to being
\omega
-categorical and Boolean, where being Boolean follows because the complement of any positively definable set is positively definable using a finite disjunction.

As a counterexample we consider the theory
T
from ?THM?
with constant symbols
\{c_{i}\}_{i<\omega}
, and which asserts that
c_{i}\neq c_{j}
for all
i\neq j
. Then
T
has a unique p.c. model, namely the model that consists only of interpretations for the constant symbols. We thus see that
T
is
\omega
-categorical, but each constant yields a different type, so we have infinitely many
1
-types.

3.7
Bibliographic remarks

In
[
Hay19
]
a stronger omitting types theorem is proved. Namely that any meagre set of types (in a different type space than we defined in Section
2.2
) can be omitted. The proof is based on the Baire Category Theorem. Our proof is more elementary and closer to the usual proof of the omitting types theorem for full first-order logic (e.g.,
[
TZ12
, Theorem 4.1.2]
). Once the omitting types theorem is proved, the proof of ?THM?
(characterising countable categoricity) goes as usual, using atomic models and prime models, which are also present in
[
Hay19
]
. However,
[
Hay19
]
does not treat positive saturation, so items (v)–(vii) in ?THM?
are new compared to
[
Hay19
]
and first appeared in
[
Kam23a
, Theorem 5.8]
.

As explained before ?THM?
, the name “Haykazyan’s test” is because that proposition was first proved by Haykazyan. The exact reference for that is
[
Hay19
, Proposition 5.1]
.

The notion of a supported type appears already in
[
Hay19
, page 844]
. That is,
[
Hay19
, page 844]
gives a topological definition of what it means for a set of formulas to be supported. We use the translation from
[
Kam23a
, Definition 5.2]
.

The fact that countable categoricity in positive logic does not necessarily correspond to finite type spaces (?THM?
) can also be found in
[
Hay19
, Example 6.6]
.

The notion of positive saturation is taken from
[
PY18
, Section 2.4]
.

Chapter 4
Saturated, homogeneous and monster models

Just like in full first-order logic, it will be convenient to work in a monster model. In this chapter we make precise what that means and how such a model can be constructed. Compared to the full first-order setting there is really nothing new going on, as the usual constructions go through in positive logic. So if the reader wishes they can just skip to the description of the monster model and the accompanying conventions in Section
4.3
. The only other thing that will be used in other places is the existence of positively saturated p.c. models, which is ?THM?
.

After having set up the conventions and notation for the monster model, we discuss some standard model-theoretic tools in Section
4.4
. This mainly concerns indiscernible sequences. We then continue in Section
4.5
with the basics concerning Lascar strong types and some subtleties that arise in positive logic.

4.1
Building positively saturated models

Earlier we defined what a positively saturated p.c. model is (?THM?
). Now we show that they can be constructed as usual.

Proposition 4.1.1
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of a theory
T
. Then for all
\kappa\geq|M|+|T|
there is a positively
\kappa^{+}
-saturated p.c. model
N
of
T
with
|N|\leq 2^{\kappa}
, which is a continuation of
M
.

Proof.

We inductively construct a continuous chain
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa^{+}}
of p.c. models of cardinality at most
2^{\kappa}
with
M_{0}=M
such that: for every
i<\kappa^{+}
we have that, for any
A\subseteq M_{i}
with
|A|\leq\kappa
, any set of formulas
\Sigma(x)
over
A
in a single variable, that is finitely satisfiable in
M_{i}
, has a realisation in
M_{i+1}
. This can be done because for each such an
M_{i}
there are at most
2^{\kappa}
many sets of formulas over subsets of cardinality at most
\kappa
. The union
N=\bigcup_{i<\kappa^{+}}M_{i}
will then be the required p.c. model (it is p.c. by ?THM?
). We spell this out in detail below.

Having constructed
M_{i}
, we note that
M_{i}
has
2^{\kappa}
subsets of cardinality at most
\kappa
. As
\kappa\geq|T|
, there are
\kappa
many formulas over a set of cardinality at most
\kappa
and therefore there are
2^{\kappa}
many sets of formulas in a single variable over such a set. There are thus
2^{\kappa}\times 2^{\kappa}=2^{\kappa}
many sets of formulas in a single variable with parameters in some
A\subseteq M_{i}
with
|A|\leq\kappa
. Enumerate all such sets of formulas that are finitely satisfiable in
M_{i}
as
\{\Sigma_{i}(x_{i})\}_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}}
. Set
\Sigma((x_{i})_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}})=\bigcup_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}}\Sigma_{i}(x_{i})
, which is then finitely satisfiable in
M_{i}
. By ?THM?
we find a p.c. model
M^{\prime}
of
T
that is a continuation of
M_{i}
such that there are
(a_{i})_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}}\in M^{\prime}
with
M\models\Sigma((a_{i})_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}})
. As
M^{\prime}
is a continuation of
M_{i}
and
M_{i}
is p.c., we may view
M_{i}
as a subset of
M^{\prime}
. By downward Löwenheim-Skolem (?THM?
) we then find a p.c. model
M_{i+1}\subseteq M^{\prime}
such that
M_{i}(a_{i})_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}}\subseteq M_{i+1}
and
|M_{i+1}|\leq|M_{i}(a_{i})_{i\leq 2^{\kappa}}|+|T|\leq 2^{\kappa}
, as required.

We are left to verify that
N=\bigcup_{i<\kappa^{+}}M_{i}
is positively
\kappa^{+}
-saturated. So let
A\subseteq N
with
A<\kappa^{+}
and let
\Sigma(x)
be a set of formulas in a single variable with parameters in
A
, that is finitely satisfiable in
N
. Then there must be some
i<\kappa^{+}
such that
A\subseteq M_{i}
. As
M_{i}
is p.c., we have that
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M_{i}
. By construction then, there is a realisation of
\Sigma(x)
in
M_{i+1}
, and hence in
N
.
∎

Proposition 4.1.2
.

Let
M
be a positively
\kappa
-saturated p.c. model of a theory
T
with JCP. Then for any model
N
of
T
with
|N|\leq\kappa
there is a homomorphism
f:N\to M
.

Proof.

Let
a
be a tuple of length
|N|
that enumerates
N
, and write
\Sigma(x)=\tp(a;N)
. So
\Sigma(x)
is essentially the positive diagram of
N
, but with variables enumerating
N
instead of constant symbols. In particular, realisations of
\Sigma(x)
in
M
correspond to homomorphisms
N\to M
(see also ?THM?
). As
M
is positively
\kappa
-saturated, it is enough to show that
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
by ?THM?
.

As
T
has JCP, there is a model
M^{\prime}
of
T
with homomorphisms
N\to M^{\prime}\leftarrow M
. Since
M
is a p.c. model, the homomorphism
M\to M^{\prime}
is an immersion. Thus, for any
\varphi(x)\in\Sigma(x)
we have

N\models\exists x\varphi(x)\implies M^{\prime}\models\exists x\varphi(x)\implies M\models\exists x\varphi(x),

which establishes that
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
, as required.
∎

4.2
Homogeneity

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal. A structure
M
is called
strongly positively
\kappa
-homogeneous
if the following equivalent conditions hold:

(i)

every partial immersion
f:M\to M
with a domain of cardinality less than
\kappa
can be extended to an automorphism,

(ii)

for every two tuples
a,b\in M
of length less than
\kappa
such that
\tp(a;M)=\tp(b;M)
there is an automorphism
h:M\to M
that sends
a
to
b
, so
h(a)=b
.

We can build strongly positively homogeneous p.c. models as usual. The main takeaway is the following, and the rest of the section is devoted to proving it.

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of a theory
T
. Then for all
\kappa
there is a positively
\kappa
-saturated and strongly positively
\kappa
-homogeneous p.c. model
N
of
T
, which is a continuation of
M
.

Definition 4.2.3
.

We call a structure
M
of cardinality
\kappa=\aleph_{\alpha}

positively special
if it is the union of a chain
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
of immersions such that
M_{i}
is
\aleph_{i+1}
-saturated. We call such a chain
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
a
positively specialising chain
.

Another formulation of ?THM?
is saying that
M
is a union of a chain
(M_{\lambda})
, where
\lambda
ranges over the cardinals
<\kappa
, such that each
M_{\lambda}
is
\lambda^{+}
-saturated.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of a theory
T
, and let
\kappa
be an uncountable cardinal such that
\kappa>|M|+|T|
and
\lambda<\kappa
implies
2^{\lambda}<\kappa
. Then there is a continuation
N
of
M
which is a p.c. model of
T
and which is a positively special structure of cardinality
\kappa
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be such that
\kappa=\aleph_{\alpha}
. By induction we build a chain
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
of p.c. models of
T
such that for each
i<\alpha
we have that
M_{i}
is
\aleph_{i+1}
-saturated and
|M_{i}|<\kappa
. Furthermore, we make it so that
M_{0}
is a continuation of
M
.

Every stage
i
—whether it is the base case, successor step or limit stage—is essentially done in the same way: starting with some p.c. model
M^{\prime}
of
T
, with
|M^{\prime}|<\kappa
, we apply ?THM?
to find a positively
\aleph_{i+1}
-saturated p.c. model
M_{i}
of
T
that is a continuation of
M^{\prime}
with
|M_{i}|\leq 2^{|M^{\prime}|+|T|+\aleph_{i}}<\kappa
. Based on which case we are in, we make different choices for
M^{\prime}
:

•

in the base case we take
M^{\prime}=M
,

•

for successor steps we take
M^{\prime}=M_{i-1}
,

•

for limit stages we take
M^{\prime}=\bigcup_{j<i}M_{j}
.

As the chain
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
consists of p.c. models, all maps between the structures are immersions and so it is a positively specialising chain. We can thus take
N=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.5
.

For any structure
M
and any infinite cardinal
\mu
there exists a continuation
N
of
M
which is a positively special structure with
\cf(|N|)\geq\mu
.

Proof.

Take
\kappa=\beth_{\mu}(|M|+|T|)
. Then
\kappa
satisfies the assumptions of ?THM?
, while we also have
\cf(\kappa)\geq\mu
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.6
.

Let
T
be a theory with JCP, and suppose
M
and
N
are p.c. models of
T
of the same cardinality that are also positively special structures. Then
M
and
N
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Write
\kappa=|M|=|N|
and let
\alpha
be such that
\kappa=\aleph_{\alpha}
. Let
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
and
(N_{i})_{i<\alpha}
be positively specialising chains with unions
M
and
N
respectively.

Claim 4.2.6.1
.

There are enumerations
(a_{j})_{j<\kappa}
and
(b_{j})_{j<\kappa}
of
M
and
N
respectively, such that for each
j<\kappa
we have that
a_{j}\in M_{i}
and
b_{j}\in N_{i}
, where
i
is such that
|j|=\aleph_{i}
.

The enumerations
(a_{j})_{j<\kappa}
and
(b_{j})_{j<\kappa}
will possibly allow repetitions.

Proof of claim.

We prove the claim for
M
, and the enumeration of
N
is then completely analogous. Let
(c_{j})_{j<\kappa}
be an enumeration of
M
without repetitions. We define
(a_{j})_{j<\kappa}
by induction on
j
. Having defined
(a_{k})_{k<j}
we let
i
be such that
|j|=\aleph_{i}
. Then let
j^{\prime}
be the least such that
c_{j^{\prime}}\in M_{i}\setminus\{a_{k}:k<j\}
, or else take any
j^{\prime}
such that
c_{j^{\prime}}\in M_{0}
. We set
a_{j}=c_{j^{\prime}}
.

The constructed sequence
(a_{j})_{j<\kappa}
clearly satisfies the desired property, so we are left to verify that it actually enumerates all elements of
M
. Suppose for a contradiction that it does not. Let
j^{\prime}<\kappa
be minimal such that
c_{j^{\prime}}\not\in\{a_{j}:j<\kappa\}
and let
i
be minimal such that
c_{j^{\prime}}\in M_{i}
. We consider the sequence
(a_{j})_{\aleph_{i}\leq j<\kappa}
. This sequence can only contain elements from
\{c_{k}:k<j^{\prime}\}
. It can also never repeat any elements, as that only happens in the second case of the definition of the
a_{j}
’s. This implies that
\kappa=|\{a_{j}:\aleph_{i}\leq j<\kappa\}|\leq|\{c_{k}:k<j^{\prime}\}|=|j^{\prime}|<\kappa
, a contradiction.
∎

We now finish the proof by a back-and-forth argument. We cannot just apply ?THM?
, because we need a special induction hypothesis (see (iii) below). We inductively construct an increasing chain of partial bijections
(f_{j}:M\to N)_{i<\kappa}
such that for each
j<\kappa
:

(i)

a_{j}\in\dom(f_{j})
;

(ii)

b_{j}\in\cod(f_{j})
;

(iii)

\dom(f_{j})\subseteq M_{i}
and
\cod(f_{j})\subseteq N_{i}
, where
i
is such that
|j|=\aleph_{i}
;

(iv)

f_{j}
is a partial immersion.

We first construct
f_{0}
. Write
p(x)=\tp(a_{0};M)
. We claim that
p(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
N_{0}
. Let
\varphi(x)\in p(x)
, then
M\models\exists x\varphi(x)
. By ?THM?
(vi),
T
having JCP means that
M
and
N
satisfy the same positive sentences, so we also have
N\models\exists x\varphi(x)
. Since
N_{0}\subseteq N
is an immersion we have that
N_{0}\models\exists x\varphi(x)
, as required. As
N_{0}
is
\aleph_{1}
-saturated, there is a realisation
b
of
p(x)
in
N_{0}
. Maximality of types in p.c. models (?THM?
) guarantees that
\tp(b;N)=\tp(b;N_{0})=p
. Set
f_{0}(a_{0})=b
. To make sure that
b_{0}\in\cod(f_{0})
we proceed as in the inductive step below.

For the inductive step we assume that
(f_{k})_{k<j}
is constructed. Write
A=\bigcup_{k<j}\dom(f_{k})
and
B=\bigcup_{k<j}\cod(f_{k})
. We define
f_{j}
on
A
by extending the
f_{k}
’s constructed so far, so for now
f_{j}
is a bijective partial immersion (by (iv))
M\to N
with domain
A
and codomain
B
. Let
p(x)=\tp(a_{j}/A;M)=\{\varphi(x,a):a\in A\text{ and }M\models\varphi(a_{j},a)\}
and set
p^{\prime}(x)=f_{j}(p(x))=\{\varphi(x,f_{j}(a)):\varphi(x,a)\in p(x)\}
. Let
i
be such that
|j|=\aleph_{i}
, and note that by (iii) we have that
A\subseteq M_{i}
and
B\subseteq N_{i}
. We claim that
p^{\prime}(x)
is finitely realisable in
N_{i}
. Let
\varphi(x,a)\in p(x)
, then
M\models\exists x\varphi(x,a)
and so
N\models\exists x\varphi(x,f_{j}(a))
. Since
N_{i}\subseteq N
is an immersion we have that
N_{i}\models\exists x\varphi(x,f_{j}(a))
, as required. As
|B|\leq|j|=\aleph_{i}
and
N_{i}
is positively
\aleph_{i+1}
-saturated, we have that
p^{\prime}(x)
has a realisation
b
in
N_{i}
. By maximality of types in p.c. models (?THM?
) we have
\tp(b/B;N)=\tp(b/B;N_{i})=p^{\prime}(x)
. Set
f_{j}(a_{j})=b
. Now let
q(x)=\tp(b_{j}/Bb;N)
. Then by a similar argument we find a realisation
a
of
{f_{j}}^{-1}(q(x))
in
M_{i}
, and we set
f_{j}(a)=b_{j}
. This completes the inductive construction.

Set
f=\bigcup_{j<\kappa}f_{j}
. Then
\dom(f)=M
by (i),
\cod(f)=N
by (ii) and it is an isomorphism by (iv). We also note that the possible repetitions in the enumerations of
M
and
N
are no problem, as the equality symbol is always part of the language.
∎

Proof of ?THM?
.

By ?THM?
there is a continuation
N
of
M
which is a p.c. model of
T
and which is a positively special structure with
\cf(|N|)\geq\kappa
. We claim that
N
is the required p.c. model. For this we fix a positively specialising chain
(N_{i})_{i<\alpha}
for
N
, where
\alpha
is such that
|N|=\aleph_{\alpha}
.

We first prove that
N
is positively
\kappa
-saturated. Let
\Sigma(x)
be a set of formulas over
A\subseteq N
, with
|A|<\kappa
, that is finitely satisfiable in
N
. As
\kappa\leq\cf(|N|)\leq\alpha
, there is
i<\alpha
such that
A\subseteq N_{i}
. As
|A|<\kappa\leq\aleph_{\alpha}
, we may assume that
i
is such that
\aleph_{i+1}\geq|A|
. Then
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
N_{i}
because
N_{i}\subseteq N
is an immersion and as
N_{i}
is positively
\aleph_{i+1}
-saturated it contains a realisation
a
of
\Sigma(x)
. Then
a
is also a realisation of
\Sigma(x)
in
N
, which proves that
N
is positively
\kappa
-saturated.

Now we prove that
N
is strongly
\kappa
-homogeneous. Let
f:N\to N
be a partial immersion with domain
A\subseteq N
, where
|A|<\kappa
. As before, there is
i<\alpha
such that
A\subseteq N_{i}
and such that
\aleph_{i+1}\geq|A|
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be the signature of
T
, and extend it to
\mathcal{L}_{A}
by adding a constant symbol for each element of
A
. For each
j\geq i
we let
(N_{j},A)
be the
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-structure where each
a\in A
is interpreted as itself and we let
(N_{j},f(A))
be the
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-structure where
a\in A
is interpreted as
f(a)
. By our assumption on
i
, each of
(N_{j},A)
and
(N_{j},f(A))
is positively
\aleph_{j+1}
-saturated. So we can form two positively specialising chains
(N^{\prime}_{j})_{j<\alpha}
and
(N^{\prime\prime}_{j})_{j<\alpha}
by setting

N^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}(N_{j},A)&\text{if }i\leq j\\
(N_{i},A)&\text{else}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad N^{\prime\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}(N_{j},f(A))&\text{if }i\leq j\\
(N_{i},f(A))&\text{else}\end{cases}

This makes
(N,A)
and
(N,f(A))
into positively special structures. They are also p.c. models of the same theory with JCP, namely the set of h-inductive sentences in
\mathcal{L}_{A}
that are true in
(N,A)
, which is the same as when taking those true in
(N,f(A))
. By ?THM?
we then have that
(N,A)
and
(N,f(A))
are isomorphic. That is, there is an automorphism of
N
that extends
f
.
∎

4.3
Monster models

It is common in model theory to work in a so-called monster model, which is a very saturated and very homogeneous model. This is mainly a notational convenience, as it allows us to view types as automorphism orbits and to find realisations of sets of formulas we no longer need to move to a bigger model.

To give a precise definition of a monster model, we first need to fix some notion of smallness. That is, we declare when a cardinal is considered “small”, and then we want our monster model to be saturated and homogeneous with respect to all “small” sets. There are various ways to make this precise, of which we name a few.

(1)

Assume inaccessible cardinals exist. Fix some inaccessible cardinal
\kappa
and let “small” mean
<\kappa
.

(2)

Work in a set theory that allows for classes and class-sized models (e.g., von Neumann-Bernays-Gödel set theory), and let “small” mean “not a proper class”.

(3)

At the start of every proof we fix a cardinal
\kappa
so that everything that we need in that proof is of cardinality
<\kappa
, and we let “small” mean
<\kappa
.

Each of these approaches has their own advantages and disadvantages.

(1)

This approach requires us to assume the existence of large cardinals, and so we are no longer within ZFC set theory.

(2)

Bernays-Gödel set theory is conservative over ZFC, meaning that everything that is proved in Bernays-Gödel set theory is provable in ZFC. So compared to approach (1) we do not need to assume extra strength of our set theory. However, sometimes we will want to move to a bigger monster model, for which our original monster model is considered “small” (e.g., when considering global types, see ?THM?
). This would be problematic, as there is nothing ‘bigger’ than classes in Bernays-Gödel set theory.

(3)

This approach stays within ZFC and we can clearly move to bigger and bigger monster models. It only requires us to trust that at the start of every proof we could indeed guarantee that there is a big enough cardinal
\kappa
that is bigger than anything we wish to consider.

In practice it turns out that approach (3) is indeed viable, so that is the author’s preferred approach. However, we stress once more that the monster model is purely a notational convenience, and so it does not matter which notion of smallness one prefers. We thus invite the reader to pick their favourite notion of smallness in the following definition.

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
T
be a theory with JCP and fix a notion of “small” as explained above. A
monster model
of
T
is a model
\mathfrak{M}
of
T
that is:

•

Positively closed
:
\mathfrak{M}
is a p.c. model of
T
.

•

Very homogeneous
: any partial immersion
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
with small domain extends to an automorphism on all of
\mathfrak{M}
. Equivalently for any two small tuples
a
and
b
in
\mathfrak{M}
we have
\tp(a;\mathfrak{M})=\tp(b;\mathfrak{M})
if and only if there is an automorphism
f
of
\mathfrak{M}
such that
f(a)=b
.

•

Very saturated
: any small set of formulas with parameters in
\mathfrak{M}
that is finitely satisfiable in
\mathfrak{M}
is satisfiable in
\mathfrak{M}
.

Whatever notion of smallness we take, ?THM?
shows that monster models exist for every theory with JCP. The point of assuming JCP is that then by ?THM?
every small model admits a homomorphism into the monster model. In particular, every small p.c. model admits an immersion into the monster model and may thus be viewed as a submodel.

Remark 4.3.2
.

If
T
has a maximal p.c. model
M
then this is the monster model
\mathfrak{M}=M
. The preferred formalism for monster models then does not matter. In this case, the monster model is sometimes said to be bounded.

To see this, we first claim that any homomorphism
f:M\to M
is an automorphism. As
M
is a p.c. model it is an immersion, so we only need to show that
f
is surjective. Suppose for a contradiction that it is not. We inductively build a chain
(M_{i})_{i<|M|^{+}}
such that
M_{i}=M
for all
i<|M|^{+}
. For successors we let the link
M_{i}\to M_{i+1}
be given by
f
. At limit stages
\ell<|M|^{+}
we let
M^{\prime}
be the union of
(M_{i})_{i<\ell}
, which is a p.c. model by ?THM?
, so there is an immersion
M^{\prime}\to M
. For each
i<\ell
the link
M_{i}\to M_{\ell}
is then the composition
M_{i}\to M^{\prime}\to M
. Let
N
be the union of
(M_{i})_{i<|M|^{+}}
. Since
f
is not surjective we can pick some
a_{i}\in M_{i+1}\setminus f(M_{i})
for every
i<|M|^{+}
. We thus obtain a set
\{a_{i}:i<|M|^{+}\}
of cardinality
|M|^{+}
in
N
, but at the same time
N
is a p.c. model (again, by ?THM?
), so it must admit an immersion
N\to M
and hence
|N|<|M|
. We arrive at a contradiction and conclude that
f
must be surjective.

We now show that any positively
(\aleph_{0}+|M|)
-saturated p.c. model
N
is (isomorphic to)
M
. Indeed, there is an immersion
g:N\to M
. By positive saturation (or more precisely, ?THM?
),
N
also realises
\Diag(M)
, and so there is an immersion
f:M\to N
. By the above claim
gf
is an automorphism, so
g
is surjective and hence an isomorphism.

The claim that
M
is the monster model then follows from the fact that positively
(\aleph_{0}+|M|)
-saturated and strongly positively
(\aleph_{0}+|M|)
-homogeneous p.c. models always exist (?THM?
).

Convention 4.3.3
.

From now on we work in a monster model
\mathfrak{M}
, so all p.c. models, tuples and sets are assumed to be small and to live in
\mathfrak{M}
.

We finish this section by establishing some (standard) notation for working in the monster model.

Convention 4.3.4
.

We generally omit the monster model
\mathfrak{M}
from the notation. So for example, we would write
\tp(a)
and
\models\varphi(a)
instead of
\tp(a;\mathfrak{M})
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi(a)
. We also fix the following notation. Everything is small unless explicitly mentioned otherwise.

•

We use lowercase Latin letters
a,b,c,\ldots
for (possibly infinite) tuples of elements in
\mathfrak{M}
.

•

We use uppercase Latin letters
A,B,C,\ldots
for arbitrary subsets of the monster. We use the letters
M
and
N
when these subsets are p.c. models.

•

For a tuple
a
and a set
B
we write

\tp(a/B)=\{\varphi(x,b):b\in B\text{ and }\models\varphi(a,b)\}

for the set of formulas over
B
that are satisfied by
a
, and we call this the
type of
a
over
B
.

•

We write
a\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}
to mean
\tp(a/B)=\tp(a^{\prime}/B)
.

•

We write
\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/B)
for the set of autmorphisms of
\mathfrak{M}
that fix
B
pointwise. So by homogeneity we have
a\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}
if and only if there is
f\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/B)
with
f(a)=a^{\prime}
.

•

For a set of formulas
\Sigma(x)
and a tuple
a
we write
a\models\Sigma
to mean that
a
satisfies
\Sigma(x)
in
\mathfrak{M}
, that is
\models\Sigma(a)
.

•

For a set of formulas
\Sigma
with parameters in
C
and some
B\subseteq C
we write
\Sigma|_{B}
for the subset of
\Sigma
consisting of those formulas with parameters from
B
.

Similar to ?THM?
we can define type spaces, but now over fixed parameter sets.

Definition 4.3.5
.

Let
B
be a set of parameters and let
I
be an index set. Then the
type space of
I
-types over
B
, written as
\operatorname{S}_{I}(B)
, is defined as follows:

\operatorname{S}_{I}(B)=\{\tp(a/B):a\text{ is indexed by }I\}.

Note that
\operatorname{S}_{I}(B)
could be topologised similarly to how we topologised
\operatorname{S}_{I}(T)
, by having closed sets correspond to sets of formulas (with parameters in
B
). Though we will have no use for this.

4.4
The toolbox

In this section we collect some tools that are essential for advanced model theory. These are tools that we know and love from the full first-order setting, and we see that in positive logic we do not have to give up any strength.

Proposition 4.4.1
.

Type-definable sets are closed under finite disjunction, infinite conjunction and existential quantification over any string of variables. More precisely, we can perform the following constructions on sets of formulas, where the tuples of variables involved can be infinite.

(i)

Given sets of formulas
\Sigma_{1}(x)
and
\Sigma_{2}(x)
with parameters, we define

\Sigma(x)=\{\varphi_{1}(x)\vee\varphi_{2}(x):\varphi_{1}(x)\in\Sigma_{1}(x)\text{ and }\varphi_{2}(x)\in\Sigma_{2}(x)\}.

Then for all
a
we have:

\models\Sigma_{1}(a)\text{ or }\models\Sigma_{2}(a)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\Sigma(a).

(ii)

Given a (potentially infinite) family of sets of formulas
\{\Sigma_{i}(x)\}_{i\in I}
with parameters, we define

\Sigma(x)=\bigcup_{i\in I}\Sigma_{i}(x).

Then for all
a
we have:

\models\Sigma_{i}(a)\text{ for all }i\in I\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\Sigma(a).

(iii)

Given a set of formulas
\Sigma_{0}(x,y)
with parameters, we define

\Sigma(x)=\{\exists y\varphi(x,y):\varphi(x,y)\in\Sigma_{0}(x,y)\},

where the existential quantification is each time really only over the variables that are mentioned in
\varphi(x,y)
. Then for all
a
we have:

\text{there is }b\text{ with }\models\Sigma_{0}(a,b)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\Sigma(a).

In light of the above proposition it makes sense to treat sets of formulas as infinitary formulas and apply positive connectives to them.

Convention 4.4.2
.

We will often apply disjunctions, conjunctions and existential quantification to sets of formulas to form a new set of formulas. The conjunctions and existential quantification are allowed to be infinite, but the disjunctions are not. The new set of formulas is then given by the corresponding item from ?THM?
.

Singleton sets will be abbreviated by the formula that they contain. So we write
\varphi(x)\vee\Sigma(x)
instead of
\{\varphi(x)\}\vee\Sigma(x)
.

Proof.

Item (ii) is immediate from the definitions. We prove the other two.

(i)
For the left to right direction we may assume, without loss of generality, that
\models\Sigma_{1}(a)
. So for any
\varphi_{1}(x)\in\Sigma_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{2}(x)\in\Sigma_{2}(x)
we have
\models\varphi_{1}(a)\vee\varphi_{2}(a)
, and so
\models\Sigma(a)
. For the converse we prove the contrapositive. So assume that
\not\models\Sigma_{1}(a)\vee\Sigma_{2}(a)
. Then there are
\varphi_{1}(x)\in\Sigma_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{2}(x)\in\Sigma_{2}(x)
such that
\not\models\varphi_{1}(a)
and
\not\models\varphi_{2}(a)
, hence
\not\models\varphi_{1}(a)\vee\varphi_{2}(a)
. We conclude that
\not\models\Sigma(a)
, as required.

(iii)
The left to right direction is immediate from the definitions. For the converse, we let
a
be such that
\models\Sigma(a)
. Let
\varphi(x,y)\in\Sigma_{0}(x,y)
then by assumption
\models\exists y\varphi(a,y)
, and so there is
b
such that
\models\varphi(a,b)
. By compactness there must thus be
b
such that
\models\Sigma_{0}(a,b)
, that is
\models\exists y\Sigma_{0}(a,y)
.
∎

We already gave a definition of an indiscernible sequence in ?THM?
. Now that we are working in a monster model, we can define what it means to be indiscernible over some parameter set.

Definition 4.4.3
.

Let
B
be a set of parameters. An
indiscernible sequence over
B
is an infinite sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
such that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}
in
I
we have

a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}\equiv_{B}a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{n}}.

We will also abbreviate this as a
B
-indiscernible sequence
.

Indiscernible sequences are often constructed by first constructing a very long sequence and then using the lemma below to find some indiscernible sequence that is based on the very long sequence in the following sense.

Definition 4.4.4
.

Let
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(b_{j})_{j\in J}
be two infinite sequences and let
C
be some parameter set. We say that
(b_{j})_{j\in J}
is
based on

(a_{i})_{i\in I}
over
C
if for any
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}
in
J
there are
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
in
I
such that
b_{j_{1}}\ldots b_{j_{n}}\equiv_{C}a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}
.

Definition 4.4.5
.

Write
\lambda_{\kappa}=\beth_{(2^{\kappa})^{+}}
for any cardinal
\kappa
and
\lambda_{T}=\lambda_{|T|}
.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

Let
B
be any parameter set and let
\kappa
be any cardinal. Then for any sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\kappa
-tuples with
|I|\geq\lambda_{|T|+|B|+\kappa}
there is a
B
-indiscernible sequence
(a_{i}^{\prime})_{i<\omega}
that is based on
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
over
B
.

The proof of ?THM?
uses the Erdős-Rado theorem, which we will state here after first recalling the necessary notation. For cardinals
\kappa,\lambda,\mu
and
n<\omega
we write
\kappa\to(\lambda)^{n}_{\mu}
if for every function
f:[\kappa]^{n}\to\mu
we can find a subset
X\subseteq\kappa
with
|X|=\lambda
such that
f
is constant on
[X]^{n}
. Here
[\kappa]^{n}
and
[X]^{n}
are the sets of subsets of size
n
of
\kappa
and
X
respectively.

Theorem 4.4.7

(Erdős-Rado)
.

For all infinite cardinals
\mu
we have

\beth_{n}^{+}(\mu)\to(\mu^{+})^{n+1}_{\mu}.

Proof of ?THM?
.

For convenience, write
\lambda=\lambda_{|T|+|B|+\kappa}=\beth_{(2^{|T|+|B|+\kappa})^{+}}
and
\tau=|\operatorname{S}_{\kappa}(B)|
. Then
\lambda
has the following properties, where the second one follows from the Erdős-Rado theorem (?THM?
):

(i)

the cofinality of
\lambda
is strictly greater than
\tau
and
\lambda
is a limit cardinal;

(ii)

for all
\mu<\lambda
and
n<\omega
, there is some
\mu^{\prime}<\lambda
such that
\mu^{\prime}\to(\mu)^{n}_{\tau}
.

We will inductively construct a sequence of types
p_{0},p_{1},\ldots
over
B
, such that for all
n<\omega
:

(1)

p_{n}
has free variables
x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}
, each of which has length
\kappa
;

(2)

for any
m<n
and any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}<n
we have that
p_{n}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})\models p_{m}(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{m}})
;

(3)

for every
\mu<\lambda
there is
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
|I^{\prime}|=\mu
such that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
in
I^{\prime}
we have
\models p_{n}(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}})
.

Property (2) can also be phrased semantically as follows: for any
m<n
and any
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1}
realising
p_{n}
we have that any subsequence of
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1}
of length
m
realises
p_{m}
.

For
n=0
there is nothing to do. So we assume that
p_{n}
has been chosen and we will find
p_{n+1}
. Let
\mu<\lambda
be arbitrary. Then by (ii) there is
\mu^{\prime}<\lambda
such that
\mu^{\prime}\to(\mu)^{n+1}_{\tau}
. By (3) we then find
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
|I^{\prime}|=\mu^{\prime}
such that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
in
I^{\prime}
we have
\models p_{n}(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}})
. We define
f:[I^{\prime}]^{n+1}\to\operatorname{S}_{(n+1)\times\kappa}(B)
by

f(\{i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n+1}\})=\tp(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n+1}}/B).

We thus find a subset
I_{\mu}\subseteq I^{\prime}
with
|I_{\mu}|=\mu
such that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n+1}
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n+1}
in
I_{\mu}
we have
\tp(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n+1}}/B)=\tp(a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{n+1}}/B)
. Set
q_{\mu}=\tp(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n+1}}/B)
, where
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n+1}
is some subsequence in
I_{\mu}
of length
n+1
(by the above
q_{\mu}
does not depend on the choice of the subsequence). As
I_{\mu}\subseteq I^{\prime}
we have that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}<n+1
:

q_{\mu}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\models p_{n}(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}}).

(
*
)

Since
\mu<\lambda
was arbitrary, we have such an
I_{\mu}
and associated
q_{\mu}
for every cardinal
\mu<\lambda
. By (i) there must be a cofinal subset
J\subseteq\lambda
of cardinals such that for any
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in J
we have
q_{\mu}=q_{\mu^{\prime}}
. Set
p_{n+1}=q_{\mu}
. The induction hypothesis is quickly verified: (1) holds by construction, (2) follows from (
*
) together with the induction hypothesis for
p_{n}
and for (3) we note that for any
\mu<\lambda
there is
\mu^{\prime}\in J
with
\mu<\mu^{\prime}
so a (
\mu
-sized subset of)
I_{\mu^{\prime}}
will then be the required
I^{\prime}
.

This finishes the construction of the sequence
(p_{n})_{n<\omega}
. In particular, from (2) it follows that this is an increasing sequence. So
\bigcup_{n<\omega}p_{n}
has a realisation
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. Then
B
-indiscernibility follows from (2), while being based on
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
over
B
follows from (3).
∎

Lemma 4.4.8
.

Let
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
be a
B
-indiscernible sequence and let
C
be any parameter set. Then there is
C^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime}\equiv_{B}C
such that
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
is
BC^{\prime}
-indiscernible.

Proof.

By compactness we may assume
|I|
is large enough to apply ?THM?
. We then base a
BC
-indiscernible sequence
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
on
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
, where we applied compactness again to assume that
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
is indexed by
I
. As
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
was already
B
-indiscernible we have
(a_{i})_{i\in I}\equiv_{B}(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
. So we find our required
C^{\prime}
by taking one such that
C^{\prime}(a_{i})_{i\in I}\equiv_{B}C(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
.
∎

4.5
Lascar strong types

We discuss Lascar strong types, and the subtleties involving them compared to full first-order logic. These types will be relevant for the independence theorem in simple theories (?THM?
) and stationarity in stable theories (?THM?
).

Definition 4.5.1
.

Let
E(x,y)
be an equivalence relation (on the monster model), and let
B
be a parameter set. We call it:

•

bounded
if there is a bounded number of equivalence classes (i.e., small with respect to the monster);

•

B
-invariant
if for every
a_{1},a_{2},a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2}
with
a_{1}a_{2}\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}_{1}a^{\prime}_{2}
we have
E(a_{1},a_{2})
if and only if
E(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})
.

We briefly note that the definition of bounded equivalence relation above is not very precise, as we never made precise what small means in Section
4.3
. So this should really be read as follows. There is a cardinal
\kappa
such that in every p.c. model (containing the parameter set
B
) there are at most
\kappa
many equivalence classes of
E
.

Definition 4.5.2
.

Let
a
and
a^{\prime}
be two tuples of the same length and let
B
be any parameter set. We say that
a
and
a^{\prime}
have
Lascar distance

at most
n
(over
B
)
, and write
\operatorname{d}_{B}(a,a^{\prime})\leq n
, if there are
a=a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}=a^{\prime}
such that
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
are on a
B
-indiscernible sequence for all
0\leq i<n
.

We say that
a
and
a^{\prime}
have the same
Lascar strong type

(over
B
)
, and write
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{B}a^{\prime}
, if the following equivalent conditions hold:

(i)

\operatorname{d}_{B}(a,a^{\prime})\leq n
for some
n<\omega
;

(ii)

for each bounded
B
-invariant equivalence relation
E(x,y)
we have
E(a,b)
.

If
B=\emptyset
we omit it as a subscript from the notation.

Remark 4.5.3
.

We note that the condition that
a
and
a^{\prime}
are on some
B
-indiscernible sequence is equivalent to
a
and
a^{\prime}
starting a
B
-indiscernible sequence. This follows from compactness. We spell the argument out below.

Let
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
be a
B
-indiscernible sequence, such that there are
j,j^{\prime}\in I
with
a=a_{j}
and
a^{\prime}=a_{j^{\prime}}
. For each
n<\omega
we let
\Sigma_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be the type
\tp(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}/B)
, where
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}\in I
. Note that the choice of the
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}
does not matter, due to
B
-indiscernibility. Define the following type:

\Sigma((x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}})=\bigcup\{\Sigma_{n}(x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{n}}):k_{1}<\ldots<k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

By construction and
B
-indiscernibility this type is finitely satisfiable, namely by finite subsequences of
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
. Let
(a^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a realisation of
\Sigma((x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}})
, then this is a
B
-indiscernible sequence. To conclude, we distinguish two cases.

•

If
j<j^{\prime}
then
aa^{\prime}=a_{j}a_{j^{\prime}}\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}_{0}a^{\prime}_{1}
and so we can let
(a^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k<\omega}
be such that
aa^{\prime}(a^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}_{0}a^{\prime}_{1}(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. Then
a^{\prime\prime}_{0}=a
and
a^{\prime\prime}_{1}=a^{\prime}
and so
(a^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
is a
B
-indiscernible sequence starting with
a,a^{\prime}
.

•

If
j^{\prime}<j
then
aa^{\prime}=a_{j}a_{j^{\prime}}\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}_{0}a^{\prime}_{-1}
and so we can let
(a^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k<\omega}
be such that
aa^{\prime}(a^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}_{0}a^{\prime}_{-1}(a^{\prime}_{-i})_{i<\omega}
. Then
a^{\prime\prime}_{0}=a
and
a^{\prime\prime}_{1}=a^{\prime}
and so
(a^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
is a
B
-indiscernible sequence starting with
a,a^{\prime}
(because a sequence remains indiscernible after inverting its order).

Lemma 4.5.4
.

The conditions in ?THM?
are indeed equivalent.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
It suffices to prove that for any
B
-indiscernible sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
we have that
E(a_{0},a_{1})
for any
B
-invariant bounded equivalence relation. Let
\kappa
be the number of equivalence classes of
E
. Using compactness we elongate the sequence to
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa^{+}}
. Then there must be
i<j<\kappa^{+}
such that
E(a_{i},a_{j})
. Hence, by
B
-indiscernibility and
B
-invariance we get
E(a_{0},a_{1})
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Clearly, the relation described in (i) is
B
-invariant, so we need to show that it is bounded. Suppose not, then for
\lambda=\lambda_{|T|+|Ba|}
there are
(a_{i})_{i<\lambda}
such that
a_{i}
and
a_{j}
cannot be connected by
B
-indiscernible sequences as in (i), for all
i<j<\lambda
. Base a
B
-indiscernible sequence
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
on
(a_{i})_{i<\lambda}
. Let
i<j<\lambda
be such that
a^{\prime}_{0}a^{\prime}_{1}\equiv_{B}a_{i}a_{j}
. After applying an automorphism over
B
we find a
B
-indiscernible sequence with
a_{i}
and
a_{j}
on it, a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 4.5.5
.

Assume thickness. Let
M
be a positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model. Then
a^{\prime}\equiv_{M}a
implies
\operatorname{d}_{M}(a,a^{\prime})\leq 2
. If we assume semi-Hausdorffness, we can drop the assumption of positive
\lambda_{T}
-saturatedness.

Proof.

Let
a\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}
with
M
a
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model. Using thickness, we let
\Sigma(x_{0},x_{1})
be the partial type expressing that there are
(x_{i})_{2\leq i<\omega}
such that
(x_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
M
-indiscernible. We show that
\Sigma(x_{0},a)\cup\Sigma(x_{0},a^{\prime})
is finitely satisfiable, which is enough. Let
\varphi(x_{0},x_{1})\in\Sigma(x_{0},x_{1})
and let
m
denote the finite part of
M
that appears in
\varphi(x_{0},x_{1})
. As
M
is positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated, we can inductively find
(a_{i})_{i<\lambda_{T}}
in
M
such that
a_{i}(a_{j})_{j<i}\equiv_{m}a(a_{j})_{j<i}
. Base an
m
-indiscernible sequence
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
on
(a_{i})_{i<\lambda_{T}}
. Then
\models\varphi(a^{\prime}_{0},a^{\prime}_{1})
. There are
i<j<\lambda_{T}
with
a_{i}a_{j}\equiv_{m}a^{\prime}_{0}a^{\prime}_{1}
, hence
\models\varphi(a_{i},a_{j})
. By construction then
\models\varphi(a_{i},a)
. As
a^{\prime}\equiv_{M}a
and
a_{i}\in M
we also have
\models\varphi(a_{i},a^{\prime})
. So we have a realisation of
\varphi(x_{0},a)\wedge\varphi(x_{0},a^{\prime})
, and we conclude that
\Sigma(x_{0},a)\cup\Sigma(x_{0},a^{\prime})
is finitely realisable, as required.

For the claim about semi-Hausdorff theories we refer to ?THM?
.
∎

Corollary 4.5.6
.

Assume thickness. Then we have that
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{B}a^{\prime}
if and only if there are positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
and
a=a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}=a^{\prime}
such that
a_{i}\equiv_{M_{i+1}}a_{i+1}
for all
0\leq i<n
.

If we assume semi-Hausdorffness, we can drop the requirement that the p.c. models are positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated.

Proof.

The right to left direction follows immediately from ?THM?
. For the other direction, we let
a=a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}=a^{\prime}
be such that
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
are on a
B
-indiscernible sequence for all
0\leq i<n
. Let
M
be a positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model containing
B
(?THM?
). Then by ?THM?
there is
M_{i+1}
for each
0\leq i<n
with
M_{i+1}\equiv_{B}M
such that the
B
-indiscernible sequence connecting
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
is
M_{i+1}
-indiscernible, hence
a_{i}\equiv_{M_{i+1}}a_{i+1}
.
∎

Definition 4.5.7
.

Assume thickness. Let
A
be a parameter set. We define
\Aut_{f}(\mathfrak{M}/A)
, the
group of Lascar strong automorphisms over
A
as the subgroup of
\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/A)
generated by

\bigcup\{\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/M):M\text{ is a positively $\lambda_{T}$-saturated p.c.\ model containing }A\}.

Corollary 4.5.8
.

Assume thickness. Then we have that
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{B}a^{\prime}
if and only if
f(a)=b
for some
f\in\Aut_{f}(\mathfrak{M}/B)
.

Proposition 4.5.9
.

The following are equivalent for a theory
T
:

(i)

T
is thick,

(ii)

the property
\operatorname{d}(x,y)\leq n
is type-definable for all
n\geq 1
,

(iii)

the property
\operatorname{d}(x,y)\leq n
is type-definable for some
n\geq 1
,

(iv)

the property
\operatorname{d}_{B}(x,y)\leq n
is type-definable (over
B
) for all sets of parameters
B
and all
n\geq 1
,

(v)

the property
\operatorname{d}_{B}(x,y)\leq n
is type-definable (over
B
) for all sets of parameters
B
some
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

The equivalences (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv) and (iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(v) are immediate, because
\operatorname{d}_{B}(x,y)\leq n
is the same as
\operatorname{d}(xb,yb)\leq n
, where
b
is a tuple that enumerates
B
. The implication (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) is trivial. We prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Let
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
be the partial type that expresses that
(x_{i})_{i<\omega}
is an indiscernible sequence. Then
\operatorname{d}(x_{0},x_{1})\leq 1
is expressed by

\exists(x_{i})_{2\leq i<\omega}\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega}),

which can be expressed by a partial type. Then
\operatorname{d}(x,y)\leq n
