is expressed by

\exists z_{0}\ldots z_{n}\left(x=z_{0}\wedge y=z_{n}\wedge\bigwedge_{i<n}\operatorname{d}(z_{i},z_{i+1})\leq 1\right),

which can again be expressed by a partial type.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
By assumption we can define a partial type
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
as follows:

\bigcup\{\operatorname{d}(x_{i_{1}}\ldots x_{i_{k}},x_{j_{1}}\ldots x_{j_{k}})\leq n:i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k}<\omega\}.

We claim that
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
expresses that
(x_{i})_{i<\omega}
is an indiscernible sequence.

Let
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
be such that
\models\Theta((a_{i})_{i<\omega})
, and let
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<\omega
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k}<\omega
. Define
h_{1}=\max(i_{k},j_{k})+1
and
h_{i}=h_{1}+i
for
1<i\leq k
. The point is that then
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<h_{1}<\ldots<h_{k}
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k}<h_{1}<\ldots<h_{k}
. So by definition of
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
we have

\operatorname{d}(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{k}},a_{h_{1}}\ldots a_{h_{k}})\leq n,

which implies
a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{k}}\equiv a_{h_{1}}\ldots a_{h_{k}}
. Similarly, we find
a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{k}}\equiv a_{h_{1}}\ldots a_{h_{k}}
. We thus have
a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{k}}\equiv a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{k}}
, and we conclude that
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is indiscernible.

Conversely, suppose that
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is indiscernible. Let
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<j_{1}<\ldots<j_{k}<\omega
. Define a sequence
(b_{m})_{m<\omega}
by
b_{0}=(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{k}})
,
b_{1}=(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{k}})
and for
m\geq 2
we set
b_{m}=(a_{j_{k}+mk},\ldots,a_{j_{k}+mk+k-1})
. Then
(b_{m})_{m<\omega}
is sequence of
k
-tuples in
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
that respect the original order. In particular,
(b_{m})_{m<\omega}
is indiscernible. So we have
\operatorname{d}(b_{0},b_{1})\leq 1
, and in particular
\operatorname{d}(b_{0},b_{1})\leq n
, which is just saying that

\operatorname{d}(a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{k}},a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{k}})\leq n.

We thus conclude
\models\Theta((a_{i})_{i<\omega})
, as required.
∎

4.6
Bibliographic remarks

The construction of positively saturated homogeneous models in ?THM?
is standard, we follow
[
TZ12
, Section 6.1]
.

For Bernays-Gödel set theory, as mentioned at in Section
4.3
, see for example
[
Jec03
, page 70]
. A reference for the Erdős-Rado theorem (?THM?
) can be found in the same book
[
Jec03
, Theorem 9.6]
.

Basing indiscernible sequences on very long sequences, such as in ?THM?
, is considered standard. A proof of this lemma in positive logic appears for example in
[
BY03b
, Lemma 1.2]
, but the proof is really not different from the full first-order setting (e.g.,
[
TZ12
, Lemma 7.2.12]
).

The definition of Lascar strong types (?THM?
) in positive logic is taken from
[
BY03b
, Definition 1.39]
. The additional equivalent characterisation for thick theories in ?THM?
is due to
[
DK22
]
.

Chapter 5
Simple theories

In this chapter we develop dividing independence for simple theories. Much of this is similar to the treatment in full first-order logic. The main extra difficulty is proving what we call “full existence” for dividing independence, see Section
5.4
. We finish this chapter with a version of the Kim-Pillay theorem for positive logic (?THM?
), which summarises the results concerning dividing independence in simple theories.

Those familiar with the usual treatment in full first-order logic may wonder why there is no mention of forking. This is because the definition of forking does not generalise so well to positive logic, and there is no actual need to work with forking instead of dividing in simple theories (see also ?THM?
).

5.1
Dividing

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\Sigma(x,b)
be a set of formulas over
Cb
. We say that
\Sigma(x,b)

divides over
C
if there is a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{i}\equiv_{C}b
for all
i<\omega
such that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}\Sigma(x,b_{i})
is inconsistent.

If
\Sigma(x,b)
contains just one formula
\varphi(x,b)
then we will also say that
\varphi(x,b)
divides over
C
.

Note that in the above definition, the condition
b_{i}\equiv_{C}b
for all
i<\omega
on the sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
can be replaced by
b_{0}=b
, and we obtain an equivalent definition. We will often use this implicitly.

Definition 5.1.2
.

Let
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be a formula, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are tuples of variables of the same length. Given a sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
, of tuples of the same length matching that of the
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
, we say that
\varphi
holds along
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
if for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
in
I
we have
\models\varphi(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}})
.

Definition 5.1.3
.

Let
\varphi(x,b)
be a formula over
Cb
and let
y
be a finite tuple of variables matching those elements of
b
that appear in
\varphi(x,b)
. Let
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
be a formula over
C
, where the length of each of
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
matches
y
. Then
\varphi(x,b)
is said to
\psi
-divide
over
C
if:

(i)

\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is an obstruction of
\exists x(\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k}))
,

(ii)

there is a sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{i}\equiv_{C}b
for all
i<\omega
, such that
\psi
holds along
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
.

Note that in (ii) in the above definition we may equivalently require there to be a sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
of tuples with
b^{\prime}_{i}\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
for all
i<\omega
, where
b^{\prime}
is the part of
b
that matches
y
, such that
\psi
holds along
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. So
\varphi(x,b)

\psi
-divides over
C
if and only if
\varphi(x,b^{\prime})

\psi
-divides over
C
.

Lemma 5.1.4
.

A set of formulas
\Sigma(x,b)
over
Cb
divides over
C
if and only if it contains a formula
\varphi(x,b)
that
\psi
-divides over
C
for some formula
\psi
.

Proof.

We first prove the left to right direction. Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence that witnesses that
\Sigma(x,b)
divides over
C
. By compactness there are
\varphi_{1}(x,z),\ldots,\varphi_{k}(x,z)\in\Sigma(x,z)
such that
\not\models\exists x(\varphi_{1}(x,b_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{k}(x,b_{k}))
. We thus find some
\psi(z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
that implies
\neg\exists x(\varphi_{1}(x,z_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{k}(x,z_{k}))
and
\models\psi(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})
. As these formulas only mention a finite number of the variables in
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
, we may omit unused variables to obtain finite subtuples of variables
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
, while we keep some unused variables to guarantee that these tuples all have the same length and match the same subtuple
y\subseteq z
. Let
\varphi(x,y)
be the formula
\varphi_{1}(x,y)\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{k}(x,y)
, so
\varphi(x,y)\in\Sigma(x,z)
and
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is an obstruction of
\exists x(\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k}))
. At the same time we have
\models\psi(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})
, which by indiscernibility implies that
\models\psi(b_{i_{1}},\ldots,b_{i_{k}})
for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<\omega
, as required.

For the other direction we let
\varphi(x,b)\in\Sigma(x,b)
be a formula that
\psi
-divides. Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be as in ?THM?
. By compactness we may elongate the sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
to
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
for some big enough
\lambda
. We can then base a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}
on
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
. As
\psi
holds along
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
it will hold along
(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}
, which in turn implies the inconsistency of
\{\varphi(x,b^{*}_{i}):i<\omega\}
and hence of
\bigcup_{i<\omega}\Sigma(x,b^{*}_{i})
.
∎

Corollary 5.1.5
.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
and
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
be formulas over
C
such that
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is inconsistent with
\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k})
. Then
\varphi(x,b)

\psi
-divides over
C
if and only if there is a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{0}=b
such that
\psi
holds along it.

Proof.

The right to left direction is immediate. The left to right follows the same proof as the second half in ?THM?
, after which we apply an automorphism over
C
to the indiscernible sequence to get
b_{0}=b
.
∎

Remark 5.1.6
.

In full first-order logic there is the notion of “
k
-dividing”, which says that a set of formulas is inconsistent along every
k
-subsequence of some infinite sequence. So this is very similar to
\psi
-dividing. In fact, if the set of formulas in question contains only one formula
\varphi(x,b)
, then it is exactly the same if we take
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
to be
\neg\exists x(\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k}))
. This is a common theme in positive logic. In full first-order logic it suffices to specify how many things are inconsistent with one another and we just use a formula that says “there does not exist …”. In positive logic we need a positive formula witnessing this.

Proposition 5.1.7
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
;

(ii)

for every
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{0}=b
there is a
Ca
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{Cb}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
;

(iii)

for every
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{0}=b
there is
a^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a
such that
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
Ca^{\prime}
-indiscernible.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (iii)
Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{0}=b
. Set
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
, so
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
is consistent. Let
a^{*}
be a realisation of this set of formulas. So we have
a^{*}b_{i}\equiv_{C}ab
for all
i<\omega
. By compactness we can elongate this sequence to
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
, where
\lambda=\lambda_{|T|+|Cba|}
, with that same property. Base a
Ca^{*}
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}
on
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
over
Ca^{*}
. Then
a^{*}b^{*}_{0}\equiv_{C}ab
, and we obtain (ii) by letting
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
be such that
a^{*}b^{*}_{0}(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{C}ab(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. To obtain (iii) we note that
(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{C}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
and we let
a^{\prime}
be such that
a^{*}(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
, so that
a^{\prime}b=a^{\prime}b_{0}\equiv_{C}a^{*}b^{*}_{0}\equiv_{C}ab
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) and (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
Write
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
and let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{0}=b
. We show in both cases that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
is consistent.

•

Assuming (ii) we find
Ca
-indiscernible
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{Cb}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
. Then
\models p(a,b^{\prime}_{0})
because
b^{\prime}_{0}=b
. So by
Ca
-indiscernibility,
a
is a realisation of
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b^{\prime}_{i})
, and the claim follows as
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{C}(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
.

•

Assuming (iii), we let
a^{\prime}
be such that
a^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a
and
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
Ca^{\prime}
-indiscernible. Then
a^{\prime}b_{0}=a^{\prime}b\equiv_{C}ab
, and so
\models p(a^{\prime},b_{0})
. So
a^{\prime}
realises
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
. ∎

5.2
Independence relations

Definition 5.2.1
.

An
independence relation

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is a ternary relation on small subsets of the monster model. If
A
,
B
and
C
are in the relation we write

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B,

which should be read as “
A
is independent from
B
over
C
”. We also allow tuples in the relation, which are then interpreted as the set they enumerate. For example, if
a
is a tuple enumerating
A
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
means the same as
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B
.

Example 5.2.2
.

As an example of a very nicely behaved independence relation we consider the theory of vector spaces over some fixed field
K
, in the signature
(\neq,0,+,\{s\cdot(-)\}_{s\in K})
, where
s\cdot(-)
for
s\in K
is a unary function symbol for scalar multiplication by
s
. So this is the usual Boolean theory of
K
-vector spaces. We define an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
based on linear independence as follows

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\linspan{(AC)}\cap\linspan{(BC)}=\linspan{(C)}.

This generalises linear independence in the following sense: a set
A
of vectors is linearly independent precisely when
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{\emptyset}A\setminus\{a\}
for all
a\in A
.

We give a list of all the properties that an independence relation can have (and we are interested in). We will not encounter all these properties straight away, but we mention them here anyway so that this definition can also serve as a reference.

Definition 5.2.3
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation. We define the following properties for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
, where
a
and
b
are arbitrary tuples and
C
is an arbitrary set.

invariance

For any
f\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
implies
f(a)\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{f(C)}f(b)
.

monotonicity

For any
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
and
b^{\prime}\subseteq b
we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
implies
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
.

normality

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then
Ca\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}Cb
.

existence

We always have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}C
.

full existence

There is always
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}\equiv_{C}b
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
.

base monotonicity

If
C\subseteq C^{\prime}\subseteq b
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
implies
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}b
.

extension

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then for any
d
there is
d^{\prime}
with
d^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}d
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bd^{\prime}
.

symmetry

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}a
.

transitivity

If
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
with
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}C^{\prime}
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}b
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
.

finite character

If for all finite
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
and all finite
b^{\prime}\subseteq b
we have
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
.

local character

For every cardinal
\kappa
there is a cardinal
\lambda
such that for all
a
with
|a|<\kappa
and any
C
there is
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<\lambda
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}C
.

independence theorem

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
with
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
then there is
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cb}a
and
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cc}a^{\prime}
such that
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bc
.

stationarity

For any
C
such that
a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}
implies
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
for all
a,a^{\prime}
, we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
and
a\equiv_{C}a
implies
a\equiv_{Cb}a^{\prime}
.

We will often use the properties
invariance
,
monotonicity
and
normality
of an independence relation implicitly.

The point of dividing is that it will give us an independence relation with some nice properties. However, it is actually the negation, non-dividing, that will mean that things are independent. Compare this for example to linear independence: we first define when vectors are linearly dependent (i.e., some non-trivial linear equation between them holds) and then we say that they are linearly independent if this does not happen (i.e., no non-trivial linear equation between them holds).

Definition 5.2.4
.

Let
A,B,C
be sets and let
a
and
b
enumerate
A
and
B
respectively. Then we write

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}B

if
\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
. We call this relation
dividing independence
.

It should be clear from the definition of dividing that if
a
and
a^{\prime}
are tuples enumerating the same set in possibly different ways then we have that
\tp(a/Cb)
divides over
C
if and only if
\tp(a^{\prime}/Cb)
divides over
C
. Similarly, nothing changes when changing the enumeration of
b
. So
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
is indeed a relation on subsets, and hence an independence relation.

Theorem 5.2.5
.

Dividing independence satisfies the following properties:
invariance
,
monotonicity
,
normality
,
existence
,
base monotonicity
,
finite character
and
left transitivity
. This final property is the same as
transitivity
with the sides of the independence relation swapped: if
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
then
C^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C^{\prime}}b
implies
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
.

Proof.

We prove each property separately.

invariance

By contrapositive: suppose that
p(x,f(b))=\tp(f(a)/f(C)f(b))
divides over
f(C)
. Then there is an
f(C)
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b^{\prime}_{i}\equiv_{f(C)}f(b)
for all
i<\omega
, such that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b^{\prime}_{i})
is inconsistent. Then
(f^{-1}(b^{\prime}_{i}))_{i<\omega}
witnesses dividing over
C
for
\tp(a/Cb)
.

monotonicity

By contrapositive: suppose that
p^{\prime}(x^{\prime},b^{\prime})=\tp(a^{\prime}/Cb^{\prime})
divides over
C
, as witnessed by a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. Elongate this to a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\lambda}
for
\lambda=\lambda_{|T|+|Cb|}
. For each
i<\lambda
use
b^{\prime}_{i}\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
to find
b_{i}\supseteq b^{\prime}_{i}
with
b_{i}b_{i}^{\prime}\equiv_{C}bb^{\prime}
. Let
(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence based on
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
over
C
. The restriction of
(b^{*}_{i})_{i<\omega}
to the subtuples matching
b^{\prime}
has the same type over
C
as
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
and so after applying an automorphism we may as well assume that
b^{\prime}_{i}
is the restriction of
b^{*}_{i}
to the subtuple matching
b^{\prime}
. Set
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
, then
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p^{\prime}(x^{\prime},b_{i})\subseteq\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b^{*}_{i})
, and so inconsistency of the former implies inconsistency of the latter.

normality

Let
c
enumerate
C
and set
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
and
q(xz,bc)=\tp(ac/Cbc)
. Let
(b_{i}c_{i})_{i<\omega}
be any
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{i}c_{i}\equiv_{C}bc
for all
i<\omega
. As
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{i}\equiv_{C}b
for all
i<\omega
and
p(x,b)
does not divide over
C
, there is a realisation
a^{\prime}
of
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
. For each
i<\omega
we have that
c_{i}=c
, and so
\models q(a^{\prime}c,b_{i}c_{i})
. We conclude that
a^{\prime}c
realises
\bigcup_{i<\omega}q(xz,b_{i}c_{i})
, showing that this set is consistent and hence that
q(xz,bc)
does not divide over
C
.

existence

Let
c
enumerate
C
and set
p(x,c)=\tp(a/Cc)
. Let
(c_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
c_{i}\equiv_{C}c
for all
i<\omega
. That is,
c_{i}=c
for all
i<\omega
. Hence
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,c_{i})=p(x,c)
, which is consistent.

base monotonicity

We will use the characterisation of dividing in ?THM?
(iii). Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C^{\prime}
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{0}=b
. Then
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is in particular
C
-indiscernible, because
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
. Since
\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
there is
a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a
such that
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
Ca^{\prime}
-indiscernible. As
C^{\prime}\subseteq b
we have
a^{\prime}\equiv_{C^{\prime}b}a
, and we conclude that
\tp(a/C^{\prime}b)
does not divide over
C^{\prime}
.

finite character

By contrapositive: suppose that
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
divides over
C
. Then by ?THM?
there is
\varphi(x,b)\in p(x,b)
and some
\psi
such that
\varphi(x,b)

\psi
-divides over
C
. Let
x^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
be the finite parts of
x
and
b
respectively that appear in
\varphi(x,b)
, then
\varphi(x^{\prime},b^{\prime})

\psi
-divides over
C
. As
\varphi(x^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\tp(a^{\prime}/Cb^{\prime})
, where
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
matches
x^{\prime}
, we can apply ?THM?
again (now in the other direction) to see that
\tp(a^{\prime}/Cb^{\prime})
divides over
C
.

left transitivity

We will use the characterisation of dividing in ?THM?
(ii). Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{0}=b
. As
C^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
there is a
C^{\prime}
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{Cb}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
. In particular,
b^{\prime}_{0}=b
, and so because
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C^{\prime}}b
there is a
C^{\prime}a
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
(b^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{C^{\prime}b}(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
. In particular, we have
(b^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{Cb}(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{Cb}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
, and so we conclude that
\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
. ∎

5.3
Definition of simplicity: local character and NTP

Definition 5.3.1
.

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be ordinals. Write
\alpha^{<\beta}
for the tree of functions
\gamma\to\alpha
for
\gamma<\beta
. The tree structure is given by setting
\nu\unlhd\eta
if
\eta
is an extension of
\nu
.

For a function
\eta:\gamma\to\alpha
and some
i<\alpha
, we write
\eta^{\frown}i
for the function
\gamma+1\to\alpha
that appends
i
to
\eta
. Formally:

\eta^{\frown}i(x)=\begin{cases}\eta(x)&\text{if }x<\gamma,\\
i&\text{if }x=\gamma.\end{cases}

The above notation is in line with viewing a function
\eta:\gamma\to\alpha
as a sequence of length
\gamma
of elements in
\alpha
. We will often take this view and as such we will often refer to the domain of
\eta
as its
length
.

Definition 5.3.2
.

Let
k\geq 2
be a natural number. A formula
\varphi(x,y)
is said to have the
k
-tree property
(
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
) if there are parameters
(a_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
and an obstruction
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
of the formula
\exists x(\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k}))
such that:

(i)

for all
\sigma\in\omega^{\omega}
the set
\{\varphi(x,a_{\sigma|_{n}}):n<\omega\}
is consistent,

(ii)

for all
\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}
and
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<\omega
we have
\models\psi(a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}},\ldots,a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{k}})
.

A formula
\varphi(x,y)
has the
tree property
(
\mathsf{TP}
) if there exists a natural number
k\geq 2
such that
\varphi(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
.

A theory has the
tree property
(
\mathsf{TP}
) if there is a formula that has the tree property, and otherwise it is
\mathsf{NTP}
.

Theorem 5.3.3
.

The following are equivalent for a theory
T
.

(i)

Dividing independence
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
local character
.

(ii)

For any finite
a
and any
C
there is
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<|T|^{+}
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C^{\prime}}C
.

(iii)

For every cardinal
\kappa
there is a cardinal
\lambda
such that for all
a
with
|a|<\kappa
and any sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\mu}
there is
i_{0}<\lambda
with
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{(b_{j})_{j<i_{0}}}(b_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<\mu}
.

(iv)

For any finite
a
and any sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\mu}
there is some
i_{0}<|T|^{+}
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{(b_{j})_{j<i_{0}}}(b_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<\mu}
.

(v)

The theory
T
is
\mathsf{NTP}
.

Definition 5.3.4
.

We call a theory
T

simple
if the equivalent conditions from ?THM?
hold.

We prove ?THM?
in the remainder of this section. A substantial part of it works for arbitrary independence relations.

Proposition 5.3.5
.

Suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
base monotonicity
,
normality
and
monotonicity
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
local character
, i.e. for every cardinal
\kappa
there is a cardinal
\lambda
such that for all
a
with
|a|<\kappa
and any
C
there is
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<\lambda
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}C
;

(ii)

for every cardinal
\kappa
there is a cardinal
\lambda
such that for all
a
with
|a|<\kappa
, any
C
and any sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\mu}
there is
i_{0}<\lambda
with
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C(b_{j})_{j<i_{0}}}(b_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<\mu}
.

Furthermore, for every
\kappa
we can take the
\lambda
in (i) and (ii) to be the same.

If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
also satisfies
finite character
then the above statements are further equivalent to the case where
\kappa
is fixed to be
\omega
. That is, if there is such a
\lambda
for
\kappa=\omega
then there is such a
\lambda
for every
\kappa
.

Proof.

Fix
\kappa
. We first prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Let
a
and
(b_{i})_{i<\mu}
be as in the statement of (ii). Set
B=\{b_{i}:i<\mu\}
and apply
local character
to
BC
. Then we find
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|B^{\prime}C^{\prime}|<\lambda
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{B^{\prime}C^{\prime}}BC
. As
|B^{\prime}|<\lambda
there is
i_{0}<\lambda
such that
B^{\prime}\subseteq\{b_{j}:j<i_{0}\}
. The result then follows by
base-monotonicity
and
monotonicity
.

For the converse we let
a
be such that
|a|<\kappa
and
C
be any set. Let
(c_{i})_{i<\mu}
enumerate
C
. By assumption there is
i_{0}<\lambda
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{(c_{j})_{j<i_{0}}}(c_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<\mu}
. Setting
C^{\prime}=\{c_{j}:j<i_{0}\}
then yields the required independence, after an application of
normality
.

Finally, assuming
finite character
, we will show that if (i) holds for
\kappa=\omega
then it holds for all
\kappa
. So let
\kappa
be arbitrary and let
a
be such that
|a|<\kappa
. Let
\lambda
be as in (i) for the
\omega
case and set
\lambda^{\prime}=|\lambda\times[\kappa]^{<\omega}|^{+}
. We claim that for any
C
there is
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<\lambda^{\prime}
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}C
. For each finite
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
we let
C_{a^{\prime}}\subseteq C
be such that
|C_{a^{\prime}}|<\lambda
and
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C_{a^{\prime}}}C
. Set
C^{\prime}=\bigcup\{C_{a^{\prime}}:a^{\prime}\subseteq a\text{ finite}\}
, then by construction
|C^{\prime}|<\lambda^{\prime}
. For all finite
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
we have
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}C
by
base monotonicity
. Hence by
finite character
we have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}C
, as required.
∎

Proof of ?THM?
.

By ?THM?
we have (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) and (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv), in the latter it is crucial that the
\lambda
in ?THM?
can be taken to be the same in both cases. By that same result we have that (ii) and (iv) imply (i) and (iii). It thus suffices to prove (v)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (iii)
\Rightarrow
(v), both of which we prove by contrapositive.

(v)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
Let
a
be finite and let
C
be such that
\tp(a/C)
divides over
C^{\prime}
for all
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<|T|^{+}
. We will construct an instance of
\mathsf{TP}
. We first construct a tree
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<|T|^{+}}}
by induction on its height. Let
\zeta_{\alpha}\in\omega^{\alpha}
denote the constant zero function. Our induction hypothesis at
\delta<\lambda
, where we have constructed
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{\leq\delta}}
, will be as follows:

(1)

c_{\zeta_{\alpha}}
is a finite tuple of elements from
C
for all
\alpha\leq\delta
;

(2)

(c_{\eta|_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}\equiv(c_{\zeta_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}
for all
\eta\in\omega^{\delta}
;

(3)

if
\delta=\gamma+1
is a successor then there are
\varphi_{\delta}(x,y)
and an obstruction
\psi_{\delta}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k_{\delta}})
of
\exists x(\varphi_{\delta}(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{\delta}(x,y_{k_{\delta}}))
such that
\models\varphi_{\delta}(a,c_{\zeta_{\delta}})
and for any
\eta\in\omega^{\gamma}
we have that
\psi_{\delta}
holds along
(c_{\eta^{\frown}i})_{i<\omega}
.

For
\delta<|T|^{+}
a limit or zero we let all
c_{\eta}
, where
\eta\in\omega^{\delta}
, be the empty tuple. Now suppose that
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{\leq\delta}}
has been constructed. By (1) we have that
|\{c_{\zeta_{\alpha}}:\alpha\leq\delta\}|<|T|^{+}
. So
\tp(a/C)
divides over
\{c_{\zeta_{\alpha}}:\alpha\leq\delta\}
. Let then
\varphi_{\delta+1}(x,c)\in\tp(a/C)
and
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
be such that
\varphi_{\delta+1}(x,c)

\psi
-divides over
\{c_{\zeta_{\alpha}}:\alpha\leq\delta\}
. Then there is a sequence
(d_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
d_{i}\equiv_{(c_{\zeta_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}}c
for all
i<\omega
such that
\psi
holds along
(d_{i})_{i<\omega}
. After applying an automorphism, we may assume that
d_{0}=c
. Furthermore, we may assume that the part of
\{c_{\zeta_{\alpha}}:\alpha\leq\delta\}
that is mentioned in
\varphi_{\delta+1}(x,c)
and
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is contained in
c
(and hence in each
d_{i}
), and so
\varphi_{\delta+1}(x,y)
and
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
do not contain any parameters. We now define
c_{\zeta_{\delta}^{\frown}i}=d_{i}
for all
i<\omega
. This ensures (1). For
\eta\in\omega^{\delta}
we have
(c_{\eta|_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}\equiv(c_{\zeta_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}
by (2) from the induction hypothesis. We then let
(c_{\eta^{\frown}i})_{i<\omega}
be such that
(c_{\eta^{\frown}i})_{i<\omega}(c_{\eta|_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}\equiv(d_{i})_{i<\omega}(c_{\zeta_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}
. Then (2) follows because
d_{i}\equiv_{(c_{\zeta_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta}}c=d_{0}=c_{\zeta_{\delta+1}}
for all
i<\omega
. Finally, (3) follows because for any
\eta\in\omega^{\delta}
the sequence
(c_{\eta^{\frown}i})_{i<\omega}
is an automorphic copy of
(d_{i})_{i<\omega}
, along which
\psi
holds. This completes the inductive construction of the tree
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<|T|^{+}}}
.

There are
|T|
possible pairs for formulas
\varphi(x,y)
and
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
. We have
|T|^{+}
successor levels in our tree, each of which is assigned a pair
\varphi_{\delta}(x,y)
and
\psi_{\delta}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k_{\delta}})
. Hence, by the pigeonhole principle there is an infinite set
l_{0}<l_{1}<l_{2}<\ldots<|T|^{+}
of levels to which the same
\varphi_{\delta}
and
\psi_{\delta}
are assigned. We write just
\varphi
and
\psi
for these formulas. We consider the following subtree
(f_{\mu})_{\mu\in\omega^{<\omega}}
that consists of the chosen levels (with the root being the leftmost point on level
l_{0}
). To be precise, for
\mu\in\omega^{<\omega}
of length
n
we define
\eta_{\mu}\in\omega^{l_{n}}
of length
l_{n}
as

\eta_{\mu}(l)=\begin{cases}\mu(i)&\text{if }l=l_{i+1}-1,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
l_{i+1}-1
makes sense because we have only chosen successor levels. We then set
f_{\mu}=c_{\eta_{\mu}}
.

We claim that
(f_{\mu})_{\mu\in\omega^{<\omega}}
witnesses
\mathsf{TP}
for
\varphi(x,y)
. Let
\sigma\in\omega^{\omega}
. By construction and (2), we have
(f_{\sigma|_{n}})_{n<\omega}\equiv(f_{\zeta_{n}})_{n<\omega}
. Then by (1) and (3) we have that
a
realises
\{\varphi(x,f_{\zeta_{n}}):n<\omega\}
, and so we conclude that
\{\varphi(x,f_{\sigma|_{n}}):n<\omega\}
is consistent. Finally,
\psi
holds along
(f_{\eta^{\frown}i})_{i<\omega}
for every
\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}
by (3) and because we only chose successor levels for
(l_{n})_{n<\omega}
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(v)
We will show that (iii) is violated for
\kappa=\omega
. Let
\varphi(x,y)
have
\mathsf{TP}
as witnessed by
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
and
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
. Let
\lambda
be any cardinal. Set
\mu=(2^{|T|+\lambda})^{+}
and, using compactness, enlarge our tree to
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\mu^{<\lambda}}
.

We construct some
\sigma\in\mu^{\lambda}
by induction on its length. Let
\sigma|_{\gamma}
be defined for
\gamma<\lambda
. Write
C=\{c_{\sigma|_{i}}:i\leq\gamma\}
and
\eta=\sigma|_{\gamma}
. There are at most
2^{|T|+\lambda}
different types over
C
. So by our choice of
\mu
, there exists an infinite
I_{\gamma}\subseteq\mu
such that for any
i,j\in I_{\gamma}
we have
c_{\eta^{\frown}i}\equiv_{C}c_{\eta^{\frown}j}
. Let
i_{0}
be the least element of
I_{\gamma}
and set
\sigma(\gamma)=i_{0}
.

Having finished the construction of
\sigma
we define
b_{i}=c_{\sigma|_{i+1}}
for all
i<\lambda
. Then there is a realisation
a
of
\{\varphi(x,b_{i}):i<\lambda\}
. Let
\gamma<\lambda
then by construction we have that
c_{{\sigma|_{\gamma}}^{\frown}i}\equiv_{(b_{j})_{j<\gamma}}b_{\gamma}
for all
i\in I_{\gamma}
, while
\psi
holds along
(c_{{\sigma|_{\gamma}}^{\frown}i})_{i\in I_{\gamma}}
. In particular, this means that
\varphi(x,b_{\gamma})

\psi
-divides over
(b_{j})_{j<\gamma}
and since
\varphi(x,b_{\gamma})\in\tp(a/(b_{i})_{i<\lambda})
we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{(b_{j})_{j<\gamma}}(b_{j})_{\gamma\leq j<\lambda}
. As
\lambda
and
\gamma
were arbitrary, and
a
is finite, we conclude that (iii) fails.
∎

5.4
Thickness implies full existence for dividing independence

The aim of this section is to prove that dividing independence satisfies full existence in simple theories. It turns out that we also need to assume thickness (see ?THM?
). We start with the statement of the main result of this section, and the remainder is devoted to the rather technical proof (whose tools will have no further use to us).

Theorem 5.4.1
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is simple then dividing independence has
full existence
. That is, for any
a,b,C
, there is
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}\equiv_{C}b
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b^{\prime}
.

Definition 5.4.2
.

A
sequence of dividing witnesses

over
C
is a sequence
\zeta=(\varphi_{i}(x,y^{i}),\psi_{i}(y^{i}_{1},\ldots,y^{i}_{k_{i}}))_{i\in I}
of pairs for formulas over
C
such that
\psi_{i}(y^{i}_{1},\ldots,y^{i}_{k_{i}})
is an obstruction of
\exists x(\varphi_{i}(x,y^{i}_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{i}(x,y^{i}_{k_{i}}))
.

Given such a sequence of
\zeta
of dividing witnesses, a
\zeta
-dividing sequence

(over
C
)
is a sequence of tuples
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
\{\varphi_{i}(x,b_{i}):i\in I\}
is consistent and for all
i\in I
we have that
\varphi_{i}(x,b_{i})

\psi_{i}
-divides over
C(b_{j})_{j<i}
.

It is clear from the definition that dividing sequences are stable under taking subsequences. More precisely, suppose we are given a sequence of dividing witnesses
\zeta
and a
\zeta
-dividing sequence
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
. Let
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, and let
\zeta^{\prime}\subseteq\zeta
be the corresponding subsequence of dividing witnesses. Then
(b_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
is a
\zeta^{\prime}
-dividing sequence.

Lemma 5.4.3
.

Assume thickness. Let
\zeta=(\varphi_{i}(x,y^{i}),\psi_{i}(y^{i}_{1},\ldots,y^{i}_{k_{i}}))_{i\in I}
be a sequence of dividing witnesses over
C
. Then being a
\zeta
-dividing sequence over
C
is type-definable over
C
. Moreover, the defining set of formulas is entirely determined by its restriction to finite subsequences.

More precisely, for any
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
there is a set of formulas
\Sigma_{I^{\prime}}((y^{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}})
over
C
such that
\models\Sigma_{I^{\prime}}((b_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}})
if and only if
(b_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
is a
(\varphi_{i}(x,y^{i}),\psi_{i}(y^{i}_{1},\ldots,y^{i}_{k_{i}}))_{i\in I^{\prime}}
-dividing sequence over
C
. Moreover, for infinite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
this set of formulas is given by
\Sigma_{I^{\prime}}=\bigcup\{\Sigma_{I_{0}}:I_{0}\subseteq I^{\prime}\text{ is finite}\}
.

Proof.

For
i\in I
we let
\Gamma_{i}(y^{i},z)
be the set of formulas over
C
expressing

\exists(y_{j})_{j<\omega}(\text{``$(y_{j})_{j<\omega}$ is $Cz$-indiscernible''}\wedge\text{``$\psi_{i}$ holds along $(y_{j})_{j<\omega}$''}\wedge y_{0}=y^{i}).

Note that we used the thickness assumption here to have the first conjunct in the above be type-definable.

For
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
we define
\Sigma_{I^{\prime}}((y^{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}})
to be the set of formulas expressing

\exists x\bigwedge_{i\in I^{\prime}}\varphi(x,y^{i})\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in I^{\prime}}\Gamma_{i}(y^{i},(y^{j})_{j\in I^{\prime},j<i}).

Then
\Sigma_{I^{\prime}}
defines being a
(\varphi_{i}(x,y^{i}),\psi_{i}(y^{i}_{1},\ldots,y^{i}_{k_{i}}))_{i\in I^{\prime}}
-dividing sequence over
C
, as is seen by writing out definitions, where we used the characterisation of
\psi
-dividing from ?THM?
.

To see the claim about finite subsequences, we note that for any infinite
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
we have that
\Gamma_{i}(y^{i},(y^{j})_{j\in I^{\prime},j<i})
is equivalent to
\bigcup\{\Gamma_{i}(y^{i},(y^{j})_{j\in I_{0},j<i}):I_{0}\subseteq I^{\prime}\text{ is finite}\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4.4
.

Let
\zeta
be some finite sequence of dividing witnesses and let
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
be a
\zeta
-dividing sequence, both over
C
. Then for any tuple
d
there is
d^{\prime}
with
d^{\prime}\equiv_{C}d
such that
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
is a
\zeta
-dividing sequence over
Cd^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Write
\zeta=(\varphi_{i},\psi_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
. We will prove by induction on
0\leq k\leq n
that there is
d_{k}
with
d_{k}\equiv_{C}d
such that for all
i\geq 1
with
i\leq k
there is a
C(b_{j})_{j<i}d_{k}
-indiscernible sequence starting with
b_{i}
such that
\psi_{i}
holds along it. Then taking
d^{\prime}=d_{n}
yields the desired result.

For
k=0
we take
d
. Now assume we have constructed
d_{k}
. Let
s
be a
C(b_{j})_{j\leq k}
-indiscernible sequence starting with
b_{k+1}
such that
\psi_{k+1}
holds along it. We apply ?THM?
to
s
and
d_{k}
to find
d_{k+1}
such that
d_{k+1}\equiv_{C(b_{j})_{j\leq k}}d_{k}
and
s
is
C(b_{j})_{j\leq k}d_{k+1}
-indiscernible. Then
d_{k+1}
satisfies the induction hypothesis: for
i=k+1
by construction and for
i\leq k
because that part of the induction hypothesis is invariant under automorphisms over
C(b_{j})_{j\leq k}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4.5
.

Assume thickness. Suppose that there are
a,b,C
such that any extension of
\tp(a/C)
to a type over
Cb
divides over
C
. Then for any ordinal
\delta
there is a sequence of dividing witnesses
\zeta
and a
\zeta
-dividing sequence
(b_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
, where
\delta^{\textup{op}}
carries the opposite order of
\delta
.

Proof.

We construct a sequence
\zeta=(\varphi_{i}(x,y),\psi_{i}(y^{i}_{1},\ldots,y^{i}_{k_{i}}))_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
and
(b_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
such that
a
realises
\{\varphi_{i}(x,b_{i}):i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}\}
by induction on
\delta
. The base case is trivial and the limit stage follows from type-definability of dividing sequences (?THM?
).

So let us assume that
\zeta
and
(b_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
have been constructed. We will construct
b_{\delta}
and a pair
(\varphi(x,y),\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}))
such that
(b_{i})_{i\in(\delta+1)^{\textup{op}}}
is a
(\varphi,\psi)^{\frown}\zeta
-dividing sequence. Let
\Sigma((y_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}})
be the set of formulas from ?THM?
that expresses that
(y_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
is a
\zeta
-dividing sequence over
Cb
. We claim that the set of formulas

\tp((b_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}/C)\cup\Sigma((y_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}})

over
Cb
is consistent. Indeed, for any finite subsequence
(b_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}\subseteq(b_{i})_{i\in I}
we can apply ?THM?
to find
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}\equiv_{C}b
such that
(b_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}
is a
\zeta_{0}
-dividing sequence over
Cb^{\prime}
(where
\zeta_{0}\subseteq\zeta
matches
I_{0}\subseteq I
). Hence there is
(b^{*}_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}
with
b(b^{*}_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}(b_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}
, which realises the corresponding finite part of the above set of formulas.

Let
(b^{*}_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
realise the above set of formulas. Let
b_{\delta}
then be such that

b_{\delta}(b_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}\equiv_{C}b(b^{*}_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}},

so
(b_{i})_{i\in\delta^{\textup{op}}}
is a
\zeta
-dividing sequence over
Cb_{\delta}
and
\tp(a/Cb_{\delta})
divides over
C
, because it is the image of an extension of
\tp(a/C)
to
Cb
under an automorphism over
C
. Therefore, there is some
\varphi(x,b_{\delta})\in\tp(a/Cb_{\delta})
and a
\psi
such that
\varphi(x,b_{\delta})

\psi
-divides over
C
. By ?THM?
these two facts together say precisely that
(b_{i})_{i\in(\delta+1)^{\textup{op}}}
is a
(\varphi,\psi)^{\frown}\zeta
-dividing sequence over
C
. This completes the inductive construction, and hence the proof.
∎

Proof of ?THM?
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
full existence
fails for dividing independence. Then there is
p(x)=\tp(a/C)
and some
b
such that any extension of
p(x)
to a type over
Cb
divides over
C
. Let
\kappa=(|C|+|T|)^{+}
and apply ?THM?
to find a sequence
\zeta=(\varphi_{i},\psi_{i})_{i\in\kappa^{\textup{op}}}
of dividing witnesses and a
\zeta
-dividing sequence
(b_{i})_{i\in\kappa^{\textup{op}}}
. As
\varphi_{i}
and
\psi_{i}
are formulas over
C
for each
i<\kappa
, we can apply the pigeonhole principle to find infinite
I\subseteq\kappa
such that for all
i,j\in I
we have
(\varphi_{i},\psi_{i})=(\varphi_{j},\psi_{j})
. Let us call this tuple of formulas
(\varphi,\psi)
and let
\zeta^{\prime}
be the constant sequence of length
|T|^{+}
whose entries are
(\varphi,\psi)
. Let
\Sigma((y_{i})_{i<|T|^{+}})
be the set of formulas from ?THM?
that expresses that
(y_{i})_{i<|T|^{+}}
is a
\zeta^{\prime}
-dividing sequence over
C
. This type is finitely satisfiable by the finite subsequences of
(b_{i})_{i\in I^{\textup{op}}}
, and so we find a
\zeta^{\prime}
-dividing sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<|T|^{+}}
over
C
. Let
a^{\prime}
be a realisation of
\{\varphi(x,b^{\prime}_{i}):i<|T|^{+}\}
. For all
i<|T|^{+}
we have that
\varphi(x,b^{\prime}_{i})

\psi
-divides over
C(b^{\prime}_{j})_{j<i}
. As
\varphi(x,b^{\prime}_{i})\in\tp(a^{\prime}/C(b^{\prime}_{j})_{j\leq|T|^{+}})
, we have that this type divides over
C(b^{\prime}_{j})_{j<i}
. However, this contradicts the assumption that
T
is simple by ?THM?
.
∎

5.5
Morley sequences and Kim’s lemma

Definition 5.5.1
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation. A
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}
-independent sequence
is a sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
a_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}(a_{j})_{j<i}
for all
i\in I
.

Proposition 5.5.2
.

If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
invariance
,
monotonicity
and
full existence
then for any
a
and
C
and any cardinal
\kappa
there is a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}
-independent sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
with
a_{i}\equiv_{C}a
for all
i<\kappa
.

Proof.

Let
N^{\prime}\supseteq C
be a positively
(|Ca|^{+}+\kappa)
