is a simple theory, then dividing independence satisfies the
independence theorem
. That is, suppose we are given
a,a^{\prime},b,c,C
, such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c
with
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
. Then there is
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cb}a
and
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cc}a^{\prime}
such that
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}bc
.

Proof.

We first argue that we may assume
b
and
c
to enumerate positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models containing
C
. Let
M
be a positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model containing
Cb
(?THM?
). By
extension
(and
symmetry
) applied to
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c
there is
M^{\prime}
with
M^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}M
such that
M^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c
. Applying
strong extension
(?THM?
), this time to
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
, together with
invariance
yields
a^{*}
such that
a^{*}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cb}a
and
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}M^{\prime}
. We now replace
a
with
a^{*}
and
b
with
M^{\prime}
. Analogously, we can replace
a^{\prime}
with a tuple that has the same Lascar strong type over
Cc
and replace
c
with some positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model containing
Cc
. Finding an
a^{\prime\prime}
as in the conclusion of the theorem now also works for our original
a,a^{\prime},b
and
c
.

Set
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
and
q(x,c)=\tp(a^{\prime}/Cc)
. By ?THM?
, and our assumption on
b
and
c
, it then suffices to prove that
p(x,b)\cup q(x,c)
does not divide over
C
. Using
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
we let
c^{\prime}
be such that
ac^{\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}c
. Applying ?THM?
to
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c^{\prime}
we find
c^{\prime\prime}
with
c^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Ca}c^{\prime}
,
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}bc^{\prime\prime}
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c^{\prime\prime}
. Applying ?THM?
again, this time to
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c^{\prime\prime}
, we find
c^{*}
with
c^{*}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cb}c^{\prime\prime}
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}cc^{*}
.

Since
c^{*}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}c^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}c^{\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}c
, there are
c^{*}=c_{0},\ldots,c_{n}=c
such that
c_{i}
and
c_{i+1}
start a Morley sequence over
C
for all
i<n
by ?THM?
. By
extension
applied to
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}cc^{*}
we may assume that
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}c_{0}\ldots c_{n}
. It then follows by induction on
i\leq n
that
p(x,b)\cup q(x,c_{i})
does not divide over
C
. Indeed, for the base case we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}bc^{\prime\prime}
, so
p(x,b)\cup q(x,c^{\prime\prime})
does not divide over
C
, and it follows that
p(x,b)\cup q(x,c_{0})
does not divide over
C
because
bc_{0}=bc^{*}\equiv_{C}bc^{\prime\prime}
. The induction step is precisely ?THM?
. This concludes our proof, because
c_{n}=c
.
∎

5.8
The Kim-Pillay style theorem

Theorem 5.8.1

(Kim-Pillay style theorem)
.

Assume thickness. A theory
T
is simple if and only if there is an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfying
invariance
,
monotonicity
,
normality
,
existence
,
full existence
,
base monotonicity
,
extension
,
symmetry
,
transitivity
,
finite character
,
local character
and
independence theorem
. Furthermore, in this case,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
.

Remark 5.8.2
.

One direction of ?THM?
can be strengthened as follows. To conclude that
T
is simple we can assume a weaker version of
independence theorem
. More precisely, suppose that
T
is thick and that we are given an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfying all the properties in ?THM?
, except that instead of
independence theorem
we assume the following. For any positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model
M
and any
a,a^{\prime},b,c
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}c
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}c
with
a\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}
there is
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Mb}a
and
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Mc}a^{\prime}
such that
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}bc
. Then
T
is simple and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
. So in particular, the full version of
independence theorem
then holds for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
.

We also note in the weakened version of the
independence theorem
we only assume
a\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}
and not
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{M}a^{\prime}
. However, the latter is automatic by ?THM?
, so this is truly a weakening of
independence theorem
.

Remark 5.8.3
.

The properties
normality
and
full existence
could be left out from ?THM?
. This is because they follow from the remaining properties.

If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
extension
and
symmetry
then it satisfies
normality
. Indeed, if
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then we apply
extension
to find
c^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}C
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}C^{\prime}b
, but then
C^{\prime}=C
and so
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}Cb
. We get
Ca\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}Cb
by applying
symmetry
, repeating the argument and then applying
symmetry
again.

If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
existence
and
extension
then it satisfies
full existence
. Indeed, let
a
,
b
and
C
be arbitrary. Then by
existence
we have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}C
. So by
extension
we find the required
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}\equiv_{C}b
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
.

Proof of ?THM?
.

Earlier in this chapter, we have shown that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies all the properties listed in the theorem, provided that
T
is simple. We will now prove that given an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfying the above list of properties (with
independence theorem
adjusted as in ?THM?
), we have that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
. Simplicity of
T
then follows, because this means in particular that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
local character
.

We first prove that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
implies
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
. By ?THM?
there is a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-Morley sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
over
C
with
b_{0}=b
. In particular,
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is a
C
-indiscernible sequence, so, writing
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
, we have that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
is consistent. Applying ?THM?
we find
a^{\prime}
with
\models p(a^{\prime},b)
and
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
. So
a^{\prime}b\equiv_{C}ab
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
follows from
invariance
.

Now assume
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
. We prove that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
. Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{0}=b
, and write
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
. We will show that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
is consistent.

By compactness we can elongate
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
to
(b_{i})_{i\leq\lambda}
, taking
\lambda
big enough to apply
local character
for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
with respect to
\kappa=|b|
. We inductively construct a chain
(M_{i})_{i<\lambda}
of positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models such that for all
i<\lambda
:

(1)

C(b_{j})_{j<i}\subseteq M_{i}
,

(2)

(b_{j})_{i\leq j\leq\lambda}
is
M_{i}
-indiscernible.

The base case and limit stage follow the same argument. Let
\ell<\lambda
be a limit (or
\ell=0
). Let
M
be any
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model containing
C(M_{i})_{i<\ell}
. By the induction hypothesis
(b_{i})_{\ell\leq i\leq\lambda}
is
C(M_{i})_{i<\ell}
-indiscernible. So by ?THM?
we find
M_{\ell}
with
M_{\ell}\equiv_{C(M_{i})_{i<\ell}}M
such that
(b_{i})_{\ell\leq i\leq\lambda}
is
M_{\ell}
-indiscernible.

For the successor step we assume
M_{i}
has been constructed. Let
M
be any
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model containing
M_{i}b_{i}
. By the induction hypothesis
(b_{j})_{i\leq j\leq\lambda}
is
M_{i}
-indiscernible, and so
(b_{j})_{i+1\leq j\leq\lambda}
is
M_{i}b_{i}
-indiscernible. So by ?THM?
we find
M_{i+1}
with
M_{i+1}\equiv_{M_{i}b_{i}}M
such that
(b_{j})_{i+1\leq j\leq\lambda}
is
M_{i+1}
-indiscernible. This finishes the construction of the chain
(M_{i})_{i<\lambda}
.

Set
M=\bigcup_{i<\lambda}M_{i}
and apply ?THM?
to
(M_{i})_{i<\lambda}
viewed as a sequence to find
i_{0}<\lambda
with
b_{\lambda}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}M
. Let
(b^{\prime}_{n})_{n<\omega}
be the sequence defined by
b^{\prime}_{n}=b_{i_{0}+n}
, then
(b^{\prime}_{n})_{n<\omega}
is a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-Morley sequence over
M_{i_{0}}
. Indeed, this sequence is
M_{i_{0}}
-indiscernible by construction. To see that it is a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}
-independent sequence we use that for any
n<\omega
we have
b_{\lambda}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}(b_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<i_{0}+n}
by
monotonicity
and conclude by
b_{\lambda}(b_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<i_{0}+n}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}b_{i_{0}+n}(b_{j})_{i_{0}\leq j<i_{0}+n}
and
invariance
. By
C
-indiscernibility of
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
, of which
(b^{\prime}_{n})_{n<\omega}
is a subsequence, it suffices now to prove that
\bigcup_{n<\omega}p(x,b^{\prime}_{n})
is consistent.

As
b^{\prime}_{0}\equiv_{C}b
there is
a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}_{0}\equiv_{C}ab
and so
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}_{0}
. Applying
extension
to this last independence, and after applying an automorphism, we find
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Cb^{\prime}_{0}}a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}M_{i_{0}}b^{\prime}_{0}
. By
base monotonicity
and
monotonicity
we then have
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{0}
. By induction on
n<\omega
we will prove that there is
a_{n}
that realises
\bigcup_{i\leq n}p(x,b^{\prime}_{i})
with
a_{n}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}a^{\prime\prime}
and
a_{n}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{0}\ldots b^{\prime}_{n}
. This suffices, because then by compactness
\bigcup_{n<\omega}p(x,b^{\prime}_{n})
is consistent.

For the base case we take
a_{0}=a^{\prime\prime}
, which can be done because
\models p(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}_{i_{0}})
and so
\models p(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime}_{i_{0}})
. For the inductive step we assume that we have constructed
a_{n}
. As
b^{\prime}_{n+1}
is on some
M_{i_{0}}
-indiscernible sequence that
b^{\prime}_{0}
is on as well, we have
b^{\prime}_{n+1}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{0}
. Let
a^{*}
be such that
a^{*}b^{\prime}_{n+1}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}a^{\prime\prime}b^{\prime}_{0}
, so
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{D}b^{\prime}_{n+1}
. Using
a_{n}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}a^{*}
we apply the weakened version of
independence theorem
for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
to
a_{n}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{0}\ldots b^{\prime}_{n}
,
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{n+1}
and
b^{\prime}_{n+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{0}\ldots b^{\prime}_{n}
to find
a_{n+1}
with
a_{n+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}b^{\prime}_{0}\ldots b^{\prime}_{n+1}
, as well as
a_{n+1}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}b^{\prime}_{0}\ldots b^{\prime}_{n}}a_{n}
and
a_{n+1}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}b^{\prime}_{n+1}}a^{*}
. Either of these last two equalities of types then implies
a_{n+1}\equiv_{M_{i_{0}}}a^{\prime\prime}
, and together they imply that
a_{n+1}
realises
\bigcup_{i\leq n+1}p(x,b^{\prime}_{i})
. This completes the inductive construction and thus the argument.
∎

5.9
Bibliographic remarks

Simplicity in positive logic was first developed by Pillay in 2000 for what we call Pillay theories
[
Pil00
]
. Later, Ben-Yaacov generalised this in 2003 to the full generality of positive logic
[
BY03b
]
. In our treatment we have emphasised the semantic side of independence and work as much as possible with abstract independence relations
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
and general properties of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
.

In Pillay’s approach, simplicity was defined as forking independence having
local character
(so he does treat forking in positive logic, cf. ?THM?
), whereas Ben-Yaacov defined it as dividing independence having
local character
. Since both forking independence and dividing independence always satisfy
base monotonicity
, Pillay’s approach automatically gives
full existence
. In Ben-Yaacov’s approach
full existence
is not automatic, and as we have seen it requires a lot of work in thick theories (see Section
5.4
). There is even a stable positive theory where
full existence
for dividing independence fails
[
BY03b
, Example 4.3]
(see also the discussion of ultrametric spaces with distances in
\mathbb{N}
in Section
2.5
), so this theory is not simple in Pillay’s sense. We therefore view Ben-Yaacov’s definition as the right notion of simplicity, which is furthermore confirmed by it being equivalent to
\mathsf{NTP}
(?THM?
). Of course, for thick theories, the two definitions coincide because assuming either it can be proved that forking and dividing coincide.

We have decided to only treat simplicity for thick theories, which simplifies the treatment and allows us to stay closer to the treatment in full first-order logic. This still captures a large class of positive theories, see for example
[
Kam24
, Section 2]
for a list of (classes of) examples of thick theories.

Remark 5.9.1
.

The proof strategy for ?THM?
,
full existence
for dividing independence in a thick simple theory, is very similar to the usual approach in full first-order logic. In both cases one constructs dividing sequences, which have to keep track of some witness of dividing. In full first-order logic this is the
k
in
k
-dividing, but in positive logic this becomes a formula, namely the
\psi
in
\psi
-dividing (see also ?THM?
). This makes it so that we have to consider longer sequences. However, the real use for thickness is ?THM?
, ensuring that being a dividing sequences is type-definable, which is also heavily used in the full first-order approach. This allows us to reshape the dividing sequences, and to reduce to finite dividing sequences, which is important for ?THM?
.

Without thickness not all is lost, but the theory becomes more subtle and complicated. For example, we no longer get
full existence
for dividing over any set, even in stable theories (see also the discussion of ultrametric spaces with distances in
\mathbb{N}
in Section
2.5
). However, we do get it over positively
\lambda
-saturated p.c. models for big enough
\lambda
. This is all worked out in
[
BY03b
]
.

Adapting the definition of
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
to positive logic (?THM?
) is now a standard trick that is due to
[
HK21
]
, where they give positive versions of
\mathsf{TP_{2}}
and
\mathsf{SOP_{1}}
. The particular definition of
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
first appears in
[
DGK23
, Definition 4.3]
, where its equivalence to simplicity (in terms of local character for dividing) was also proved
[
DGK23
, Theorem 6.14]
.

The reason for calling ?THM?
a “Kim-Pillay style theorem” is because Kim and Pillay first characterised simple theories in terms of the existence of a (unique) independence relation
[
KP97
, Theorem 4.2]
. Their theorem is for full first-order logic. ?THM?
can be pieced together from Ben-Yaacov’s work
[
BY03b
,
BY03c
]
, but the addition of the thickness assumption allows for a simpler statement similar to the original
[
KP97
, Theorem 4.2]
.

In the next chapter we treat stability in positive logic and connect it to simplicity as treated in this chapter. Another direction would be to treat the development of Kim-independence in thick
\mathsf{NSOP_{1}}
theories, for which we refer the reader to
[
DK22
]
(with a correction to the proof of the independence theorem in
[
DK24
]
).

Chapter 6
Stable theories

In this chapter we view stable theories through the lens of dividing independence. We build on the results for simple theories from Chapter
5
, and use this to show that the main difference between stability and simplicity is the
stationarity
property for dividing independence. This results in a Kim-Pillay style theorem for stable theories (?THM?
).

Classically there are many equivalent definitions of stability. We use the type counting definition (?THM?
), but we establish the usual equivalence with definability of types and the non-existence of binary trees (?THM?
). Notably, we do not consider the order property. This is solely because we have no use for it in these notes, and not because it causes problems in positive logic. In fact, when correctly formulated, not having the order property is further equivalent to the conditions we give here (see also ?THM?
).

6.1
Invariant types

Definition 6.1.1
.

A
global type
is a type
q(x)
over the monster model. That is, it is a maximally consistent set of formulas over the monster model.

A global type will generally not have a realisation in the monster model. However, it will often be convenient to work with realisations of global types. This can be done in roughly two different ways:

1.

the realisation lives in some bigger monster model;

2.

we restrict the global type to some sufficiently saturated p.c. submodel of the monster, containing all the other parameters we are interested in, and realise this restriction.

We will take the first view, but if the reader is not comfortable with this due to their preferred formalism for the monster model then using the second approach does not change the arguments whatsoever.

Convention 6.1.2
.

We generally use Greek lowercase letters
\alpha,\beta,\ldots
for realisations of global types.

Definition 6.1.3
.

Let
p(x)=\tp(a/B)
be a type and let
C\subseteq B
. We say that
p(x)
is
C
-invariant
if the following equivalent conditions hold:

(i)

for any
b,b^{\prime}\in B
with
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
we have
ab\equiv_{C}ab^{\prime}
;

(ii)

for any
b,b^{\prime}\in B
with
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
and any formula
\varphi(x,y)
we have
\varphi(x,b)\in p(x)
if and only if
\varphi(x,b^{\prime})\in p(x)
.

The equivalence of the conditions in the above definition should be obvious.

We are particularly interested in global invariant types. In full first-order logic there are many of these (as also follows from ?THM?
). However, in positive logic they may not exist.

Example 6.1.4
.

The theory from ?THM?
is also an example of a theory where a type over a p.c. model
M
does not necessarily extend to a global
M
-invariant type. In light of ?THM?
this yields another proof that this theory is not semi-Hausdorff. We use the same notation as in ?THM?
.

We claim that the type
p(x)=\tp(a_{\omega}/M)
does not extend to a global
M
-invariant type. Suppose that there is a global
M
-invariant extension
q(x)\supseteq p(x)
. Then either
q(x)=\tp(a_{\omega}/N)
or
q(x)=\tp(b_{\omega}/N)
, as
N
is the monster model of this theory (see ?THM?
). So since
a_{\omega}\equiv_{M}b_{\omega}
we must have by
M
-invariance that
R(x,a_{\omega})
and
R(x,b_{\omega})
are both in
q(x)
. The only possible realisations of
q(x)
are
a_{\omega}
and
b_{\omega}
, so this contradicts the fact that
T\models\neg\exists xR(x,x)
.

Proposition 6.1.5
.

Assume semi-Hausdorffness. Then any type over any p.c. model
M
extends to a global
M
-invariant type.

Proof.

Let
p(x)=\tp(a/M)
be a type over a p.c. model
M
. Recall that semi-Hausdorffness means that equality of types is type-definable. In particular, there is a partial type
\Omega(xy,xy^{\prime})
over
M
such that
\models\Omega(a^{\prime}b,a^{\prime}b^{\prime})
if and only if
a^{\prime}b\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
for any
a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}
. Let
\Sigma(x)
be the (large) set of formulas

p(x)\cup\bigcup\{\Omega(xb,xb^{\prime}):b,b^{\prime}\text{ finite tuples in the monster with }b\equiv_{M}b^{\prime}\}.

We claim that this set is finitely satisfiable. Indeed, let
\varphi(x,m)\in p(x)
. As
\models\exists x\varphi(x,m)
and
M
is p.c., there must be
a^{\prime}\in M
with
M\models\varphi(a^{\prime},m)
. Now for any
b,b^{\prime}
with
b\equiv_{M}b^{\prime}
we have in particular
a^{\prime}b\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
. So
a^{\prime}
realises

\{\varphi(x,m)\}\cup\bigcup\{\Omega(xb,xb^{\prime}):b,b^{\prime}\text{ finite tuples in the monster with }b\equiv_{M}b^{\prime}\},

which establishes finite realisability of
\Sigma(x)
. By compactness there is a realisation
\alpha
of
\Sigma(x)
. So
\tp(\alpha/\mathfrak{M})
extends
p(x)
and is a global
M
-invariant type
1
1

1

If the reader insists on not allowing a bigger monster model then alternatively Zorn’s lemma can be used to get a maximally consistent set of formulas containing
\Sigma(x)
, which will be the desired global
M
-invariant type extending
p(x)
.
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.6
.

Let
p(x)=\tp(a/B)
be a type and let
C\subseteq B
. Suppose that
p(x)
is
C
-invariant and
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}\subseteq B
is a sequence such that
a_{i}\models p|_{C(a_{j})_{j<i}}
for all
i<\omega
. Then
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
C
-indiscernible.

Proof.

We prove by induction on
n<\omega
that for any
i_{0}<\ldots<i_{n}<\omega
we have
a_{i_{0}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}\equiv_{C}a_{0}\ldots a_{n}
. For
n=0
this is immediate as
a_{i_{0}}
and
a_{0}
both realise
p|_{C}
. Now assume the induction hypothesis for
n
. Then by
C
-invariance and the fact that
a_{i_{n+1}}
and
a_{n+1}
both realise
p|_{Ca_{0}\ldots a_{n}}
we have that

a_{i_{0}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}a_{i_{n+1}}\equiv_{C}a_{0}\ldots a_{n}a_{i_{n+1}}\equiv_{C}a_{0}\ldots a_{n}a_{n+1},

as required.
∎

Proposition 6.1.7
.

Suppose that a type
p(x)=\tp(a/B)
extends to a global
B
-invariant type. Then
a^{\prime}\equiv_{B}a
implies
\operatorname{d}_{B}(a^{\prime},a)\leq 2
.

Proof.

By assumption there is a global
B
-invariant
q(x)\supseteq p(x)
. Inductively construct
(a_{i})_{1\leq i<\omega}
such that
a_{i}\models q|_{Maa^{\prime}(a_{j})_{j<i}}
. Then by ?THM?
we have that the sequences
a,a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
and
a^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
are both
B
-indiscernible, and so
\operatorname{d}_{B}(a,a^{\prime})\leq 2
.
∎

We now obtain as a corollary what was already claimed in ?THM?
.

Corollary 6.1.8
.

Assume semi-Hausdorffness. Then for any p.c. model
M
we have that
a\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}
implies
\operatorname{d}_{M}(a,a^{\prime})\leq 2
.

Proof.

Combine ?THM?
and ?THM?
.
∎

Invariant types are non-dividing types, in the following sense.

Lemma 6.1.9
.

Suppose that
p(x)=\tp(a/M)
is a
C
-invariant type and
M\supseteq C
is a positively
(\aleph_{0}+|C|)^{+}
-saturated p.c. model. Then
p(x)
does not divide over
C
. In particular, any global
C
-invariant type does not divide over
C
.

Proof.

By
finite character
of dividing it is enough to show that for every finite
b\in M
, the type
p^{\prime}(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
. Let
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a
C
-indiscernible sequence with
b_{i}\equiv_{C}b
for all
i<\omega
. By saturation of
M
we find
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\subseteq M
such that
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{C}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
. For each
i<\omega
we have
b^{\prime}_{i}\equiv_{C}b
and so by
C
-invariance
ab^{\prime}_{i}\equiv_{C}ab
. Hence
\models p^{\prime}(a,b^{\prime}_{i})
for all
i<\omega
, and so
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p^{\prime}(x,b^{\prime}_{i})
is realised by
a
. We conclude that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p^{\prime}(x,b_{i})
is consistent, as required.
∎

6.2
Stability

Definition 6.2.1
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal. A theory
T
is called
\lambda
-stable
if for all parameter sets
B
with
|B|\leq\lambda
and all finite index sets
I
we have
|\operatorname{S}_{I}(B)|\leq\lambda
. We call
T

stable
if it is
\lambda
-stable for some
\lambda
.

If
T
is single-sorted then
\lambda
-stability is simply saying that
|\operatorname{S}_{n}(B)|\leq\lambda
for all
n<\omega
and all
B
with
|B|\leq\lambda
.

The following is a more practical condition to check.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal. A theory
T
is
\lambda
-stable if and only if for all
B
with
|B|\leq\lambda
and all singleton index sets
I
we have
|\operatorname{S}_{I}(B)|\leq\lambda
.

If
T
is single-sorted then the above simplifies to:
T
is
\lambda
-stable if and only if for all
B
with
|B|\leq\lambda
we have
|\operatorname{S}_{1}(B)|\leq\lambda
. For notational convenience, we give the proof for the single-sorted case. It should be clear that the same proof works in the multi-sorted case, and the only difference is purely notational.

Proof.

The variables in this proof are all single variables. The left to right direction is trivial. For the converse we prove by induction on
n<\omega
that
|\operatorname{S}_{n}(B)|\leq\lambda
for all
B
with
|B|\leq\lambda
. The base case
n=0
follows because there is only one type in no variables over
B
: the set of formulas over
B
with no variables that hold in the monster model.

For the successor step we assume that the induction hypothesis holds for types in at most
n
variables. Let
B
be a parameter set with
|B|\leq\lambda
. By the induction hypothesis there are at most
\lambda
many types in
n
variables over
B
. Enumerate those types as
(p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))_{i<\lambda}
. For each
i<\lambda
let
b_{i}
be a realisation of
p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. So each
b_{i}
is a finite tuple, hence
B^{\prime}=B\cup\bigcup_{i<\lambda}b_{i}
has cardinality at most
\lambda
. By assumption, there are at most
\lambda
many types in one variable over
B^{\prime}
. Enumerate those types as
(q_{i}(x))_{i<\lambda}
. Set
P=\{p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):i<\lambda\}
and
Q=\{q_{i}(x):i<\lambda\}
. We will define a surjection
f:P\times Q\to\operatorname{S}_{n+1}(B)
, from which
|\operatorname{S}_{n+1}(B)|\leq\lambda
follows, thus concluding the induction and hence the proof.

Let
(p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),q_{j}(x))\in P\times Q
. Recall that
q_{j}|_{Bb_{i}}(x)
is the restriction of
q_{j}(x)
to the parameters
Bb_{i}
. Let
q(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be the result of replacing the tuple
b_{i}
in
q_{j}|_{Bb_{i}}(x)
by the tuple of variables
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Then
q(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is the type over
B
such that
q(x,b_{i})=q_{j}|_{Bb_{i}}(x)
. We let
f
send
(p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),q_{j}(x))
to
q(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

Having defined
f
, we now show that it is a surjection. Let
q(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be any type over
B
in
n+1
variables. Let
p(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be the restriction of
q(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. Then
p(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a type over
B
and so there is
i<\lambda
such that
p(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. There is then also
j<\lambda
such that
q(x,b_{i})=q_{j}(x)
. By construction
f
sends
(p_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),q_{j}(x))
to
q(x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, and we conclude that
f
is surjective.
∎

Proposition 6.2.3
.

Any theory with a maximal p.c. model is stable.

Proof.

Let
M
be the maximal p.c. model. We claim that
T
is
|M|+\aleph_{0}
-stable. By ?THM?
we have that
M
is the monster model. There are at most
|M|+\aleph_{0}
many distinct finite tuples in
M
. So for any set
B
there are at most
|M|+\aleph_{0}
many types over
B
in finitely many free variables, since each of these types is realised in
M
.
∎

Definition 6.2.4
.

Let
p(x)
be a type over
B
and let
\varphi(x,y)
be a formula without parameters. A
\varphi
-definition over
C
of
p(x)
is a set of formulas
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
over
C
with
|\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)|\leq|T|
such that for all
b\in B
we have

\varphi(x,b)\in p(x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(b).

We say that
p(x)
is
definable over
C
if it has a
\varphi
-definition over
C
for every formula
\varphi(x,y)
. If
p(x)
is definable over
B
then we just say that
p(x)
is
definable
.

In full first-order logic the
\varphi
-definition
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
is only a formula, not a set of formulas. Even after this relaxation of the definition of a definable type to allow for sets of formulas, we can still prove many of the important results that we know from full first-order logic. For example, that a theory is stable if and only if every type is definable (see ?THM?
). At the same time it is really necessary to relax the definition, see ?THM?
.

Example 6.2.5
.

We give an example of a stable theory
T
, a type
p(x)
and a formula
\varphi(x,y)
such that the
\varphi
-definition
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
has to be an infinite set of formulas and can thus not be given by a single formula.

We recall the theory from ?THM?
. Our language
\mathcal{L}
has a constant symbol for each element of
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:0<q<1\}
, as well as an order symbol
\leq
. The theory
T
is the set of h-inductive sentences true in
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}
, viewed as an obvious
\mathcal{L}
-structure. There is a maximal model given by the real unit interval
[0,1]
. So
T
is stable by ?THM?
.

We claim that for
p(x)=\tp(\sqrt{2}/\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)})
and
\varphi(x,y)
the formula
x\leq y
the
\varphi
-definition
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
cannot be finite. Using ?THM?
it is straightforward to see that
T
has positive quantifier elimination. So if
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
were to be a single formula then it would be a finite union of closed intervals with rational endpoints. At the same time we have
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi([0,1])=[\sqrt{2},1]
, which cannot be written as such a finite union. We conclude that
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
cannot be finite.

The
\varphi
-definition that is constructed in ?THM?
is rather abstract. Just to give some extra intuition we give an explicit construction for the above
p(x)
and
\varphi(x,y)
. Let
(q_{i})_{i<\omega}
be a sequence in
\mathbb{Q}_{(0,1)}
that approaches
\sqrt{2}
from below. Then we can take

\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)=\{q_{i}\leq y:i<\omega\},

and we clearly have
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi([0,1])=[\sqrt{2},1]
, as required.

Definition 6.2.6
.

For contradictory formulas
\varphi(x,y)
and
\psi(x,y)
we define the
(\varphi,\psi)
-rank

R_{\varphi,\psi}(-)
as follows. Its input is a set of formulas, possibly with parameters, in free variables
x
. Then
R_{\varphi,\psi}(-)
is the least function into the ordinals (augmented by
-1
and a “biggest ordinal”
\infty
) such that:

•

R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq 0
if
\Sigma(x)
is consistent;

•

R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq\alpha+1
if there is some
b
such that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\varphi(x,b)\})\geq\alpha
and
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\psi(x,b)\})\geq\alpha
;

•

R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq\ell
if
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq\alpha
for all
\alpha<\ell
, where
\ell
is a limit ordinal.

So in the above definition we have
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)=-1
if and only if
\Sigma(x)
is inconsistent, while
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)=\infty
means that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq\alpha
for all ordinals
\alpha
.

Lemma 6.2.7
.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
and
\psi(x,y)
be contradictory formulas.

(i)

If
\Sigma(x)
implies
\Sigma^{\prime}(x)
then
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\leq R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma^{\prime})
.

(ii)

The property
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq n
is type-definable by

\exists(y_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}\left(\bigwedge_{\sigma\in 2^{n}}\exists x\left(\Sigma(x)\wedge\bigwedge_{k<n}\chi_{\sigma(k)}(x,y_{\sigma|_{k}})\right)\right),

where
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
are
\varphi
and
\psi
respectively. In particular, if
\Sigma
is finite (i.e. a formula), then this is just a formula.

Note that ?THM?
(ii) makes sense, even for infinite
\Sigma
, by ?THM?
. What this is really saying is that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq n
is witnessed by a binary tree of parameters, represented by the variables
(y_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}
. For each branch we then form a set of formulas as follows. At every node along the branch, we pick either
\varphi
or
\psi
based on how the branch continues, and we plug in the variable corresponding to that node. We then require this set of formulas to be consistent, for every branch. This is depicted in figure
6.1
for
n=3
.

y_{\emptyset}
y_{0}
y_{1}
y_{01}
y_{00}
y_{11}
y_{10}
\Sigma(x)\land\varphi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\varphi(x,y_{0})\land\varphi(x,y_{00})
\Sigma(x)\land\varphi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\varphi(x,y_{0})\land\psi(x,y_{00})
\Sigma(x)\land\varphi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\psi(x,y_{0})\land\varphi(x,y_{01})
\Sigma(x)\land\varphi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\psi(x,y_{0})\land\psi(x,y_{01})
\Sigma(x)\land\psi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\varphi(x,y_{1})\land\varphi(x,y_{10})
\Sigma(x)\land\psi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\varphi(x,y_{1})\land\psi(x,y_{10})
\Sigma(x)\land\psi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\psi(x,y_{1})\land\varphi(x,y_{11})
\Sigma(x)\land\psi(x,y_{\emptyset})\land\psi(x,y_{1})\land\psi(x,y_{11})

Figure 6.1:
Picturing
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq 3
as a binary tree.

Proof.

We prove (i) by induction: if
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq\alpha
then
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma^{\prime})\geq\alpha
. The base step and limit step are trivial. Now let
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq\alpha+1
, so there is
b
with
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\varphi(x,b)\})\geq\alpha
and
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\psi(x,b)\})\geq\alpha
. As
\Sigma(x)\cup\{\varphi(x,b)\}
implies
\Sigma^{\prime}(x)\cup\{\varphi(x,b)\}
we have by the induction hypothesis that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\{\varphi(x,b)\})\geq\alpha
. Similarly we get
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\{\psi(x,b)\})\geq\alpha
. We conclude that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma^{\prime})\geq\alpha+1
, as required.

For (ii) we first prove that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq n
implies the given set of formulas, by induction on
n
. For
n=0
the type just says
\exists x\Sigma(x)
. Now if
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq n+1
then there is
b
such that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\varphi(x,b)\})\geq n
and
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\psi(x,b)\})\geq n
. By the induction hypothesis we then find trees of parameters
(b^{\prime}_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}
and
(b^{\prime\prime}_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}
. Then we define a new tree of parameters
(b_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n+1}}
by making
b
the root, so
b_{\emptyset}=b
. We then set
b_{0^{\smallfrown}\eta}=b^{\prime}_{\eta}
and
b_{1^{\smallfrown}\eta}=b^{\prime\prime}_{\eta}
for all
\eta\in 2^{<n}
. Now
(b_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n+1}}
is the required realisation of
(y_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n+1}}
.

For the converse of (ii) we again proceed by induction on
n
. The base case is trivial. For the induction step, let
(b_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n+1}}
realise
(y_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n+1}}
. Then by the induction hypothesis
(b_{0^{\smallfrown}\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}
witnesses
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\varphi(x,b_{\emptyset})\})\geq n
, as it realises
(y_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}
. Similarly
(b_{1^{\smallfrown}\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<n}}
witnesses
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma\cup\{\psi(x,b_{\emptyset})\})\geq n
. So we conclude that indeed
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\Sigma)\geq n+1
.
∎

Theorem 6.2.8
.

The following are equivalent for a theory
T
:

(i)

T
is stable,

(ii)

R_{\varphi,\psi}(x=x)<\omega
for all contradictory formulas
\varphi(x,y)
and
\psi(x,y)
,

(iii)

every type is definable,

(iv)

T
is
\lambda
-stable for every
\lambda
with
\lambda^{|T|}=\lambda
.

Proof.

We prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv), and (iv)
\Rightarrow
(i) is immediate as there is at least one such
\lambda
(e.g.,
\lambda=2^{|T|}
).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
We prove the contrapositive. So let
\varphi(x,y)
and
\psi(x,y)
be contradictory formulas such that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(x=x)\geq\omega
. Let
\lambda
be any cardinal, we will prove that
T
is not
\lambda
-stable. Let
\mu
be minimal such that
2^{\mu}>\lambda
. Then
|2^{<\mu}|\leq\lambda
. Write
\chi_{0}
and
\chi_{1}
for
\varphi
and
\psi
respectively. Following ?THM?
(ii), we can use compactness to find
(b_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<\mu}}
such that for all
\sigma\in 2^{\mu}
set

\Sigma_{\sigma}(x)=\{\chi_{\sigma(i)}(x,b_{\sigma|_{i}}):i<\mu\}

is consistent. For each such
\sigma
we thus find a type
p_{\sigma}(x)\supseteq\Sigma_{\sigma}(x)
over
B=(b_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<\mu}}
. By construction
p_{\sigma}\neq p_{\sigma^{\prime}}
whenever
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
. We conclude that there are more than
2^{\mu}>\lambda
types over
B
in variables
x
(and
x
is a finite tuple). At the same time,
|B|\leq\lambda
, and so
T
is not
\lambda
-stable.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
Let
p(x)
be a type over some parameter set
B
and let
\varphi(x,y)
be any formula. We will show that there is a
\varphi
-definition of
p(x)
over
B
. Let
\psi(x,y)
be an obstruction of
\varphi(x,y)
. By ?THM?
(i) we have
R_{\varphi,\psi}(p)\leq R_{\varphi,\psi}(x=x)<\omega
, and so there is
n_{\psi}<\omega
such that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(p)=n_{\psi}
. In particular,
R_{\varphi,\psi}(p)\not\geq n_{\psi}+1
. So the corresponding set of formulas in ?THM?
(ii) is inconsistent and by compactness we find
\chi_{\psi}(x)\in p(x)
such that
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\chi_{\psi})\not\geq n_{\psi}+1
. Again, using ?THM?
(ii), we let
\theta_{\psi}(y)
be a formula equivalent to
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\chi_{\psi}(x)\wedge\varphi(x,y))\geq n_{\psi}
. Note that this formula does indeed have a free variable
y
and it has the same parameters as
\chi_{\psi}
. Set
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)=\{\theta_{\psi}(y):\psi\text{ is an obstruction of }\varphi\}
, so clearly
|\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)|\leq|T|
and it only has parameters in
B
.

We verify that
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
is indeed a
\varphi
-definition of
p(x)
. First assume
\varphi(x,b)\in p(x)
. Let
\psi
be an obstruction of
\varphi
. We have
\chi_{\psi}(x)\wedge\varphi(x,b)\in p(x)
, so
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\chi_{\psi}(x)\wedge\varphi(x,b))\geq R_{\varphi,\psi}(p)=n_{\psi}
and thus
\models\theta_{\psi}(b)
. As
\psi
was an arbitrary obstruction we have
\models\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(b)
. We prove the contrapositive of the converse. So assume that
\varphi(x,b)\not\in p(x)
. Then there must be some
\psi(x,b)\in p(x)
, such that
\psi(x,y)
is an obstruction of
\varphi(x,y)
. So we have
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\chi_{\psi}\wedge\psi(x,b))\geq R_{\varphi,\psi}(p)=n_{\psi}
. We must thus have
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\chi_{\psi}\wedge\varphi(x,b))<n_{\psi}
as otherwise
R_{\varphi,\psi}(\chi_{\psi})\geq n_{\psi}+1
. Hence we have
\not\models\theta_{\psi}(b)
and thus
\not\models\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(b)
, as required.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
Let
\lambda
be such that
\lambda^{|T|}=\lambda
, and note that this implies
\lambda>|T|
. We will prove that
T
is
\lambda
-stable. Let
B
be any set of parameters with
|B|\leq\lambda
. There are at most
|B|+|T|
many formulas over
B
and so there are at most
(|B|+|T|)^{|T|}\leq\lambda^{|T|}=\lambda
many sets of formulas of cardinality
\leq|T|
over
B
. By assumption, every type
p(x)
over
B
is definable and is thus fully determined by its
\varphi
-definitions, where
\varphi(x,y)
ranges over all formulas. As there are at most
\lambda
many different possibilities for
\varphi
-definitions, we have that there are at most
|T|\times\lambda=\lambda
many types over
B
, as required.
∎

Lemma 6.2.9
.

Let
p(x)
be a type over
B
and suppose that
p(x)
is definable over
C\subseteq B
. Then
p(x)
is
C
-invariant.

Proof.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
be any formula without parameters and let
b,b^{\prime}\in B
with
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
. By assumption there is a
\varphi
-definition
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
of
p(x)
over
C
. Then

\varphi(x,b)\in p(x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(b)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(b^{\prime})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\varphi(x,b^{\prime})\in p(x),

where the middle equivalence follows from
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 6.2.10
.

Let
M\supseteq C
be a positively
(\aleph_{0}+|C|)^{+}
-saturated p.c. model. Suppose that
p(x)
is a type over
M
that is definable over
C
. Then
p(x)
does not divide over
C
.

Proof.

Combine ?THM?s
and
.
∎

Lemma 6.2.11
.

Suppose that
T
is not simple. Then there is a chain
(M_{i})_{i<|T|^{+}}
of positively
|T|^{+}
-saturated p.c. models and some
a
such that, for
M=\bigcup_{i<|T|^{+}}M_{i}
, the type
\tp(a/M)
divides over
M_{i}
for all
i<|T|^{+}
.

Proof.

The construction is the same as in the proof of (iii)
\Rightarrow
(v) in ?THM?
, with a bit more care taken to end up with the desired chain of models.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
have
\mathsf{TP}
as witnessed by
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
and
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
. Let
\lambda=\beth_{|T|^{+}}
and, using compactness, enlarge our tree to
(c_{\eta})_{\eta\in(\lambda^{+})^{<|T|^{+}}}
.

We construct some
\sigma\in(\lambda^{+})^{<|T|^{+}}
by induction on its length, at the same time as a chain
(M_{i})_{i<|T|^{+}}
of positively
|T|^{+}
-saturated p.c. models, such that for each
\gamma<|T|^{+}
we have:

(1)

\{c_{\sigma|_{i}}:i<\gamma\}\subseteq M_{\gamma}
,

(2)

|M_{\gamma}|<\lambda
,

(3)

there is an infinite
I_{\gamma}\subseteq(\lambda^{+})
such that
\{c_{{\sigma|_{\gamma}}^{\frown}i}:i\in I_{\gamma}\}
all have the same type over
M_{\gamma}
and
\sigma(\gamma)\in I_{\gamma}
.

Suppose that we have constructed
\sigma|_{\gamma}
and
(M_{i})_{i<\gamma}
, for
\gamma<|T|^{+}
. Let
M_{\gamma}
be any positively
|T|^{+}
-saturated p.c. model of cardinality
<\lambda
containing
\{c_{\sigma|_{i}}:i<\gamma\}\cup\bigcup_{i<\gamma}M_{i}
. As
|\{c_{\sigma|_{i}}:i<\gamma\}\cup\bigcup_{i<\gamma}M_{i}|<\lambda
by the induction hypothesis, such an
M_{\gamma}
exists by ?THM?
. There are at most
2^{|M_{\gamma}|}<\lambda
many types over
M_{\gamma}
. So there is infinite
