I_{\gamma}\subseteq\lambda^{+}
as in property (3). We finish the construction by letting
\sigma(\gamma)
be any element (say, the least one) in
I_{\gamma}
.

Let
a
be a realisation of
\{\varphi(x,\sigma|_{i}):i<|T|^{+}\}
, and set
M=\bigcup_{i<|T|^{+}}M_{i}
. Fix
\gamma<|T|^{+}
, it remains to prove that
\tp(a/M)
divides over
M_{\gamma}
. Let
I_{\gamma}
be as in (3) of the induction hypothesis. Then
c_{\sigma|_{\gamma+1}}\in\{c_{{\sigma|_{\gamma}}^{\frown}i}:i\in I_{\gamma}\}
, and so
c_{{\sigma|_{\gamma}}^{\frown}i}\equiv_{M_{\gamma}}c_{\sigma|_{\gamma+1}}
for all
i\in I_{\gamma}
. At the same time, by definition of
\mathsf{TP}
, we have that
\psi
holds along
(c_{{\sigma|_{\gamma}}^{\frown}i})_{i\in I_{\gamma}}
. Therefore,
\varphi(x,c_{\sigma|_{\gamma+1}})

\psi
-divides over
M_{\gamma}
. As
c_{\sigma|_{\gamma+1}}\in M_{\gamma+1}\subseteq M
we have by choice of
a
that
\varphi(x,c_{\sigma|_{\gamma+1}})\in\tp(a/M)
, and we conclude that
\tp(a/M)
divides over
M_{\gamma}
.
∎

Theorem 6.2.12
.

Every stable theory is simple.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
T
is stable and not simple. Let
a
and
(M_{i})_{i<|T|^{+}}
be as in ?THM?
. Set
M=\bigcup_{i<|T|^{+}}M_{i}
. By ?THM?

p(x)=\tp(a/M)
is definable. So for each formula
\varphi(x,y)
there is a
\varphi
-definition
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
of
p(x)
over
M
. Let
C
be the union of all parameters mentioned in
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
, as
\varphi(x,y)
ranges over all possible formulas. Then
C\subseteq M
and
|C|\leq|T|
as
|\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)|\leq|T|
for all
\varphi(x,y)
. So there is
i<|T|^{+}
such that
C\subseteq M_{i}
. As
\tp(a/M)
divides over
M_{i}
, we have by finite character of dividing that there is some
i<j<|T|^{+}
such that
\tp(a/M_{j})
divides over
M_{i}
. In particular, by
base monotonicity
of dividing, we have that
\tp(a/M_{j})
divides over
C
. At the same time,
\tp(a/M_{j})
is by construction definable over
C
, and so ?THM?
implies that it does not divide over
C
. We have arrived at our contradiction and conclude that
T
must be simple.
∎

6.3
Stationarity

Definition 6.3.1
.

A
stationary type
is a type
p(x)=\tp(a/C)
that admits exactly one non-dividing extension to any parameter set. That is, for any
B\supseteq C
, there is a type
p^{\prime}(x)\supseteq p(x)
over
B
such that:

(i)

p^{\prime}(x)
does not divide over
C
;

(ii)

for any type
r(x)\supseteq p(x)
over
B
that does not divide over
C
we have
r(x)=p^{\prime}(x)
.

We can reformulate the above definition in terms of the
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
notation. A type
p(x)=\tp(a/C)
is stationary if for any
B
there is some
a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}\equiv_{C}a
and
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}B
. Furthermore, for any
B
and any
a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime}\equiv_{C}a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{C}a
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}B
and
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}B
we have that
a^{\prime}\equiv_{CB}a^{\prime\prime}
.

Theorem 6.3.2
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is a stable theory then
\tp(a/C)
is stationary if and only if we have for all
a^{\prime}
that
a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}
implies
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
. In particular,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
stationarity
in stable theories.

Proof.

First we note that
T
is simple, by ?THM?
. So we can, and will, use the properties for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
summarised in ?THM?
. That being said, the left to right direction actually goes through for any theory
T
.

We first prove the left to right direction, so assume that
\tp(a/C)
is stationary. Let
q(x)\supseteq p(x)
be a global non-dividing extension and let
\alpha
be a realisation of
q(x)
. Let
b,b^{\prime}
be any two tuples with
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
and let
\alpha^{*}
be such that
\alpha^{*}b\equiv_{C}\alpha b^{\prime}
. As
q
does not divide over
C
we have that
\alpha\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}bb^{\prime}
. Hence
\alpha\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
and
\alpha^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
. So by stationarity we have that
\alpha\equiv_{Cb}\alpha^{*}
, and thus

\alpha b\equiv_{C}\alpha^{*}b\equiv_{C}\alpha b^{\prime}.

As
b
and
b^{\prime}
were arbitrary with
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
, we see that
q(x)
is global
C
-invariant. So
p(x)
extends to a global
C
-invariant type, and we conclude by ?THM?
.

We prove the contrapositive of the right to left direction. So suppose that there is a non-stationary type
p(x)=\tp(a/C)
such that
a^{\prime}\models p
implies
a^{\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a
. As
p(x)
is not stationary and every type has non-dividing extensions in a thick simple theory (?THM?
), there must be two distinct non-dividing extensions of
p(x)
. That is, there is
b
and distinct extensions
p_{0}(x)=\tp(a_{0}/Cb)
and
p_{1}(x)=\tp(a_{1}/Cb)
of
p(x)
, so that both
p_{0}(x)
and
p_{1}(x)
do not divide over
C
. We may assume that
b
is finite.

By ?THM?
(iv) it is enough to prove that
T
is not
\lambda
-stable for
\lambda=(|C|+2)^{|T|}
, as this satisfies
\lambda^{|T|}=\lambda
. Let
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
be a Morley sequence over
C
with
b_{0}=b
, which exists by simplicity (?THM?
). We will construct types
(q_{\eta}(x))_{\eta\in 2^{\leq\lambda}}
by induction on the length of
\eta
, such that for
\eta\in 2^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\leq\lambda
:

(1)

for all
\nu\unlhd\eta
we have
q_{\nu}(x)\subseteq q_{\eta}(x)
,

(2)

q_{\eta}(x)
is a type over
C(b_{i})_{i<\gamma}
,

(3)

q_{\eta}(x)\supseteq p_{\eta(i)}(x,b_{i})
for all
i<\gamma
,

(4)

q_{\eta}(x)
does not divide over
C
.

For the base case,
\gamma=0
, we simply set
q_{\emptyset}(x)=p(x)
. Now assume that we have constructed
(q_{\eta}(x))_{\eta\in 2^{<\gamma}}
. If
\gamma
is a limit then for
\eta\in 2^{\gamma}
we set
q_{\eta}(x)=\bigcup_{i<\gamma}q_{\eta|_{i}}(x)
. Then (1)–(3) are immediate, and (4) follows from
finite character
of dividing independence.

This leaves the successor step. So suppose that
\gamma=\delta+1
and let
\eta\in 2^{\gamma}
. As
b_{\delta}\equiv_{C}b_{0}=b
, we find
a^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}b_{\delta}\equiv_{C}a_{\eta(\delta)}b
. Let
a^{\prime\prime}
be a realisation of
q_{\eta|_{\delta}}
. Then
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}(b_{i})_{i<\delta}
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b_{\delta}
and
b_{\delta}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}(b_{i})_{i<\delta}
. The first independence is (4) from the induction hypothesis, the second follows from our choice of
a^{\prime}
and the fact that
p_{\eta(\delta)}(x)
does not divide over
C
and the third is immediate from
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
being a Morley sequence over
C
. Furthermore, we have
a^{\prime}\equiv_{C}a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime\prime}
, where the second equivalence follows because
p(x)=q_{\emptyset}(x)\subseteq q_{\eta|_{\delta}}(x)
. So by assumption
a^{\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime\prime}
. We can thus apply the
independence theorem
for dividing independence to find
a^{*}
with
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}(b_{i})_{i<\gamma}
,
a^{*}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cb_{\delta}}a^{\prime}
and
a^{*}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C(b_{i})_{i<\delta}}a^{\prime\prime}
. We set
q_{\eta}(x)=\tp(a^{*}/C(b_{i})_{i<\gamma})
, which immediately takes care of property (2). Properties (4), (3) and (1) follow from the respective properties that the
independence theorem
gives for
a^{*}
(as well as the induction hypothesis).

This finishes the construction. Now for any distinct
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in 2^{\lambda}
, property (3) of the inductive construction guarantees that
q_{\eta}(x)\neq q_{\eta^{\prime}}(x)
. So
\{q_{\eta}(x):\eta\in 2^{\lambda}\}
is a set of
2^{\lambda}
distinct types over
C(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
, while
|C(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}|\leq\lambda
. Therefore,
T
is not
\lambda
-stable.
∎

Corollary 6.3.3
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is stable then any type over any positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model is stationary. If we assume semi-Hausdorffness then any type over any p.c. model is stationary.

Proof.

By ?THM?
having the same type over a positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model
M
implies having the same Lascar strong type over
M
, and so the result follows from ?THM?
. If we assume semi-Hausdorffness then we can drop the saturatedness assumption in ?THM?
and hence in this result.
∎

Example 6.3.4
.

The assumption in ?THM?
that the p.c. model is positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated is necessary. Consider the theory from ?THM?
. We will use the same notation. As that theory has a maximal model
N
(which is thus the monster model, see ?THM?
), the theory is clearly
|N|
-stable. Furthermore, it is thick. However, the type
p(x)=\tp(a_{\omega}/M)
is not stationary. As
N
is a maximal model, all indiscernible sequences are constant sequences and so no type divides (over any base set). In particular,
p_{1}(x)=\tp(a_{\omega}/N)
and
p_{2}(x)=\tp(b_{\omega}/N)
are two distinct non-dividing extensions of
p(x)
, showing that
p(x)
is not stationary (cf. ?THM?
).

Theorem 6.3.5
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is simple then the following are equivalent:

(i)

T
is stable;

(ii)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
stationarity
;

(iii)

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
stationarity
over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models: for any positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model
M
and any
a,a^{\prime},b
with
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{M}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{M}b
and
a\equiv_{M}a^{\prime}
we have
a\equiv_{Mb}a^{\prime}
.

The proof of ?THM?
rests mainly on the following notion and lemma, that allows us to reduce checking
\lambda
-stability from types over arbitrary parameter sets to types over certain p.c. models.

Definition 6.3.6
.

Fix an infinite cardinal
\lambda
. A
\lambda
-directed poset
is a poset
I
such that any
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
|I^{\prime}|<\lambda
has an upper bound in
I
. A
\lambda
-directed system
is a functor from a
\lambda
-directed poset
I
into the category of
\mathcal{L}
-structures.

Note that this generalises ?THM?
to higher cardinals in the sense that a directed poset/system is just an
\omega
-directed poset/system. This then immediately gives rise to the notion of
union of a
\lambda
-directed system
or
\lambda
-directed union
as in that definition, because every
\lambda
-directed poset is in particular directed. However, we will only be interested in the case where all structures in the system are p.c. models and live in the monster model, resulting in the following simplified definition.

Definition 6.3.7
.

Let
I
be a
\lambda
-directed poset and let
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of p.c. submodels of the monster model, such that for all
i\leq j
in
I
we have
M_{i}\subseteq M_{j}
. We then say that
M=\bigcup_{i\in I}M_{i}
is the
\lambda
-directed union
of the
\lambda
-directed system
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
.

Note that in the above definition
M
is in particular a p.c. model, because it is the directed union of a directed system.

Lemma 6.3.8
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal with
\lambda\geq|T|
, and let
\kappa
be any cardinal such that
\kappa^{<\lambda}=\kappa
and
\kappa\geq 2^{\lambda}
. Then any parameter set
B
with
|B|\leq\kappa
is contained in a p.c. model
M
that is a
\lambda
-directed union of positively
\lambda
-saturated p.c. models, each of cardinality
\leq 2^{\lambda}
, such that
|M|\leq\kappa
.

We also note that
M
in the above lemma is also positively
\lambda
-saturated. This follows from the general fact that the
\lambda
-directed union of a system of positively
\lambda
-saturated models is itself always positively
\lambda
-saturated. However, we will have no further use for these facts.

Proof.

For
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
with
|B^{\prime}|<\lambda
we let
M_{B^{\prime}}
be some positively
\lambda
-saturated p.c. model containing
B^{\prime}
with
|M_{B^{\prime}}|\leq 2^{\lambda}
. This exists by ?THM?
. Let
[B]^{<\lambda}
be the set of subsets of
B
of cardinality
<\lambda
. Then
[B]^{<\lambda}
is a
\lambda
-directed poset, ordered by inclusion. Thus
M=\bigcup_{B^{\prime}\in[B]^{<\lambda}}M_{B^{\prime}}
is a
\lambda
-directed union. It remains to check that
M
satisfies the required cardinality bound. Indeed,
|[B]^{<\lambda}|\leq\kappa^{<\lambda}=\kappa
and so
|M|\leq\kappa\times 2^{\lambda}=\kappa
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.9
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal with
\lambda\geq|T|
, and let
\kappa
be any cardinal such that
\kappa^{<\lambda}=\kappa
and
\kappa\geq 2^{\lambda}
. Suppose that for every p.c. model
M
, which is a
\lambda
-directed union of positively
\lambda
-saturated p.c. models of cardinality
\leq 2^{\lambda}
and with
|M|\leq\kappa
, we have that
|\operatorname{S}_{I}(M)|\leq\kappa
for all finite index sets
I
. Then
T
is
\kappa
-stable.

Proof.

Let
B
be any parameter set of cardinality at most
\kappa
. By ?THM?
there is a p.c. model
M
containing
B
that satisfies the description in the statement. Therefore
|\operatorname{S}_{I}(B)|\leq|\operatorname{S}_{I}(M)|\leq\kappa
, and we conclude that
T
is
\kappa
-stable.
∎

Proof of ?THM?
.

The implications (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) are ?THM?
and ?THM?
respectively. So we prove (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i).

Let
\kappa=2^{2^{\lambda_{T}}}
, then
\kappa^{<\lambda_{T}}=\kappa
and
\kappa\geq 2^{\lambda_{T}}
. So we can, and will, use ?THM?
to prove that
T
is
\kappa
-stable. So let
M
be a p.c. model of cardinality
\leq\kappa
that is a
\lambda_{T}
-directed union of positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models, each of cardinality
\leq 2^{\lambda_{T}}
. Let
(M_{j})_{j\in J}
be this
\lambda_{T}
-directed system. For any type
p(x)\in\operatorname{S}_{I}(M)
, where
I
is a finite index set, there is by
local character
some
C\subseteq M
with
|C|\leq|T|
such that
p(x)
does not divide over
C
. For each
c\in C
there is
j_{c}\in J
such that
c\in M_{j_{c}}
. As
|C|\leq|T|<\lambda_{T}
, there is an upper bound
j_{p}\in J
of
\{j_{c}:c\in C\}
, and so
C\subseteq M_{j_{p}}
. By
base monotonicity
we have that
p(x)
does not divide over
M_{j_{p}}
. As
M_{j_{p}}
is a positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model, we have by the stationarity assumption that
p(x)
is completely determined by its restriction to
M_{j_{p}}
. We have thus shown that the following assignment is an injection:

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{I}(M)

\displaystyle\to\coprod_{j\in J}\operatorname{S}_{I}(M_{j}),

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\mapsto(j_{p},p|_{M_{j_{p}}}).

As
|M_{j}|\leq 2^{\lambda_{T}}
for all
j\in J
, we have that
|J|\leq|M|^{2^{\lambda_{T}}}\leq\kappa^{2^{\lambda_{T}}}=\kappa
, as well as
|\operatorname{S}_{I}(M_{j})|\leq 2^{|M_{j}|}\leq 2^{2^{\lambda_{T}}}=\kappa
. We thus conclude that the above disjoint union has cardinality at most
\kappa
and so
|\operatorname{S}_{I}(M)|\leq\kappa
, as required.
∎

6.4
Stable independence

Definition 6.4.1
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation and let
C
be a parameter set. We say that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
stationarity over
C
if for all
a,a^{\prime},b
we have that if
a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
and
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then
a\equiv_{Cb}a^{\prime}
.

With this terminology ?THM?
(iii) can be rephrased as “
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies stationarity over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models” and the
stationarity
property becomes stationarity over those sets
C
over which types and Lascar-strong types coincide.

Lemma 6.4.2
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation satisfying
invariance
,
monotonicity
and
extension
. Suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies stationarity over some parameter set
C
. Then for any
a,a^{\prime},b,c
with
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
and
a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}
there is
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a
and
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Cc}a^{\prime}
such that
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bc
.

Proof.

Let
a,a^{\prime},b,c
be as in the statement. By
extension
there is
c^{\prime}
with
c^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}c
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bc^{\prime}
. Let
a^{\prime\prime}
be such that
ac^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a^{\prime\prime}c
. We claim that this is the desired
a^{\prime\prime}
. By
invariance
we indeed have
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bc
, and by construction
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a
. For the final equality of types we apply
monotonicity
to see
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
and together with
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
this implies
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv_{Cc}a^{\prime}
by stationarity over
C
.
∎

Corollary 6.4.3
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation satisfying
invariance
,
monotonicity
and
extension
. If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies stationarity over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models then it satisfies independence theorem over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models.

By “independence theorem over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models” we mean the weakening described in ?THM?
.

Theorem 6.4.4

(Kim-Pillay style characterisation of stable theories)
.

Assume thickness. A theory
T
is stable if and only if there is an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfying
invariance
,
monotonicity
,
normality
,
existence
,
full existence
,
base monotonicity
,
extension
,
symmetry
,
transitivity
,
finite character
,
local character
and
stationarity
. Furthermore, in this case,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
.

Remark 6.4.5
.

Similar to ?THM?
, we can strengthen one direction of ?THM?
. To conclude stability of
T
and that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
we can replace
stationarity
by stationarity over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models.

Proof.

If
T
is stable then it is in particular simple (?THM?
). So from ?THM?
we know that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies all the listed properties, except for
stationarity
, which follows from ?THM?
. Conversely, suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an arbitrary independence relation, satisfying the listed properties (with stationarity only over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models, as per ?THM?
). By ?THM?
,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
independence theorem
over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models, and so following ?THM?
we have that ?THM?
applies. We thus have that
T
is simple and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
. As
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies stationarity over positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models we conclude
T
is stable by ?THM?
.
∎

Remark 6.4.6
.

Note that ?THM?
does not list
independence theorem
, as that has been replaced by the stronger
stationarity
(see ?THM?s
and
). Following ?THM?
we could also have left out
normality
and
full existence
, but as in ?THM?
we chose to include all basic properties.

6.5
Bibliographic remarks

Shelah already proved results for stable theories in positive logic
[
She75
]
. Later, Ben-Yaacov established the connection of stability with simplicity in positive logic
[
BY03b
, Section 2]
, similar to the contents of this chapter. Just like in Chapter
5
, we assume thickness in various places to simplify the treatment.

The reader might be familiar with the definition of stability in terms of the order property. This also works in positive logic, after adjusting the definition of the order property similarly to how we adjusted the definition of the tree property (?THM?
). We chose not to treat the order property, because we have no use for it in these notes and the proof of its equivalence to the other characterisations of stability (?THM?
) is long and technical. Instead, we just state what is true here and give references to
[
DGK23
]
, which is the first place where they appear in print in the modern terminology of positive logic, but is hardly the original source of the arguments.

Definition 6.5.1

(
[
DGK23
, Definition 3.5]
)
.

A formula
\varphi(x,y)
has the
order property
(
\mathsf{OP}
) if there are sequences
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
and
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
and an obstruction
\psi(x,y)
of
\varphi(x,y)
such that for all
i,j<\omega
, we have

\displaystyle\models\varphi(a_{i},b_{j})

\displaystyle\text{if }i<j,

\displaystyle\models\psi(a_{i},b_{j})

\displaystyle\text{if }i\geq j.

In fact, the usual results for local stability go through in positive logic. That is, we can call a formula stable if that particular formula does not have the order property. This then has many equivalent conditions, such as type counting, which are listed in
[
DGK23
, Theorem 3.11]
. Linking this back to our treatment of stability, we have the following.

Theorem 6.5.2
.

A theory
T
is stable if and only if no formula has the order property.

The observation that the theory in ?THM?
contains a type over a p.c. model
M
that does not extend to a global
M
-invariant type is due to Mennuni.

We defined invariant types in Section
6.1
and proved that these exist over p.c. models in semi-Hausdorff theories (?THM?
). One can also define a notion of
Lascar-invariant type
, which can be proved to exist over p.c. models in thick theories. We refer the reader to
[
DK22
, Section 3]
for more details.

Just as was the case for the Kim-Pillay style theorem for simple theories (?THM?
), the stable version (?THM?
) can be pieced together from Ben-Yaacov’s work
[
BY03b
,
BY03c
]
. Though, as before, the addition of the thickness assumption allows for a much simpler statement and easier treatment.

Chapter 7
Examples

We consider two classes of examples that can be studied in the framework of positive logic, but generally not in the framework of full first-order logic.

The first class of examples (Section
7.1
) describes how hyperimaginaries can be added to any positive theory (and so in particular to a full first-order theory) while preserving the important properties of the theory. So this yields an
(-)^{\textup{heq}}
construction, analogous to the
(-)^{\textup{eq}}
construction we know from full first-order logic.

The second class of examples (Section
7.2
) describes how continuous logic can be studied in positive logic. More precisely, it describes how to turn a monster model of a continuous theory into a monster model of a positive theory in such a way that the automorphisms remain the same. In particular, this means that both perspectives agree on types (which correspond to automorphisms orbits). This means that the model theory in both perspectives remains the same, in the sense that both perspectives agree on things such as dividing, stability, simplicity, etc. In fact, because of the explicit description of the construction, one obtains an explicit dictionary to translate model-theoretic definitions and results from positive logic to continuous logic (e.g., ?THM?s
,
, and
).

7.1
Hyperimaginaries

We still work in a monster model, which we recall is denoted by
\mathfrak{M}
, as per ?THM?
. We will extend the monster model with new sorts to a new monster model that contains hyperimaginary elements. To distinguish between these two different structures we will no longer omit them from the notation.

Definition 7.1.1
.

A
type-definable equivalence relation
is a set of formulas
E(x,y)
without parameters, where
x
and
y
are (possibly infinite, but small) tuples of variables, such that
E
defines an equivalence relation in
\mathfrak{M}
.

The idea is that we fix a set of such type-definable equivalence relations and add a new sort for each type-definable equivalence relation in this set. Then we will extend the monster model so that the elements of each of these new sorts are the equivalence classes of the corresponding equivalence relation. To capture the interaction between these equivalence classes and their representatives, we also need to add further symbols to the language. However, to stay within a finitary first-order language we cannot add projection function symbols, as is commonly done in the
(-)^{\textup{eq}}
-construction, because the representatives of these equivalence classes can be infinite. We will thus add relation symbols that capture all the possible finitary interactions between the original sort(s) and the new sorts. We then recover (the graphs of) the projection functions as partial types (see ?THM?
).

Definition 7.1.2
.

Given a set
\mathcal{E}
of type-definable equivalence relations, we define the
hyperimaginary signature

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
as a multisorted extension of
\mathcal{L}
. The sort(s) already in
\mathcal{L}
will be called the
real sort
(s)
. Then for each
E\in\mathcal{E}
, we add a sort
S_{E}
, called a
hyperimaginary sort
. For a hyperimaginary sort
S_{E}
, we write
S_{E,r}
for the tuple of real sorts, matching the sorts of the representatives of the
E
-equivalence classes. For a variable
y
of sort
S_{E}
, we write
y_{r}
for a tuple of variables of sort
S_{E,r}
.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
contains the following relation symbols. Let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
and let
y_{i}
be a variable of sort
S_{E_{i}}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. Let
\varphi(x,y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{n,r})
be an
\mathcal{L}
-formula, and write
S_{x}
for the sort of
x
. Then we add a relation symbol
R_{\varphi}(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
of sort
S_{x}\times S_{E_{1}}\times\ldots\times S_{E_{n}}
.

We note that in the above definition, not all variables in
\varphi(x,y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{n,r})
need to actually appear in the formula. So it is not a problem for the
y_{i,r}
to be infinite tuples. Similarly, when we write something like
\exists y_{r}\varphi(y_{r})
, then we really only quantify over the variables that actually appear in
\varphi
, so this is still a finitary formula. We also point out that
x
can be a tuple of variables, so that
S_{x}
is a tuple of the corresponding real sorts (which is what we mean by “the sort of
x
”).

Definition 7.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a set of type-definable equivalence relations.
We extend
\mathfrak{M}
to an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-structure
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
as follows. For each
E\in\mathcal{E}
the sort
S_{E}
is interpreted as the collection of
E
-equivalence classes in
\mathfrak{M}
, and its elements are called
hyperimaginary element
s
. For
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\in\mathcal{E}
and
\varphi(x,y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{n,r})
, where
y_{i}
is a variable of sort
E_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we interpret the symbol
R_{\varphi}
as follows. We let
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varphi}(a,c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
if and only if there are representatives
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
of
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
respectively such that
\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi(a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
.

We define the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-theory
T^{\mathcal{E}}
to be the set of all h-inductive
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-sentences that are true in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
, where
T
is the theory for which
\mathfrak{M}
is a monster model.

The current setup allows for flexibility in which hyperimaginary sorts are being added. Often one wants to add hyperimaginary sorts for all type-definable equivalence relations. However, we cannot take
\mathcal{E}
to be all type-definable equivalence relations, as that would be large with respect to the monster
\mathfrak{M}
. For example, for every
\lambda
there is the equality relation in variables
(x_{i})_{i<\lambda}
. To solve this, we show that we can effectively restrict to hyperimaginaries of length
\leq|T|
.

Lemma 7.1.4
.

Let
\Sigma(x)
be any set of formulas, and suppose that
\varphi(x)
is such that
\models\Sigma(a)
implies
\models\varphi(a)
. Then there is
\Sigma^{\prime}(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)
with
|\Sigma^{\prime}(x)|\leq|T|
such that
\models\Sigma^{\prime}(a)
implies
\models\varphi(a)
.

Proof.

Let
\psi(x)
be any obstruction of
\varphi(x)
. Then
\Sigma(x)\cup\{\psi(x)\}
is inconsistent, so by compactness there is
\chi_{\psi}(x)\in\Sigma(x)
such that
\chi_{\psi}(x)
is an obstruction of
\psi(x)
. Define

\Sigma^{\prime}(x)=\{\chi_{\psi}(x):\psi(x)\text{ is an obstruction of }\varphi(x)\}.

As
\varphi(x)
has
\leq|T|
obstructions, we see that
|\Sigma^{\prime}(x)|\leq|T|
. Now suppose for a contradiction that
\models\Sigma^{\prime}(a)
and
\not\models\varphi(a)
. Then there is an obstruction
\psi(x)
of
\varphi(x)
such that
\models\psi(a)
. However,
\models\Sigma^{\prime}(a)
implies
\models\chi_{\psi}(a)
, contradicting that
\chi_{\psi}(x)
is an obstruction of
\psi(x)
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.5
.

Let
E(x,y)
be a type-definable equivalence relation and let
\varphi(x,y)\in E(x,y)
. Then there is a type-definable equivalence relation
E_{\varphi}(x,y)
, such that
\varphi(x,y)\in E_{\varphi}(x,y)
,
E_{\varphi}(x,y)\subseteq E(x,y)
and
|E_{\varphi}(x,y)|\leq|T|
.

Proof.

For any
\psi(x,y)\in E(x,y)
we have that
\models E(a,b)\wedge E(b,c)
implies
\models\psi(a,c)
. We can thus apply ?THM?
to
E(x,y)\cup E(y,z)
and
\psi(x,z)
to find
\Sigma^{\prime}(x,y,z)
with
|\Sigma^{\prime}(x,y,z)|\leq|T|
and
\models\Sigma^{\prime}(a,b,c)
implies
\models\psi(a,c)
. Pick
E^{\prime}_{\psi}(x,y)\subseteq E(x,y)
such that
|E^{\prime}_{\psi}(x,y)|\leq|T|
and
\Sigma^{\prime}(x,y,z)\subseteq E^{\prime}_{\psi}(x,y)\cup E^{\prime}_{\psi}(y,z)
.

We now inductively define
E^{0}_{\varphi}(x,y)=\{\varphi(x,y)\}
and

E^{n+1}_{\varphi}(x,y)=E^{n}_{\varphi}(x,y)\cup E^{n}_{\varphi}(y,x)\cup\bigcup_{\psi\in E^{n}_{\varphi}}E^{\prime}_{\psi}(x,y),

and we set
E_{\varphi}(x,y)=\bigcup_{n<\omega}E^{n}_{\varphi}(x,y)
. We claim that this is the desired set of formulas. By construction we have
\varphi(x,y)\in E_{\varphi}(x,y)\subseteq E(x,y)
and
|E_{\varphi}(x,y)|\leq|T|
, so we are left to check that it defines an equivalence relation. Reflexivity is immediate from
E_{\varphi}(x,y)\subseteq E(x,y)
. Symmetry follows because step
E^{n}_{\varphi}(y,x)\subseteq E^{n+1}_{\varphi}(x,y)\subseteq E_{\varphi}(x,y)
for all
n<\omega
. We check transitivity, so suppose that
\models E_{\varphi}(a,b)\wedge E_{\varphi}(b,c)
and let
\psi(x,y)\in E_{\varphi}(x,y)
be arbitrary. Then
\psi(x,y)\in E^{n}_{\varphi}(x,y)
for some
n<\omega
, and so
E^{\prime}_{\psi}(x,y)\subseteq E^{n+1}_{\varphi}(x,y)\subseteq E_{\varphi}(x,y)
. We thus have
\models E^{\prime}_{\psi}(a,b)\wedge E^{\prime}_{\psi}(b,c)
, which by construction of
E^{\prime}_{\psi}(x,y)
implies that
\models\psi(a,c)
, as required.
∎

The following corollary can be summarised as “every hyperimaginary is interdefinable with a set of hyperimaginaries whose representing tuples have length
\leq|T|
”.

Corollary 7.1.6
.

Let
E(x,y)
be a type-definable equivalence relation. Then there is a set
\{E_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in I}
of type-definable equivalence relations such that for each
i\in I
the tuples
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
have length
\leq|T|
and are subtuples of
x
and
y
respectively, and such that
\models E(a,b)
if and only if
\models E_{i}(a,b)
for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Using ?THM?
, we take
\{E_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in I}
to be an enumeration of
\{E_{\varphi}(x,y):\varphi(x,y)\in E(x,y)\}
, where we restrict the variables each time to those that are actually mentioned. Then by construction
E_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\subseteq E(x,y)
for all
i\in I
, so
\models E(a,b)
implies
\models E_{i}(a,b)
for all
i\in I
. For the converse we let
\varphi(x,y)\in E(x,y)
and let
i\in I
be such that
E_{i}=E_{\varphi}
. So
\varphi\in E_{i}
, therefore
\models E_{i}(a,b)
implies
\models\varphi(a,b)
. As
\varphi
was arbitrary, we conclude
\models E(a,b)
.
∎

Definition 7.1.7
.

Define

{\textup{heq}}=\{E(x,y)\text{ a type-definable equivalence relation}:|x|=|y|\leq|T|\}.

Convention 7.1.8
.

For the remainder of this section we fix a set
\mathcal{E}
of hyperimaginaries.

Taking
\mathcal{E}={\textup{heq}}
we thus will be considering
T^{\textup{heq}}
and
\mathfrak{M}^{\textup{heq}}
, which by ?THM?
effectively means we have added all hyperimaginaries.

Many arguments will be easier and smoother if we can treat real elements and hyperimaginary elements notationally in the same way. We formalise this in the following definition. In particular, we extend the notation
S_{E,r}
and
y_{r}
for hyperimaginary sorts and variables to tuples, and we introduce notation for the projection functions that send tuples to the classes they represent. In doing so, one might prefer to think of the real sorts as hyperimaginary sorts themselves (namely modulo the equivalence relation
x=y
).

Definition 7.1.9
.

Let
S=(S_{i})_{i\in I}
be a tuple of sorts and let
x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}
be a tuple of corresponding variables. We write
S_{r}=(S_{i,r})_{i\in I}
and
x_{r}=(x_{i,r})_{i\in I}
, where
S_{i,r}=S_{i}
and
x_{i,r}=x_{i}
whenever
S_{i}
is already a real sort.

Given a tuple
a=(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathfrak{M}
of sort
S_{r}
, we write
[a]
for the corresponding tuple of equivalence classes of sort
S
. That is,
[a]=([a_{i}])_{i\in I}
, where
[a_{i}]
is defined as follows: if
S_{i}=S_{E}
is a hyperimaginary sort then
[a_{i}]
is the
E
-equivalence class represented by
a_{i}
, otherwise
[a_{i}]=a_{i}
.

With the above definition the description of the new relation symbols in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
becomes simpler: for every tuple of variables
x
and every
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x_{r})
we have a relation symbol
R_{\varphi}(x)
, and we have
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varphi}([a])
if and only if there is
b
such that
[b]=[a]
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi(b)
.

Lemma 7.1.10
.

Let
\varphi(x)
be an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-formula. Then there is a set of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas
\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r})
such that
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\varphi}(a)
if and only if
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varphi([a])
.

Proof.

We first assume that
\varphi(x)
is of the form

\exists y\left(\varepsilon(x,y)\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in I}R_{\chi_{i}}(x,y)\right),

where
\varepsilon(x,y)
is a conjunction of equalities and
\chi_{i}(x_{r},y_{r})
is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula for each
i\in I
.

We define a set of formulas
\Gamma_{\varphi}
as follows. For each
i\in I
we let
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
be copies of
x_{r}
and
y_{r}
respectively. We let
E_{\varepsilon}(x_{r},y_{r})
be the union of partial types in
\mathcal{E}
expressing
\varepsilon([x_{r}],[y_{r}])
. Finally, let
E_{x}(x_{r},x^{\prime}_{r})
and
E_{y}(y_{r},y^{\prime}_{r})
be the partial types expressing
[x_{r}]=[x^{\prime}_{r}]
and
[y_{r}]=[y^{\prime}_{r}]
respectively. Then we let
\Gamma_{\varphi}(x_{r},y_{r},(x_{i})_{i\in I},(y_{i})_{i\in I})
be

\left\{\bigwedge_{i\in I}\chi_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})\right\}\cup E_{\varepsilon}(x_{r},y_{r})\cup\bigcup\{E_{x}(x_{r},x_{i}):i\in I\}\cup\bigcup\{E_{y}(y_{r},y_{i}):i\in I\}.

Let
\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r})
express the following (see also ?THM?
)

\exists y_{r}(x_{i})_{i\in I}(y_{i})_{i\in I}\Gamma_{\varphi}(x_{r},y_{r},(x_{i})_{i\in I},(y_{i})_{i\in I}).

We claim that
\Sigma_{\varphi}
is as required. So suppose that
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\varphi}(a)
, then we find
b
,
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
\mathfrak{M}\models\Gamma_{\varphi}(a,b,(a_{i})_{i\in I},(b_{i})_{i\in I})
. Let
i\in I
, then by construction
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\chi_{i}}([a_{i}],[b_{i}])
. As
\mathfrak{M}\models E_{x}(a,a_{i})
and
\mathfrak{M}\models E_{y}(b,b_{i})
, we have
[a]=[a_{i}]
and
[b]=[b_{i}]
, and so
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\chi_{i}}([a],[b])
. Since
\mathfrak{M}\models E_{\varepsilon}(a,b)
, we also have
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varepsilon([a],[b])
, and so indeed
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varphi([a])
.

For the converse, we assume that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varphi([a])
. Then there is
b
such that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varepsilon([a],[b])\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in I}R_{\chi_{i}}([a],[b])
. So
\mathfrak{M}\models E_{\varepsilon}(a,b)
. Furthermore, for every
i\in I
there are
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{M}\models\chi_{i}(a_{i},b_{i})
with
[a_{i}]=[a]
and
[b_{i}]=[b]
. The latter means that
\mathfrak{M}\models E_{x}(a,a_{i})
and
\mathfrak{M}\models E_{y}(b,b_{i})
. Hence
\mathfrak{M}\models\Gamma_{\varphi}(a,b,(a_{i})_{i\in I},(b_{i})_{i\in I})
and so
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\varphi}(a)
.

We assumed
\varphi
to be of a particular form. Recall that a regular formula is one that is built from atomic formulas, conjunction and existential quantification. Any regular
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-formula is logically equivalent to a
\varphi
of the assumed form. This is quickly seen because any atomic
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\chi(x,y)
is equivalent to
R_{\chi}(x,y)
. As every positive formula is logically equivalent to a finite disjunction of regular formulas, we only need to define
\Sigma_{\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}}(x)
, where
\varphi_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{2}(x)
are of the above form, which can be done using ?THM?
(i).
∎

Lemma 7.1.11
.

Let
\Gamma(x)
be a set of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-formulas. Then there is a set of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas
\Sigma_{\Gamma}(x_{r})
such that
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\Gamma}(a)
if and only if
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Gamma([a])
.

Proof.

Define

\Sigma_{\Gamma}(x_{r})=\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r}),

where
\Sigma_{\varphi}
is as in ?THM?
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.12
.

We have
\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([b];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
if and only if there is
b^{\prime}
such that
\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a;\mathfrak{M})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(b^{\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
. In particular, if
a
and
b
consist only of real elements then
\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}(a;\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}(b;\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
if and only if
\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a;\mathfrak{M})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(b;\mathfrak{M})
.

Proof.

We first prove the left to right direction. Write
p(x_{r})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a;\mathfrak{M})
and

\Sigma(x_{r})=p(x_{r})\cup E(x_{r},b),

where
E(x_{r},x^{\prime}_{r})
is the set of formulas expressing
[x_{r}]=[x^{\prime}_{r}]
. It is enough to show that
\Sigma(x_{r})
is finitely satisfiable. Let
\varphi(x_{r})\in p(x_{r})
. Then
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varphi}([a])
, so
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varphi}([b])
. So there is
b^{\prime}
with
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
, that is
\mathfrak{M}\models E(b^{\prime},b)
, and
\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi(b^{\prime})
, as required.

Conversely, let
b^{\prime}
be such that
\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a;\mathfrak{M})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(b^{\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
. It suffices to prove that
\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([b^{\prime}];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
. Write
q(x)=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
and let
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})
be as in ?THM?
. Then
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})\subseteq\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a;\mathfrak{M})=\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(b^{\prime};\mathfrak{M})
, and so
\mathfrak{M}^{E}\models q([b^{\prime}])
. We thus have
\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})=q(x)\subseteq\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([b^{\prime}];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
. A symmetric argument shows that
\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([b^{\prime}];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})\subseteq\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
, which concludes our proof.
∎

Lemma 7.1.13
.

For every tuple of variables
x
there is a partial
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-type
\Xi(x_{r},x)
such that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Xi(a,[a^{\prime}])
if and only if
[a^{\prime}]=[a]
.

Proof.

Let
E(x_{r},x^{\prime}_{r})
be the set of formulas that expresses
[x_{r}]=[x^{\prime}_{r}]
. Define

\Xi(x_{r},x)=\{R_{\varepsilon}(x_{r},x):\varepsilon\in E\}.

We prove that
\Xi
is as required. Suppose that
[a^{\prime}]=[a]
. Then
\mathfrak{M}\models E(a,a^{\prime})
. Let
\varepsilon\in E
, then
\mathfrak{M}\models\varepsilon(a,a^{\prime})
, and so
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varepsilon}(a,[a^{\prime}])
. Thus indeed
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Xi(a,[a^{\prime}])
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Xi(a,[a^{\prime}])
. Consider the partial type

\Gamma(x_{r})=E(a,x_{r})\cup E(x_{r},a^{\prime}).

For any
\varepsilon(a,x_{r})\in E(a,x_{r})
we have
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varepsilon}(a,[a^{\prime}])
. So there is
a^{*}
with
[a^{*}]=[a^{\prime}]
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\varepsilon(a,a^{*})
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{M}\models\varepsilon(a,a^{*})\wedge E(a^{*},a^{\prime})
. We thus see that
\Gamma(x_{r})
is finitely satisfiable, so there is a realisation
a^{\prime\prime}
, and we conclude that
[a]=[a^{\prime\prime}]=[a^{\prime}]
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.14
.

Any automorphism
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
extends uniquely to an automorphism
f^{\mathcal{E}}:\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
by setting
f^{\mathcal{E}}([a])=[f(a)]
.

Proof.

We first prove that
f^{\mathcal{E}}
is well defined and preserves the new relation symbols in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
. The fact that
f^{\mathcal{E}}
is an automorphism then follows from applying the same construction to
f^{-1}
, yielding an inverse
(f^{-1})^{\mathcal{E}}
to
f^{\mathcal{E}}
that also preserves the new relation symbols in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
.

•

Well defined
. Let
E\in\mathcal{E}
and let
a
and
a^{\prime}
be tuples in
\mathfrak{M}
matching the variables in
E
such that
[a]=[a^{\prime}]
. Then
\mathfrak{M}\models E(a,a^{\prime})
, and so
\mathfrak{M}\models E(f(a),f(a^{\prime}))
. Therefore
[f(a)]=[f(a^{\prime})]
, showing that
f^{\mathcal{E}}
is well defined.

•

Preservation of relation symbols
. Preservation of equality is just being well defined. Suppose that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varphi}([a])
. Then there is
a^{\prime}
such that
[a^{\prime}]=[a]
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi(a^{\prime})
. So
\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi(f(a^{\prime}))
and thus
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\varphi}([f(a^{\prime})])
. We conclude by
[f(a^{\prime})]=f^{\mathcal{E}}([a^{\prime}])=f^{\mathcal{E}}([a])
.

We are left to prove that
f^{\mathcal{E}}
is unique. Suppose that
g:\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
is an automorphism extending
f
. For any tuple
a
we have that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Xi(a,[a])
, by ?THM?
. So since
g
is an automorphism, we must have
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Xi(g(a),g([a]))
. That is,
g([a])=[g(a)]=[f(a)]=f^{\mathcal{E}}([a])
, as required.
∎

Theorem 7.1.15
.

The structure
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
is a monster model of
T^{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

We prove that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
is a p.c. model of
T^{\mathcal{E}}
, and that it is just as saturated and homogeneous as
\mathfrak{M}
. So whatever formalism one prefers for the monster model (see ?THM?
),
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
will be a monster model of
T^{\mathcal{E}}
according to the same formalism. Let
\kappa
(possibly not ‘small’) be such that
\mathfrak{M}
is positively
\kappa
-saturated and strongly positively
\kappa
-homogeneous. We may assume that
\kappa
is bigger than the length of any tuple representing a hyperimaginary.

Positively closed
. We will prove (iii) in ?THM?
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\not\models\varphi([a])
. Then
\mathfrak{M}\not\models\Sigma_{\varphi}(a)
, where
\Sigma_{\varphi}
is as in ?THM?
. There is thus
\psi(x_{r})\in\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r})
such that
\mathfrak{M}\not\models\psi(a)
. Because
\mathfrak{M}
is p.c. we find
\chi(x_{r})
with
T\models\neg\exists x_{r}(\psi(x_{r})\wedge\chi(x_{r}))
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\chi(a)
. Thus
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\chi}([a])
. We will conclude by proving that
T^{\mathcal{E}}\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge R_{\chi}(x))
. Suppose for a contradiction that there is
[b]
such that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varphi([b])\wedge R_{\chi}([b])
. Then there is
b^{\prime}
with
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\chi(b^{\prime})
. So
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varphi([b^{\prime}])
and thus
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\varphi}(b^{\prime})
. We then get
\mathfrak{M}\models\psi(b^{\prime})\wedge\chi(b^{\prime})
, contradicting our choice of
\chi
.

Saturation
. Let
\Gamma(x,y)
be a set of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-formulas, and let
[b]
be a tuple with
|[b]|<\kappa
such that
\Gamma(x,[b])
is finitely satisfiable in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
. Let
\Sigma_{\Gamma}(x_{r},y_{r})
be as in ?THM?
. By the construction there we have

\Sigma_{\Gamma}(x_{r},y_{r})=\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r},y_{r}),

where
\Sigma_{\varphi}
is as in ?THM?
. So finite satisfiability of
\Gamma(x,[b])
implies finite satisfiability of
\Sigma_{\Gamma}(x_{r},b)
. Since
|b|<\kappa
we find a realisation
a
in
\mathfrak{M}
with
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\Gamma}(a,b)
, and hence
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Gamma([a],[b])
.

Homogeneity
. Suppose that
\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a])=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([b])
, with
|[a]|=|[b]|<\kappa
. By ?THM?
there is
b^{\prime}
such that
\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a)=\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(b^{\prime})
and
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
. Let
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
be an automorphism such that
f(a)=b^{\prime}
. By ?THM?
we find an automorphism
f^{\mathcal{E}}:\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
such that
f^{\mathcal{E}}([a])=[f(a)]=[b^{\prime}]=[b]
, as required.
∎

By ?THM?
the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-type of a tuple of real elements is determined by its
\mathcal{L}
-type. In particular, any sequence of (tuples of) real elements is indiscernible in
\mathfrak{M}
if and only if it is indiscernible in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
, so there is no ambiguity in the statement (and proof) below.

Lemma 7.1.16
.

A sequence
([a_{i}])_{i\in I}
is indiscernible if and only if there is an indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
[b_{i}]=[a_{i}]
for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

We first prove the left to right direction. By compactness we may assume
I
to be as long as we need, and at the end we simply forget about the extra elements. We thus find an indiscernible sequence
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
based on
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
. Let
p((x_{i,r})_{i\in I})=\tp((a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I})
and define

\Gamma((x_{i,r})_{i\in I})=p((x_{i,r})_{i\in I})\cup\{\Xi(x_{i,r},[a_{i}]):i\in I\}.

Then a realisation of
\Gamma
is precisely what we need. So we prove that
\Gamma
is finitely satisfiable. That is, for
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}\in I
, we will produce a realisation of
\Gamma
restricted to the variables
x_{i_{1},r},\ldots,x_{i_{n},r}
and parameters
[a_{i_{1}}],\ldots,[a_{i_{n}}]
. By construction there are
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}\in I
such that
a^{\prime}_{i_{1}}\ldots a^{\prime}_{i_{n}}\equiv a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{n}}
. As
[a_{i_{1}}]\ldots[a_{i_{n}}]\equiv[a_{j_{1}}]\ldots[a_{j_{n}}]
, we have by ?THM?
that there are
a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{n}}
such that
a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{n}}\equiv a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{n}}
, while also
[a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{k}}]=[a_{i_{k}}]
for all
1\leq k\leq n
. So
a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}},\ldots,a^{\prime\prime}_{i_{n}}
is the desired realisation of the restriction of
\Gamma
.

For the right to left direction, we have for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}\in I
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}\in I
that
b_{i_{1}}\ldots b_{i_{n}}\equiv b_{j_{1}}\ldots b_{j_{n}}
. By ?THM?
this implies

[a_{i_{1}}]\ldots[a_{i_{n}}]=[b_{i_{1}}\ldots b_{i_{n}}]\equiv[b_{j_{1}}\ldots b_{j_{n}}]=[a_{j_{1}}]\ldots[a_{j_{n}}],

and we conclude that
([a_{i}])_{i\in I}
is indeed indiscernible.
∎

Theorem 7.1.17
.

The following properties are preserved under
(-)^{\mathcal{E}}
:

•

Hausdorff,

•

semi-Hausdorff,

•

thick.

That is, if
T
has the property then
T^{\mathcal{E}}
has it as well.

Proof.

Hausdorff
. Let
p(x)=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
and
q(x)=\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([b];\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}})
be distinct types. Let
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})
and
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})
be as in ?THM?
.

Let
s(x_{r})
be any
\mathcal{L}
-type such that
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})\subseteq s(x_{r})
. We will produce formulas
\alpha_{s}(x_{r})
and
\beta_{s}(x_{r})
such that
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\cup\{\alpha_{s}(x_{r})\}
is inconsistent,
\beta_{s}(x_{r})\not\in s(x_{r})
and
T\models\forall x_{r}(\alpha_{s}(x_{r})\vee\beta_{s}(x_{r}))
. Let
t(x_{r})\supseteq\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})
be an
\mathcal{L}
-type. Then
t(x_{r})\neq s(x_{r})
, because
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\cup\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})
is inconsistent. As
T
is Hausdorff, there are
\chi_{t}(x_{r})
and
\theta_{t}(x_{r})
such that
\chi_{t}(x_{r})\not\in t(x_{r})
and
\theta_{t}(x_{r})\not\in s(x_{r})
, while
T\models\forall x_{r}(\chi_{t}(x_{r})\vee\theta_{t}(x_{r}))
. Then
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\cup\{\chi_{t}(x_{r}):t(x_{r})\supseteq\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\}
is inconsistent. There are thus
t_{1}(x_{r}),\ldots,t_{n}(x_{r})
such that
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\cup\{\chi_{t_{1}}(x_{r})\wedge\ldots\wedge\chi_{t_{n}}(x_{r})\}
is inconsistent. We can now take
\alpha_{s}(x_{r})
to be
\chi_{t_{1}}(x_{r})\wedge\ldots\wedge\chi_{t_{n}}(x_{r})
and
\beta_{s}(x_{r})
to be
\theta_{t_{1}}(x_{r})\vee\ldots\vee\theta_{t_{n}}(x_{r})
.

By construction
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})\cup\{\beta_{s}(x_{r}):s(x_{r})\supseteq\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})\}
is inconsistent. So there are
s_{1}(x_{r}),\ldots,s_{k}(x_{r})
such that
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})\cup\{\beta_{s_{1}}(x_{r})\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta_{s_{n}}(x_{r})\}
is inconsistent. Let
\beta(x_{r})
be the formula
\beta_{s_{1}}(x_{r})\wedge\ldots\wedge\beta_{s_{k}}(x_{r})
and let
\alpha(x_{r})
be the formula
\alpha_{s_{1}}(x_{r})\vee\ldots\vee\alpha_{s_{k}}(x_{r})
. Then
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\cup\{\alpha(x_{r})\}
and
\Sigma_{q}(x_{r})\cup\{\beta(x_{r})\}
are both inconsistent and
T\models\forall x_{r}(\alpha(x_{r})\vee\beta(x_{r}))
.

We now consider the formulas
R_{\alpha}(x)
and
R_{\beta}(x)
. By construction we have
T^{\mathcal{E}}\models\forall x(R_{\alpha}(x)\vee R_{\beta}(x))
. We claim that
R_{\alpha}(x)\not\in p(x)
. Suppose for a contradiction that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models R_{\alpha}([a])
. Then there is
a^{\prime}
with
[a^{\prime}]=[a]
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\alpha(a^{\prime})
. At the same time
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models p([a^{\prime}])
and so
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{p}(a^{\prime})
, contradicting the inconsistency of
\Sigma_{p}(x_{r})\cup\{\alpha(x_{r})\}
. Analogously we have that
R_{\beta}(x)\not\in q(x)
, and so we conclude that
T^{\mathcal{E}}
is Hausdorff.

Semi-Hausdorff
. Suppose that equality of
\mathcal{L}
-types is type-definable by a partial
\mathcal{L}
-type
\Omega
. Then for any tuple
x
of variables in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
, we let
\Omega^{\mathcal{E}}(x,x^{\prime})
be the set of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-formulas that expresses

\exists x_{r}x^{\prime}_{r}(\Xi(x_{r},x)\wedge\Xi(x^{\prime}_{r},x^{\prime})\wedge\Omega(x_{r},x^{\prime}_{r})).

We claim that
\Omega^{\mathcal{E}}
expresses equality of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}
-types.

If
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\Omega^{\mathcal{E}}([a],[b])
then this is saying that there are
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
such that
[a^{\prime}]=[a]
,
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
and
\mathfrak{M}\models\Omega(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. So we have
a^{\prime}\equiv b^{\prime}
and hence
[a]=[a^{\prime}]\equiv[b^{\prime}]=[b]
by ?THM?
.

Conversely, suppose that
[a]\equiv[b]
. Then by ?THM?
there is
b^{\prime}
such that
[b^{\prime}]=[b]
and
a\equiv b^{\prime}
. In particular
\mathfrak{M}\models\Xi(a,[a])\wedge\Xi(b^{\prime},[b])\wedge\Omega(a,b^{\prime})
, as required.

Thick
. Let
\Theta
express indiscernibility of a sequence of (tuples of) real elements. Then

\exists(x_{i,r})_{i<\omega}\left(\Theta((x_{i,r})_{i<\omega})\wedge\bigwedge_{i<\omega}\Xi(x_{i,r},x_{i})\right)

expresses indiscernibility of
(x_{i})_{i<\omega}
in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
. Here we use that a sequence in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
is indiscernible if and only if there is an indiscernible sequence of representatives, see ?THM?
.
∎

Example 7.1.18
.

Being Boolean is not preserved when moving from
T
to
T^{\mathcal{E}}
. For example, start with any theory
T
in full first-order logic (considered as a positive theory through Morleyisation, see ?THM?
). Then
T
is by construction Boolean. Assume that
T
is complete with an infinite model, and consider the type-definable equivalence relation

E(x,y)=\{\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow\varphi(y):\varphi\text{ is a formula in }T\}.

Then
E
expresses that
x
and
y
have the same type. Set
\mathcal{E}=\{E\}
, then in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
the elements of the sort
S_{E}
are exactly the types (with free variables
x
) in
\mathfrak{M}
. If there are infinitely many types with free variables
x
(e.g.,
x
is an infinite tuple of variables in the sort whose underlying set is infinite) then
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
has a bounded infinite definable set. So
T^{\mathcal{E}}
cannot be Boolean: inequality on
S_{E}
is not equivalent to a positive formula (modulo
T^{\mathcal{E}}
), because otherwise by compactness we would find arbitrarily many elements in
S_{E}
.

Another example is to consider the full first-order theory of the real numbers as an ordered field
T=\Th(\mathbb{R};0,1,+,\cdot,-,\leq)
. This is also known as the theory of
real closed fields
. Consider the type-definable equivalence relation

E(x,y)=\{-1/n<x-y<1/n:n<\omega\}.

Then
E
expresses that
x
and
y
are infinitesimally close. Set
\mathcal{E}=\{E\}
, then the real unit interval
[0,1]
is definable in the sort
S_{E}
in
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
by the formula
[0]\leq x\wedge x\leq[1]
. Here
[0]
and
[1]
are the
E
-equivalence classes of
0
and
1
respectively, and the relation
\leq
is an abbreviation for
R_{x_{r}\leq y_{r}}(x,y)
.

Adding hyperimaginaries does not impact many model-theoretic properties of the theory, such as simplicity or stability. We will prove this for those two here, based on the characterisations in these notes (?THM?
and ?THM?
respectively).

Theorem 7.1.19
.

The theory
T
is simple if and only if
T^{\mathcal{E}}
is simple.

Proof.

We will prove that
T
is
\mathsf{NTP}
if and only if
T^{\mathcal{E}}
is
\mathsf{NTP}
. The left to right direction is trivial. We prove the converse.

Suppose that
\varphi(x,y)
has
\mathsf{TP}
in
T^{\mathcal{E}}
, as witnessed by parameters
([a_{\eta}])_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
and some formula
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
. Let
\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r},y_{r})
and
\Sigma_{\psi}(y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{k,r})
be as in ?THM?
. Then

\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r},y_{1,r})\cup\ldots\cup\Sigma_{\varphi}(x_{r},y_{1,k})\cup\Sigma_{\psi}(y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{1,k})
