is inconsistent. Hence there are
\varphi^{\prime}(x_{r},y_{r})\in\Sigma_{\varphi}
and
\psi^{\prime}(y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{k,r})\in\Sigma_{\psi}
such that

\varphi^{\prime}(x_{r},y_{1,r})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi^{\prime}(x_{r},y_{k,r})\wedge\psi^{\prime}(y_{1,r},\ldots,y_{k,r})

is inconsistent modulo
T^{\mathcal{E}}
. As the above is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula, it is also inconsistent modulo
T
. We claim that
\varphi^{\prime}(x_{r},y_{r})
has
\mathsf{TP}
, as witnessed by
(a_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
and
\psi^{\prime}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
. We check ?THM?
.

(i)

Let
\sigma\in\omega^{\omega}
. Then
\{\varphi(x,[a_{\sigma|_{n}}]):n<\omega\}
is consistent. So there is
[b]
such that
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\varphi([b],[a_{\sigma|_{n}}])
for all
n<\omega
. That is, we have
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\varphi}(b,a_{\sigma|_{n}})
for all
n<\omega
. In particular,
\{\varphi^{\prime}(x,a_{\sigma|_{n}}):n<\omega\}
is consistent.

(ii)

Let
\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}
and let
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<\omega
. Then
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}\models\psi([a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}}],\ldots,[a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{k}}])
, so
\mathfrak{M}\models\Sigma_{\psi}(a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}},\ldots,a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{k}})
and in particular
\mathfrak{M}\models\psi^{\prime}(a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}},\ldots,a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{k}})
.

∎

Theorem 7.1.20
.

The theory
T
is stable if and only if
T^{\mathcal{E}}
is stable.

Proof.

If
T^{\mathcal{E}}
is
\lambda
-stable then
T
is
\lambda
-stable by ?THM?
. For the converse we let
\lambda
be an upper bound for the lengths of the tuples of variables appearing in the equivalence relations in
\mathcal{E}
. Note that
\lambda
is still small compared to the monster. We will prove that if
T
is
\lambda
-stable then
T^{\mathcal{E}}
is
\lambda
-stable.

Let
B
be a set of parameters from
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
with
|B|\leq\lambda
and let
x
be a finite tuple of hyperimaginary variables. Write
\operatorname{S}_{x}(B)
for the set of types over
B
in free variables
x
. We need to show that
|\operatorname{S}_{x}(B)|\leq\lambda
. Enumerate
B
as a tuple
[b]
. Then
[b]
has length
\leq\lambda
and since every hyperimaginary is represented by at most
\lambda
elements, we have
|b|\leq\lambda
. Let
\operatorname{S}_{x_{r}}(b)
be the set of
\mathcal{L}
-types over
b
in free variables
x_{r}
. For every finite subtuple
x^{\prime}\subseteq x_{r}
we have by assumption that there are at most
\lambda
many
\mathcal{L}
-types over
b
in free variables
x^{\prime}
. Since types are determined by their finite restrictions, we have that
|\operatorname{S}_{x_{r}}(b)|\leq|x_{r}|^{<\omega}\times\lambda\leq\lambda^{<\omega}\times\lambda=\lambda
. Define a map
\operatorname{S}_{x_{r}}(b)\to\operatorname{S}_{x}(B)
by

\tp_{\mathcal{L}}(a/b)\mapsto\tp_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{E}}}([a]/[b]),

which is well-defined by ?THM?
. By construction and saturation of
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathcal{E}}
this map is surjective and so
|\operatorname{S}_{x}(B)|\leq\lambda
, as required.
∎

7.2
Continuous logic

In this section we will see how continuous logic can be studied using positive logic. Unlike the situation with full first-order logic, positive logic is not a direct generalisation of continuous logic (see also ?THM?
). However, for many abstract model-theoretic purposes, positive logic is more general and developing the abstract theory in the generality of positive logic allows us to immediately apply it to continuous logic.

The framework for continuous logic that we will consider is that of
[
BYBHU08
]
. We will use the notation and terminology from there, which we will assume the reader to be familiar with. We just note that what they call a
\kappa
-universal domain
is what we call a monster model (at least, when
\kappa
is bigger than all “small” cardinals, see Section
4.3
), and we will omit the
\kappa
from the notation. For simplicity of notation we will assume that all bounded intervals in our continuous logic are simply
[0,1]
.

Definition 7.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a universal domain of some continuous theory
T
in some metric signature
\mathcal{L}
. We define
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
to be the following relational signature (in the first-order sense). For every
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)
we introduce a relation symbol
R_{\varphi}(x)
of the same arity. We then turn
\mathcal{U}
into an
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-structure
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
by interpreting
R_{\varphi}
as the set
\{a\in\mathcal{U}:\varphi(a)=0\}
. Let
T_{\textup{pos}}
be the positive theory of
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. That is,
T_{\textup{pos}}
is the set of all h-inductive sentences in
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
that are true in
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
.

Convention 7.2.2
.

For the remainder of this section,
\mathcal{U}
is a universal domain of some fixed continuous theory
T
in some metric signature
\mathcal{L}
, and we let
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
,
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
T_{\textup{pos}}
be as in ?THM?
.

Remark 7.2.3
.

We write
\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}
for the truncated subtraction operation
[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
. That is, for
r,s\in[0,1]

r\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}s=\begin{cases}r-s&\text{if }r\geq s,\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Then given any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)
and any
r\in[0,1]
we get an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}r
. Then
R_{\varphi\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 3.01389pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}r}
is interpreted in
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
as

\{a\in\mathcal{U}:\varphi(a)\leq r\}.

We will thus use the notation
R_{\varphi\leq r}
for
R_{\varphi\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 3.01389pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}r}
. Similarly, we write
R_{\varphi\geq r}
for
R_{r\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 3.01389pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}\varphi}
.

Proposition 7.2.4
.

Modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
, we have the following equivalences of formulas:

(i)

\bot
is equivalent to
R_{r}
, where
r
is the
\mathcal{L}
-formula with constant value
r
, for any
r>0
;

(ii)

x=y
is equivalent to
R_{d}(x,y)
, where
d(x,y)
is the metric on
\mathcal{U}
;

(iii)

R_{\varphi}(x)\wedge R_{\psi}(x)
is equivalent to
R_{\max(\varphi,\psi)}(x)
;

(iv)

R_{\varphi}(x)\vee R_{\psi}(x)
is equivalent to
R_{\min(\varphi,\psi)}(x)
;

(v)

\exists yR_{\varphi}(x,y)
is equivalent to
R_{\inf_{y}\varphi}(x)
.

In particular, every
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formula is equivalent to
R_{\varphi}
for some
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
.

Proof.

Items (i)–(iv) follow immediately from the definitions. The final claim follows by induction on the construction of
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formulas, using (i) and (ii) for the base case and using (iii)–(v) for the inductive steps. So we are left to prove (v).

Suppose that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\inf_{y}\varphi}(a)
. Then
\mathcal{U}\models\inf_{y}\varphi(a,y)=0
and so the set
\mathcal{L}
-conditions
\Sigma(y)=\{\varphi(a,y)\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}\frac{1}{n}=0:1\leq n<\omega\}
is finitely satisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
. By saturation of
\mathcal{U}
there must be
b\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{U}\models\Sigma(b)
, which is exactly saying that
\mathcal{U}\models\varphi(a,b)=0
. That is,
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\varphi}(a,b)
, and so
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models\exists yR_{\varphi}(a,y)
.

Conversely, suppose that there is
b\in\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
such that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\varphi}(a,b)
. Then
\mathcal{U}\models\varphi(a,b)=0
, and so
\mathcal{U}\models\inf_{y}\varphi(a,y)=0
. We conclude that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\inf_{y}\varphi}(a)
, as required.
∎

Theorem 7.2.5
.

We have the following properties for
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
T_{\textup{pos}}
.

(i)

The structure
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is a monster model of
T_{\textup{pos}}
.

(ii)

The structures
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mathcal{U}
have the same automorphisms.

(iii)

The theory
T_{\textup{pos}}
is Hausdorff.

Proof.

(i)
We first prove that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is a p.c. model of
T_{\textup{pos}}
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\not\models\theta(a)
for some
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formula
\theta(x)
and some
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. By ?THM?
there is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)
such that
\theta(x)
is equivalent to
R_{\varphi}(x)
. Set
r=\varphi(a)
, then
r\neq 0
because
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\not\models R_{\varphi}(a)
. Hence
0<r/2<r
and so
R_{\varphi\geq r/2}(\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}})\cap R_{\varphi}(\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}})=\emptyset
. Therefore
R_{\varphi\geq r/2}(x)
is an obstruction of
R_{\varphi}(x)
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
, while
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\varphi\geq r/2}(a)
. We thus conclude that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is a p.c. model of
T_{\textup{pos}}
.

Next we check that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is as saturated as
\mathcal{U}
. Let
\Sigma(x)
be a small set of
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formulas with parameters in
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
, which is finitely satisfiable in
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. By ?THM?

\Sigma(x)
is (equivalent to) a set of relation symbols of the form
R_{\varphi}(x)
, where
\varphi(x)
is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula. Define the following set of
\mathcal{L}
-conditions

\Sigma^{\prime}(x)=\{\varphi(x)=0:\varphi(x)\text{ is an $\mathcal{L}$-formula s.t.\ }R_{\varphi}(x)\in\Sigma(x)\}.

Then
\Sigma^{\prime}(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
and so by saturation there is
a\in\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{U}\models\Sigma^{\prime}(a)
. By construction
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models\Sigma(a)
, as required.

Finally, we check that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is as homogeneous as
\mathcal{U}
. Let
a
and
b
be small tuples in
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
be such that
\tp(a;\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}})=\tp(b;\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}})
. Then they satisfy the same set of
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-relation symbols, which is precisely saying that they satisfy the same set of
\mathcal{L}
-conditions, and so by homogeneity of
\mathcal{U}
there is an automorphism
f
of
\mathcal{U}
that sends
a
to
b
. By (ii), the proof of which stands on itself,
f
is also an automorphism of
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
.

(ii)
Let
f
be an automorphism of the underlying set of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. First, suppose that
f
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{U}
. Then
f
preserves null-sets of formulas set-wise. So it respects the relation symbols in
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
, and it is thus an automorphism of
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. Conversely, suppose that
f
is an automorphism of
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. Let
\varphi(x)
be an
\mathcal{L}
-formula, let
a\in\mathcal{U}
and set
r=\varphi(a)
. Then
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\varphi\leq r}(a)
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\not\models R_{\varphi\leq s}(a)
for all
s<r
. Hence
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\models R_{\varphi\leq r}(f(a))
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\not\models R_{\varphi\leq s}(f(a))
for all
s<r
. This says exactly that
\varphi(f(a))\leq r
and
\varphi(f(a))>s
for all
s<r
. So
\varphi(f(a))=r
, and we conclude that
f
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{U}
.

(iii)
Let
p(x)
and
q(x)
be two distinct types. As
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is a monster model (see item (i)), there are realisations
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
b\in\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
of
p
and
q
respectively. Let
\theta(x)\in p(x)
such that
\theta(x)\not\in q(x)
. By ?THM?
,
\theta(x)
is (equivalent to)
R_{\varphi}(x)
for some
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)
. Set
r=\varphi(b)
, so
r>0
. Clearly we have
T_{\textup{pos}}\models\forall x(R_{\varphi\leq r/2}(x)\vee R_{\varphi\geq r/2}(x))
, and by construction
R_{\varphi\leq r/2}(x)\not\in q(x)
while
R_{\varphi\geq r/2}(x)\not\in p(x)
. We conclude that
T_{\textup{pos}}
is Hausdorff.
∎

The fact that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mathcal{U}
have the same automorphisms means that they agree on what a type is, and so they enjoy the same model-theoretic properties. For example, one is simple if and only if the other is (see also ?THM?s
and
).

Remark 7.2.6
.

We have made essential use of the fact that
\mathcal{U}
is very saturated. In particular, in proving that
\exists yR_{\varphi}(x,y)
is equivalent to
R_{\inf_{y}\varphi}(x)
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
(i.e., ?THM?
(v)). Without saturation we still get that
\exists yR_{\varphi}(x,y)
implies
R_{\inf_{y}\varphi}(x)
, but for the other direction we only get values for
y
that take
\varphi(x,y)
arbitrarily close to
0
, but we might never actually reach
0
.

Of course, to make the argument in ?THM?
(v) work, being
\omega
-saturated would be enough. So we could prove that every
\omega
-saturated metric model of
T
becomes a positively
\omega
-saturated p.c. model of
T_{\textup{pos}}
.

We have not considered the converse. Fully investigating this is beyond our scope, but we briefly discuss the obstacles and possibilities here. Firstly, any p.c. model
M
of
T_{\textup{pos}}
can quickly be turned it into a metric structure because the exact values of the metric, function symbols and relation symbols are captured by the positive formulas. For example, for elements
a
and
b
their distance will be the smallest
r
such that
M\models R_{d\leq r}(a,b)
, where
d(x,y)
is the metric symbol. The theory
T_{\textup{pos}}
captures that all symbols behave as required (e.g.,
d
is a metric and the function symbols all have the correct modulus of uniform continuity, etc.). The only issue is that the underlying metric space of a metric structure must be complete. This will obviously generally fail when starting with an arbitrary p.c. model and can be fixed by requiring
M
to be
\omega_{1}
-saturated. Indeed, given a Cauchy sequence
(a_{n})_{n<\omega}
in
M
one easily writes down a set of formulas
\Sigma(x)
with parameters
(a_{n})_{n<\omega}
that expresses that
x
is the limit of
(a_{n})_{n<\omega}
. The fact that
(a_{n})_{n<\omega}
is a Cauchy sequence means that
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable in
M
(in fact, by elements from
(a_{n})_{n<\omega}
). By saturation
\Sigma(x)
is satisfiable in
M
, which means that
(a_{n})_{n<\omega}
has a limit in
M
.

In summary, in the above we have essentially shown the following. For any infinite
\kappa
, a
\kappa
-saturated metric model of
T
yields a positively
\kappa
-saturated p.c. model of
T_{\textup{pos}}
. At the same time, we have sketched a proof of: if
\kappa\geq\omega_{1}
then every positively
\kappa
-saturated p.c. model of
T_{\textup{pos}}
yields a
\kappa
-saturated metric model of
T
.

Example 7.2.7
.

While
T_{\textup{pos}}
is always Hausdorff, this is the best we can do. That is,
T_{\textup{pos}}
will generally not be Boolean. For example, consider
[0,1]
with the Euclidean metric as a metric structure (so
\mathcal{L}
is the empty language, i.e., we only have the symbol
d
for the metric). Let
T
be the continuous theory of
[0,1]
and let
\mathcal{U}
be the corresponding universal domain. Suppose for a contradiction that
T_{\textup{pos}}
is Boolean. Then we can consider the following set of
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formulas:

\Sigma(x)=\{x\neq 0\}\cup\{R_{d\leq 1/n}(x,0):1\leq n<\omega\}.

Clearly
\Sigma(x)
is finitely satisfiable, and so there is
a\in\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
that realises
\Sigma(x)
. However, for such
a
we have both
a\neq 0
and
d(a,0)=0
, a contradiction. So we conclude that
T_{\textup{pos}}
cannot be Boolean.

In the proof that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
is a p.c. model we have already seen that
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}\not\models R_{\varphi}(a)
is always witnessed by an obstruction of the form
R_{\varphi\geq\varepsilon}(x)
for some
\varepsilon>0
. In fact, these are essentially the only possible obstructions, as is made precise below.

Proposition 7.2.8
.

Let
\theta(x)
be an
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formula and let
\varphi(x)
be an
\mathcal{L}
-formula, such that
\theta(x)
is an obstruction of
R_{\varphi}(x)
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
. Then there is
\varepsilon>0
such that
T_{\textup{pos}}\models\forall x(\theta(x)\to R_{\varphi\geq\varepsilon}(x))
.

Proof.

By ?THM?
there is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\psi(x)
such that
\theta(x)
is equivalent to
R_{\psi}(x)
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
. Consider the set of
\mathcal{L}
-conditions

\Sigma(x)=\{\psi(x)=0\}\cup\{\varphi(x)\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}\frac{1}{n}=0:1\leq n<\omega\}.

Then
\Sigma(x)
must be unsatisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
, as a realisation
a
would satisfy both
\mathcal{U}\models\psi(a)=0
and
\mathcal{U}\models\varphi(a)=0
, contradicting that
R_{\psi}(x)
is an obstruction of
R_{\varphi}(x)
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
. There is thus some
1\leq n<\omega
such that
\{\psi(x)=0,\varphi(x)\mathbin{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{.}\cr$-$\cr}}}}\frac{1}{n}=0\}
is unsatisfiable in
\mathcal{U}
. That is, for all
a\in\mathcal{U}
we have that if
\psi(a)=0
then
\varphi(a)>\frac{1}{n}
. In other words,
R_{\psi}(x)
implies
R_{\varphi\geq 1/n}(x)
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
, as required.
∎

As an example of how results in positive logic can be applied to continuous logic, using the above translation, we will treat simplicity in continuous logic (i.e., the main results from Chapter
5
).

Definition 7.2.9
.

Let
k\geq 2
be a natural number. An
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,y)
is said to have the
k
-tree property
(
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
) if there are
(a_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
in
\mathcal{U}
and some
\varepsilon>0
such that:

(i)

for all
\sigma\in\omega^{\omega}
the set
\{\varphi(x,a_{\sigma|_{n}}):n<\omega\}
is consistent,

(ii)

for all
\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}
and
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<\omega
we have that

\inf_{x}(\max(\varphi(x,a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}}),\ldots,\varphi(x,a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}})))\geq\varepsilon.

An
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,y)
has the
tree property
(
\mathsf{TP}
) if there exists some natural number
k\geq 2
such that
\varphi(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
.

The theory
T
has the
tree property
(
\mathsf{TP}
) if there is a formula that has the tree property, and otherwise we say that
T
is
\mathsf{NTP}
or
simple
.

Proposition 7.2.10
.

An
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
(in the sense of ?THM?
) if and only if the
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formula
R_{\varphi}(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
(modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
, in the sense of ?THM?
).

In particular
T
is simple if and only if
T_{\textup{pos}}
is simple.

Proof.

Throughout this proof we freely use the translation between
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formulas from ?THM?
. In particular, for
k\geq 2
we write
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
for the
\mathcal{L}
-formula

\inf_{x}(\max(\varphi(x,y_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(x,y_{k}))),

so
R_{\psi}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
is equivalent to
\exists x(R_{\varphi}(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge R_{\varphi}(x,y_{k}))
modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
.

If
\varphi(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
then the same tree of parameters witnesses
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
for
R_{\varphi}(x,y)
. We take
R_{\psi\geq\varepsilon}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
as the witnessing obstruction of the formula
\exists x(R_{\varphi}(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge R_{\varphi}(x,y_{k}))
, where
\varepsilon
is as in ?THM?
.

Conversely, if
R_{\varphi}(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
then this is witnessed by some tree of parameters and an obstruction
\theta(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
for
\exists x(R_{\varphi}(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge R_{\varphi}(x,y_{k}))
. By ?THM?
there is
\varepsilon>0
such that
T_{\textup{pos}}\models\forall y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}(\theta(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})\to R_{\psi\geq\varepsilon}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}))
, which shows that the same tree of parameters, together with
\varepsilon
, witnesses
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
for
\varphi(x,y)
.

The final claim about simplicity follows because in both cases being simple is defined as being
\mathsf{NTP}
, and because every
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{pos}}
-formula is equivalent (modulo
T_{\textup{pos}}
) to one of the form
R_{\varphi}
.
∎

Theorem 7.2.11

(Kim-Pillay for continuous logic)
.

The theory
T
is simple if and only if there is an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\mathcal{U}
satisfying
invariance
,
monotonicity
,
normality
,
existence
,
full existence
,
base monotonicity
,
extension
,
symmetry
,
transitivity
,
finite character
,
local character
and
independence theorem
. Furthermore, in this case
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
.

Remark 7.2.12
.

We make the following notes about ?THM?
.

(i)

We have not defined what it means for two tuples of
\mathcal{U}
to have the same type, but this can be understood as being in the same automorphism orbit.

(ii)

If one does not wish to translate the definition of Lascar strong types to continuous logic then the
independence theorem
property can be weakened by only allowing
\lambda_{T}
-saturated models in the base, see also ?THM?
. By ?THM?
it does not matter here if we speak about saturation in the continuous sense or in the positive sense.

(iii)

We have not defined dividing for continuous logic. There is a definition in
[
BYBHU08
, Definition 14.11]
, which is what one would expect. Importantly, both
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
agree on when a type divides (pointing out, once more, that they agree on types in the first place). So there is no difference between computing the
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
relation in
\mathcal{U}
or
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
. This also means that simplicity of
T
can be characterised as local character of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
, see ?THM?
.

Proof of ?THM?
.

Apply ?THM?
to
T_{\textup{pos}}
, noting that
T_{\textup{pos}}
is Hausdorff (?THM?
(iii)) and thus thick and
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mathcal{U}
agree on types (so
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
has the same properties in either structure), while ?THM?
tells us that
T
is simple if and only if
T_{\textup{pos}}
is simple. The final claim follows because
\mathfrak{M}_{\textup{pos}}
and
\mathcal{U}
agree on what dividing means (see ?THM?
(iii)).
∎

7.3
Bibliographic remarks

We discuss the bibliographic remarks separately for each of the two sections in this chapter.

7.3.1
Hyperimaginaries, Section
7.1

The original motivation of
[
BY03a
]
was to create a model-theoretic framework where “hyperimaginary elements could be adjoined as parameters to the language, the same way we used to do it with real and imaginary ones since the dawn of time”. In
[
BY03a
, Example 2.16]
a brief description is given of how to add hyperimaginaries, which is exactly the construction that was worked out in this chapter. The first place where these details appear is in
[
DK22
, Section 10C]
(?THM?s
,
, and
come from
[
BY03c
, Section 3]
). The present version is slightly easier and smoother because we do not notationally distinguish between real and hyperimaginary sorts (see ?THM?
).

The proof that simplicity is respected by adding hyperimaginaries (?THM?
) is essentially the same as
[
DK22
, Theorem 10.18]
, where a similar result is proved for
\mathsf{NSOP_{1}}
. As is noted there as well, this proof works for any model-theoretic dividing line that is defined like
\mathsf{TP}
,
\mathsf{SOP_{1}}
, etc. So ?THM?
could be proved in a similar way using the order property (see also ?THM?
). However, since we have not worked out the equivalence between not having the order property and stability, we chose to work with the type counting definition that we gave.

7.3.2
Continuous logic, Section
7.2

In
[
BYBHU08
]
it is already mentioned that a continuous theory can be studied as a
compact abstract theory
, which is the name that
[
BY03a
,
BY03b
,
BY03c
]
use for what is essentially positive logic. In fact,
[
BY03a
, Section 2]
gives us a recipe that allows us to cook up a positive theory from the data of a continuous theory. In Section
7.2
we just work this out explicitly.

There is nothing special about the tree property in ?THM?
. We could translate various other combinatorial model-theoretic properties (such as
\mathsf{OP}
,
\mathsf{SOP_{1}}
,
\mathsf{SOP_{2}}
,
\mathsf{IP}
,
\mathsf{TP_{1}}
,
\mathsf{TP_{2}}
, etc.) in a similar fashion. That is, one takes their definition for positive logic (see e.g.,
[
DGK23
]
) and replaces the existence of an obstruction
\chi^{\prime}
of some formula
\chi
by the existence of an
\varepsilon>0
such that
\chi
has value at least
\varepsilon
wherever
\chi^{\prime}
was supposed to hold.

We could also translate the results concerning stability (i.e., Chapter
6
) similarly to how we translated those for simplicity (e.g., ?THM?
), but this already appears in print in
[
BYBHU08
, Section 14]
.

Appendix A
The lazy model-theoretician’s guide to positive logic

We give an as-brief-as-possible summary of the necessities for positive logic. The “proofs” here are just sketches or indications of the main ingredients in the actual arguments. Each definition, lemma, proposition and theorem refers to the relevant counterpart within the notes, where the details can be found.

A.1
Basics

Definitions of signature (or language), structure and first-order formula are the same as usual. We work in some fixed signature
\mathcal{L}
, which we often drop from the notation. We will not distinguish tuples from single elements, so the notation
a\in M
means that
a
is some tuple in
M
(and similarly for variables).

Definition A.1.1

(?THM?
)
.

A
positive formula
is one that is built from atomic formulas using
\top,\bot,\wedge,\vee
and
\exists
.

An
h-inductive sentence
is one of the form
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\psi(x))
, where
\varphi(x)
and
\psi(x)
are positive formulas. An
h-universal sentence
is an h-inductive sentence of the form
\forall x(\varphi(x)\to\bot)
.

A
positive theory
is a set of h-inductive sentences.

The signature does not necessarily contain a symbol for inequality.

Convention A.1.2

(Stay positive!)
.

We will drop the “positive” from terms from now on. That is, we will just say “formula” and “theory” instead of “positive formula” and “positive theory” respectively.

Definition A.1.3

(?THM?s
and
)
.

A function
f:M\to N
between structures is called a
homomorphism
if for every formula
\varphi(x)
and every
a\in M
we have:

M\models\varphi(a)\implies N\models\varphi(f(a)).

We call
f
an
immersion
if for every formula
\varphi(x)
and every
a\in M
we have:

M\models\varphi(a)\Longleftrightarrow N\models\varphi(f(a)).

The main objects of study in positive model theory are the positively closed models. In some literature these are also called existentially closed models (abbreviated as e.c. models) or positively existentially closed models (abbreviated as pec models).

Definition A.1.4

(?THM?s
and
)
.

We call a model
M
of a theory
T
a
positively closed model
, or
p.c. model
, if the following equivalent conditions hold:

(i)

every homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
is an immersion;

(ii)

for every
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
the following holds, if there is a homomorphism
f:M\to N
with
N\models T
and
N\models\varphi(f(a))
then already
M\models\varphi(a)
;

(iii)

for every
a\in M
and
\varphi(x)
such that
M\not\models\varphi(a)
there is
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
and
M\models\psi(a)
.

Proof of the above equivalence.

Proving (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is straightforward. For (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii), use compactness and the method of diagrams.
∎

It is useful to have a name for a formula like
\psi
in (iii) above.

Definition A.1.5

(?THM?
)
.

Let
T
be a theory and
\varphi(x)
be a formula. A formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\neg\exists x(\varphi(x)\wedge\psi(x))
is called an
obstruction of
\varphi(x)
.

What is called “an obstruction of a formula” here is called “a negation of a formula” in some literature.

Theorem A.1.6

(?THM?s
and
)
.

The class of models and the class of p.c. models of a theory
T
are both closed under unions of chains.

Proof.

Show that a formula holds in the union of a chain of structures if and only if it holds in some structure in the chain. The statement for the class of models then follows, after which the statement about p.c. models is an easy consequence.
∎

The links in the chains in the above statement are homomorphisms. For p.c. models these are then automatically immersions (and thus injective maps), for arbitrary models these may not be injective so “union” should not be taken literally (see ?THM?
for a precise definition). Furthermore, “chains” can equivalently be replaced by “directed systems”.

Theorem A.1.7

(?THM?
)
.

Every model
M
of a theory
T
can be continued to a p.c. model of
T
. That is, there is some p.c. model
N
of
T
with a homomorphism
f:M\to N
.

Proof.

Enumerate all possible formulas with parameters in
M
as
(\varphi_{i})_{i<\kappa}
. Inductively construct a chain of models
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
with
M_{0}=M
as follows: if there is a homomorphism
M_{i}\to N
with
N\models T
and
N\models\varphi_{i}
then set
M_{i+1}=N
, otherwise set
M_{i+1}=M_{i}
. Call the union of this chain
M^{1}
and repeat the process to find a chain
(M^{i})_{i<\omega}
such that its union is the desired
N
.
∎

Theorem A.1.8

(?THM?
)
.

Let
T
be a theory and let
\Sigma(x)
be a set of positive formulas. Suppose that for every finite
\Sigma_{0}(x)\subseteq\Sigma(x)
there is
M\models T
with
a\in M
such that
M\models\Sigma_{0}(a)
. Then there is a p.c. model
N
of
T
with
a\in N
such that
N\models\Sigma(a)
.

Proof.

First use the usual compactness and then continue to a p.c. model.
∎

The category of p.c. models satisfies the amalgamation property.

Proposition A.1.9

(?THM?
)
.

Let
M\xleftarrow{f}M_{0}\xrightarrow{g}M^{\prime}
be a span of p.c. models of some theory
T
. Then there exists a p.c. model
N
of
T
and
M\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}N\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
.

Proof.

Use compactness and the method of diagrams to find an amalgamation and then continue to a p.c. model.
∎

Definition A.1.10

(?THM?
)
.

For a theory
T
we let
|T|
be the cardinality of the set of formulas, up to logical equivalence.

Theorem A.1.11

(?THM?
)
.

Let
M
be a p.c. model of
T
and let
A\subseteq M
. Then there is a p.c. model
M_{0}\subseteq M
with
A\subseteq M_{0}
and
|M_{0}|\leq|A|+|T|
, such that the inclusion is an elementary embedding.

Proof.

The
M_{0}
exists by the usual Löwenheim-Skolem. Combine this with the fact that if
M_{1}\to M_{2}
is an immersion with
M_{2}
a p.c. model then
M_{1}
is a p.c. model.
∎

There are many equivalent characterisations of a complete theory in full first-order logic. These are no longer equivalent in positive logic, but the important analogous property the following.

Definition A.1.12

(?THM?s
and
)
.

A theory
T
is said to have the
joint continuation property
, or
JCP
, if the following equivalent conditions hold.

(i)

For any two models
M
and
M^{\prime}
of
T
there is a model
N
of
T
with homomorphisms
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
.

(ii)

For any two p.c. models
M
and
M^{\prime}
of
T
there is a model
N
of
T
with homomorphisms
M\to N\leftarrow M^{\prime}
.

(iii)

For any two h-universal sentences
\varphi
and
\psi
we have that
T\models\varphi\vee\psi
implies
T\models\varphi
or
T\models\psi
.

There are further characterisations similar to “
T=\Th(M)
for some model
M
” (see ?THM?
), but they require definitions that we skip here.

Proof of the above equivalence.

For (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii), use that every model continues to a p.c. model. For (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i) use compactness and the method of diagrams. For (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii) use that truth of h-universal sentences is reflected by homomorphisms.
∎

A.2
Monster model and indiscernible sequences

As is common in model theory, it is convenient to work in a monster model. The construction from full first-order logic of such a model goes through in positive logic (see ?THM?
). As usual, we need to fix a notion of smallness, for which we invite the reader to pick their favourite one (e.g., smaller than some fixed inaccessible cardinal).

Definition A.2.1

(?THM?
)
.

Let
T
be a theory with JCP. A
monster model
of
T
is a model
\mathfrak{M}
of
T
that is:

•

Positively closed
:
\mathfrak{M}
is a p.c. model of
T
.

•

Very homogeneous
: any partial immersion
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
with small domain extends to an automorphism on all of
\mathfrak{M}
, equivalently any two small tuples
a
and
b
in
\mathfrak{M}
satisfy the same set of formulas if and only if there is an automorphism
f
of
\mathfrak{M}
such that
f(a)=b
.

•

Very saturated
: any small set of formulas with parameters in
\mathfrak{M}
that is finitely satisfiable in
\mathfrak{M}
is satisfiable in
\mathfrak{M}
.

Convention A.2.2
.

From now on we work in a monster model
\mathfrak{M}
, so all p.c. models, tuples and sets are assumed to be small and to live in
\mathfrak{M}
.

Convention A.2.3
.

We generally omit the monster model
\mathfrak{M}
from the notation. We also fix the following notation. Everything is small unless explicitly mentioned otherwise.

•

For a tuple
a
and a set
B
we write

\tp(a/B)=\{\varphi(x,b):b\in B\text{ and }\models\varphi(a,b)\}

for the set of formulas over
B
that are satisfied by
a
, and we call this the
type of
a
over
B
.

•

We write
a\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}
to mean
\tp(a/B)=\tp(a^{\prime}/B)
.

•

We write
\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/B)
for the set of autmorphisms of
\mathfrak{M}
that fix
B
pointwise. So by homogeneity we have
a\equiv_{B}a^{\prime}
if and only if there is
f\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M}/B)
with
f(a)=a^{\prime}
.

We stress that a
type
for us is a maximally consistent set of formulas. That is, a set of formulas
p(x)
of the form
\tp(a/B)
. An arbitrary consistent set of formulas is called a
partial type
(see also Section
2.2
).

Sometimes smaller saturated p.c. models will be useful.

Definition A.2.4

(?THM?s
and
)
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal. A structure
M
is called
positively
\kappa
-saturated
if, for every
A\subseteq M
with
|A|<\kappa
, every set
\Sigma(x)
of formulas over
A
, with
|x|\leq\kappa
, that is finitely satisfiable in
M
is satisfiable in
M
.

Proposition A.2.5

(?THM?
)
.

Let
A
be any parameter set. Then for all
\kappa\geq|A|+|T|
there is a positively
\kappa^{+}
-saturated p.c. model
M
of
T
with
|M|\leq 2^{\kappa}
and
A\subseteq M
.

Proof.

By downward Löwenheim-Skolem we may assume
A
to be a p.c. model. Then inductively construct a chain of p.c. models such that the next link realises all finitely satisfiable sets of formulas over
<\kappa
parameters in the current link. A proper choice of cardinals in this process yields
M
as the union of this chain.
∎

Definition A.2.6

(?THM?
)
.

Let
B
be a set of parameters. An
indiscernible sequence over
B
is an infinite sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
such that for any
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
and
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}
in
I
we have

a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}\equiv_{B}a_{j_{1}}\ldots a_{j_{n}}.

We will also abbreviate this as a
B
-indiscernible sequence
.

Indiscernible sequences are often constructed by first constructing a very long sequence and then using the lemma below to find some indiscernible sequence that is
based on
the very long sequence.

Definition A.2.7

(?THM?
)
.

Write
\lambda_{\kappa}=\beth_{(2^{\kappa})^{+}}
for any cardinal
\kappa
and
\lambda_{T}=\lambda_{|T|}
.

Lemma A.2.8

(?THM?
)
.

Let
B
be any parameter set and let
\kappa
be any cardinal. Then for any sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\kappa
-tuples with
|I|\geq\lambda_{|T|+|B|+\kappa}
there is a
B
-indiscernible sequence
(a_{i}^{\prime})_{i<\omega}
such that for all
n<\omega
there are
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
in
I
with
a^{\prime}_{1}\ldots a^{\prime}_{n}\equiv_{B}a_{i_{1}}\ldots a_{i_{n}}
.

Proof.

Erdős-Rado.
∎

A.3
Boolean, (semi-)Hausdorff and thick

Definition A.3.1

(?THM?
)
.

Given a
positive fragment

\Delta
of our signature (i.e., a set of full first-order formulas, closed under sub-formulas, change of variables, conjunction and disjunction) we define the
(
\Delta
-)Morleyisation

\Mor(\Delta)
to be the following theory. We extend the signature by a relation symbol
R_{\varphi}(x)
for each
\varphi(x)\in\Delta
and let
\Mor(\Delta)
express that
R_{\varphi}(x)
and
\varphi(x)
are equivalent.

To make sure
\Mor(\Delta)
is a
positive
theory, the equivalence of
\varphi(x)
and
R_{\varphi}(x)
has to axiomatised by induction on the complexity of
\varphi(x)
(see ?THM?
).

By taking
\Delta
to be the set of all full first-order formulas, every full first-order formula becomes a positive formula (modulo
\Mor(\Delta)
). In this way we can study full first-order logic using positive logic.

Definition A.3.2

(?THM?s
and
)
.

We call a theory
T

Boolean
if the following equivalent conditions hold.

(i)

Every model of
T
is a p.c. model.

(ii)

Every homomorphism between models of
T
is an immersion.

(iii)

For every positive formula
\varphi(x)
there is a positive formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\neg\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow\psi(x))
.

(iv)

For every full first-order formula
\varphi(x)
there is a positive formula
\psi(x)
such that
T\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\leftrightarrow\psi(x))
.

(v)

Every homomorphism between models of
T
is an elementary embedding.

Proof of the above equivalence.

The implications (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (iv)
\Rightarrow
(v)
\Rightarrow
(i) are straightforward. For (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) one proves the following using compactness and the method of diagrams: if
\varphi(x)
is a full first-order formula whose truth is preserved by all homomorphisms of models of
T
then
\varphi(x)
is equivalent to a positive formula modulo
T
. Finally, (iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) is by induction on the complexity of the formula.
∎

Definition A.3.3

(?THM?
)
.

Let
T
be a theory with JCP. We call
T
:

•

Hausdorff
if for any two distinct types
p(x)
and
q(x)
there are
\varphi(x)\not\in p(x)
and
\psi(x)\not\in q(x)
such that
\models\forall x(\varphi(x)\vee\psi(x))
;

•

semi-Hausdorff
if equality of types is type-definable, so there is a partial type
\Omega(x,y)
such that
\tp(a)=\tp(b)
if and only if
\models\Omega(a,b)
;

•

thick
if being an indiscernible sequence is type-definable, so there is a partial type
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
such that a sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is indiscernible if and only if
\models\Theta((a_{i})_{i<\omega})
.

The above definitions can be made sense of for any theory
T
, so without assuming JCP. In this case we need to refer to all p.c. models of
T
rather than just the single monster model.

Proposition A.3.4

(?THM?s
,
,
,
, and
)
.

Boolean implies Hausdorff implies semi-Hausdorff implies thick. None of these implications are reversible and there are non-thick positive theories.

Proof.

For Boolean
\Rightarrow
Hausdorff pick any formula
\varphi(x)
that is in one type and not in the other and then
\neg\varphi(x)
takes the role of
\psi(x)
. To prove Hausdorff
\Rightarrow
semi-Hausdorff
\Rightarrow
thick we take

\{\varphi(x,y):\;\text{for all }a,b\text{ with }\tp(a)=\tp(b)\text{ we have }\models\varphi(a,b)\}

and

\bigcup\{\Omega(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{n}};x_{j_{1}},\ldots,x_{j_{n}}):n<\omega,i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}<\omega,j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}<\omega\}.

for
\Omega(x,y)
and
\Theta((x_{i})_{i<\omega})
respectively. Finally, the referenced examples show that none of the implications are reversible.
∎

Proposition A.3.5

(?THM?
)
.

A theory
T
with JCP is Hausdorff if and only if the following amalgamation property holds. For any span
M\xleftarrow{f}M_{0}\xrightarrow{g}M^{\prime}
of models of the theory

T^{\prime}=\{\chi\text{ an h-inductive sentence}:\mathfrak{M}\models\chi\}

there is an amalgam
M\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}N\xleftarrow{g^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
, so
f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}g
, with
N\models T^{\prime}
.

As before, there is a version of the above without assuming JCP.

Proof.

First use compactness to prove the following intermediate statement (see ?THM?
). For any
M\models T^{\prime}
and
a\in M
there is a type
p(x)
such that for any homomorphism
f:M\to N
where
N
is a p.c. model of
T^{\prime}
we have that
N\models p(f(a))
. The equivalence of this intermediate statement with the amalgamation property for models of
T^{\prime}
is a straightforward argument involving compactness and the method of diagrams.
∎

A.4
Simple theories

Definition A.4.1

(?THM?
)
.

Let
\Sigma(x,b)
be a set of formulas over
Cb
. We say that
\Sigma(x,b)

divides over
C
if there is a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{i}\equiv_{C}b
for all
i<\omega
such that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}\Sigma(x,b_{i})
is inconsistent.

Proposition A.4.2

(?THM?
)
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
;

(ii)

for every
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{0}=b
there is a
Ca
-indiscernible sequence
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\omega}\equiv_{Cb}(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
;

(iii)

for every
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{0}=b
there is
a^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}a
such that
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
Ca^{\prime}
-indiscernible.

Proof.

This mostly comes down to moving things around with automorphisms. For (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (iii) we also use compactness to elongate sequences so that we can base new indiscernible sequences on them.
∎

Definition A.4.3

(?THM?s
and
)
.

Assume thickness. Let
a
and
a^{\prime}
be two tuples of the same length and let
B
be any parameter set. We say that
a
and
a^{\prime}
have the same
Lascar strong type over
B
and write
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{B}a^{\prime}
if the following equivalent conditions hold.

(i)

There are
a=a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}=a^{\prime}
such that
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
are on a
B
-indiscernible sequence for all
0\leq i<n
.

(ii)

There are
a=a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}=a^{\prime}
and positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. models
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
such that
a_{i}\equiv_{M_{i}}a_{i+1}
for all
0\leq i<n
.

Proof of the above equivalence.

We need the following fact: given
C\supseteq B
and any
B
-indiscernible sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
there is
C^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime}\equiv_{B}C
such that
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
C^{\prime}
-indiscernible (see ?THM?
). This is proved by elongating the original sequence, basing a new indiscernible sequence on it and applying an automorphism. To prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) we let
M\supseteq B
be a positively
\lambda_{T}
-saturated p.c. model and repeatedly apply the preceding fact with
M
in the role of
C
. The converse is a compactness argument using thickness (see ?THM?
).
∎

We assumed thickness because we will only need Lascar strong types in that context. Another equivalent definition of Lascar strong types can be given in terms of bounded invariant equivalence relations. The equivalence of that condition to (i) above holds in any theory (see ?THM?
).

Definition A.4.4

(?THM?
)
.

An
independence relation

\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is a ternary relation on small subsets of the monster model. If
A
,
B
and
C
are in the relation we write

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}B,

which should be read as “
A
is independent from
B
over
C
”. We also allow tuples in the relation, which are then interpreted as the set they enumerate.

Definition A.4.5

(?THM?
)
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation. We define the following properties for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
, where
a
and
b
are arbitrary tuples and
C
is an arbitrary set.

invariance

For any
f\in\Aut(\mathfrak{M})
we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
implies
f(a)\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{f(C)}f(b)
.

monotonicity

For any
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
and
b^{\prime}\subseteq b
we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
implies
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
.

normality

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then
Ca\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}Cb
.

existence

We always have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}C
.

full existence

There is always
b^{\prime}
with
b^{\prime}\equiv_{C}b
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
.

base monotonicity

If
C\subseteq C^{\prime}\subseteq b
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
implies
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}b
.

extension

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then for any
d
there is
d^{\prime}
with
d^{\prime}\equiv_{Cb}d
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bd^{\prime}
.

symmetry

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
then
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}a
.

transitivity

If
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
with
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}C^{\prime}
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}b
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
.

finite character

If for all finite
a^{\prime}\subseteq a
and all finite
b^{\prime}\subseteq b
we have
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b^{\prime}
then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
.

local character

For every cardinal
\kappa
there is a cardinal
\lambda
such that for all
a
with
|a|<\kappa
and any
C
there is
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<\lambda
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C^{\prime}}C
.

independence theorem

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}c
with
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
then there is
a^{\prime\prime}
with
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cb}a
and
a^{\prime\prime}\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{Cc}a^{\prime}
such that
a^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}bc
.

stationarity

For any
C
such that
a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}
implies
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
for all
a,a^{\prime}
, we have that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}b
and
a\equiv_{C}a
implies
a\equiv_{Cb}a^{\prime}
.

Definition A.4.6

(?THM?
)
.

Let
A,B,C
be sets and let
a
and
b
enumerate
A
and
B
respectively. Then we write

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}B

if
\tp(a/Cb)
does not divide over
C
. We call this relation
dividing independence
.

Theorem A.4.7

(?THM?
)
.

Dividing independence satisfies the following properties:
invariance
,
monotonicity
,
normality
,
existence
,
base monotonicity
,
finite character
and
left transitivity
. This final property is the same as
transitivity
with the sides of the independence relation swapped: if
C\subseteq C^{\prime}
then
C^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C^{\prime}}b
implies
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}b
.

Proof.

All of this is standard manipulation of indiscernible sequences.
∎

Definition A.4.8

(?THM?
)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be a natural number. A formula
\varphi(x,y)
is said to have the
k
-tree property
(
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
) if there are
(a_{\eta})_{\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}}
and an obstruction
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
of the formula
\exists x(\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k}))
such that:

(i)

for all
\sigma\in\omega^{\omega}
the set
\{\varphi(x,a_{\sigma|_{n}}):n<\omega\}
is consistent,

(ii)

for all
\eta\in\omega^{<\omega}
and
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<\omega
we have
\models\psi(a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{1}},\ldots,a_{\eta^{\frown}i_{k}})
.

A formula
\varphi(x,y)
has the
tree property
(
\mathsf{TP}
) if there exists a natural number
k\geq 2
such that
\varphi(x,y)
has
k
-
\mathsf{TP}
.

A theory has the
tree property
(
\mathsf{TP}
) if there is a formula that has the tree property, and otherwise it is
\mathsf{NTP}
.

Theorem A.4.9

(?THM?
)
.

A theory
T
is
\mathsf{NTP}
if and only if dividing independence
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
local character
.

There are more detailed statements in ?THM?
, referring to the exact cardinals involved in
local character
.

Proof.

Both directions are proved by contrapositive. Assuming
\mathsf{TP}
, say as witnessed by
\varphi(x,y)
, one first uses compactness to make the tree as big as necessary (this is why (ii) in ?THM?
is important). Then inductively construct a branch in the tree, which results in a sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
and a realisation
a
of
\{\varphi(x,b_{i}):i<\lambda\}
(see ?THM?
(i)) such that
\tp(a/(b_{i})_{i<\lambda})
divides over
(b_{i})_{i<\gamma}
for all
\gamma<\lambda
, contradicting
local character
. The sequence witnessing dividing is found every time as a subsequence of the immediate successors of each
b_{i}
.

Conversely, failure of
local character
implies that there are
a
and
C
such that
\tp(a/C)
does not divide over
C^{\prime}
for all
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
with
|C^{\prime}|<|T|^{+}
. We use this to inductively build a tree of height
|T|^{+}
whose zero branch consists of tuples in
C
and such that all branches have the same type. In the induction step we let
C^{\prime}
be the zero branch constructed so far, so
\tp(a/C)
divides over
C^{\prime}
. This yields an indiscernible sequence along which some
\psi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
holds that is an obstruction of
\exists x(\varphi(x,y_{1})\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi(x,y_{k}))
for some
\varphi(x,C)\in\tp(a/C)
. This indiscernible sequence will form the next level above the zero branch, and automorphic copies complete the levels above the other branches. As the tree has height
|T|^{+}
we can use the pigeonhole principle the restrict to a subtree with a uniform choice of
\varphi
and
\psi
, which is exactly saying that
\varphi(x,y)
has
\mathsf{TP}
.
∎

Definition A.4.10

(?THM?
)
.

We call a theory
T

simple
if the equivalent conditions from ?THM?
hold.

Theorem A.4.11

(?THM?
)
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is simple then dividing independence has
full existence
.

Proof.

This is a long and technical proof. The key is that the thickness assumption makes it so that a certain technical concept, namely that of a dividing sequence, type-definable. We need infinite dividing sequences, but to construct these we only need to deal with finite dividing sequences by type-definability and compactness.
∎

The importance of
full existence
is that we can build Morley sequences and prove Kim’s lemma (?THM?
).

Definition A.4.12

(?THM?
)
.

A
Morley sequence (over
C
)
is a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
a_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}(a_{j})_{j<i}
for all
i\in I
.

Proposition A.4.13

(?THM?
)
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is simple then for any
a
and
C
there is a Morley sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
over
C
with
a_{0}=a
.

Proof.

Use
full existence
to find a long enough
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}_{C}
-independent sequence (i.e., a Morley sequence without the indiscernibility). Then base a
C
-indiscernible sequence on it.
∎

Theorem A.4.14

(?THM?
)
.

Suppose that
T
is simple and let
\Sigma(x,b)
be a set of formulas over
Cb
. If
\bigcup_{i<\omega}\Sigma(x,b_{i})
is consistent for some Morley sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
over
C
with
b_{0}=b
then
\Sigma(x,b)
does not divide over
C
.

In particular, assuming thickness, we have that
\Sigma(x,b)
divides over
C
if and only if there is a Morley sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
with
b_{0}=b
such that
\bigcup_{i<\omega}\Sigma(x,b_{i})
is inconsistent.

Proof.

The first part is proved by usual argument that relies on
local character
. The second part then follows from the existence of Morley sequences (?THM?
).
∎

Theorem A.4.15

(?THM?s
and
)
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is simple then given a partial type
\Sigma(x,b)
that does not divide over
C
there is a type
p(x,b)\supseteq\Sigma(x,b)
that does not divide over
C
.

In particular, dividing independence satisfies
extension
.

Proof.

Take a Morley sequence over
C
starting with
b
and use compactness to elongate it to
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
. By non-dividing of
\Sigma(x,b)
there is a realisation
a
of
\bigcup_{i<\lambda}\Sigma(x,b_{i})
. By the pigeonhole principle there is an infinite subsequence
I\subseteq\lambda
such that
ab_{i}\equiv_{C}ab_{j}
for all
i,j\in I
, so taking
\tp(a/Cb_{i})
for
i\in I
results in the required type.
∎

The properties
symmetry
,
transivitiy
and
independence theorem
also hold in thick simple theories (see ?THM?s
,
, and
). We summarise everything in a Kim-Pillay style theorem. The proofs of all these theorems are analogous to the full first-order setting.

Theorem A.4.16

(?THM?
)
.

Assume thickness. A theory
T
is simple if and only if there is an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfying
invariance
,
monotonicity
,
normality
,
existence
,
full existence
,
base monotonicity
,
extension
,
symmetry
,
transitivity
,
finite character
,
local character
and
independence theorem
. Furthermore, in this case,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
.

We have decided to only treat simplicity for thick theories, which simplifies the treatment and allows us to stay closer to the treatment in full first-order logic. This still captures a large class of positive theories, see for example
[
Kam24
, Section 2]
for a list of (classes of) examples of thick theories. Even though much is still possible without the thickness assumption,
full existence
may fail
[
BY03b
, Example 4.3]
and the treatment of simplicity becomes much more complicated (see also ?THM?
).

A.5
Stable theories

Definition A.5.1

(?THM?
)
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite cardinal. A theory
T
is called
\lambda
-stable
if for all parameter sets
B
with
|B|\leq\lambda
there are at most
\lambda
many types in finitely many variables over
B
. We call
T

stable
if it is
\lambda
-stable for some
\lambda
