.

Definition A.5.2

(?THM?
)
.

Let
p(x)
be a type over
B
and let
\varphi(x,y)
be a formula without parameters. A
\varphi
-definition over
C
of
p(x)
is a set of formulas
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
over
C
with
|\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)|\leq|T|
such that for all
b\in B
we have

\varphi(x,b)\in p(x)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\models\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(b).

We say that
p(x)
is
definable over
C
if it has a
\varphi
-definition over
C
for every formula
\varphi(x,y)
. If
p(x)
is definable over
B
then we just say that
p(x)
is
definable
.

Definition A.5.3

(?THM?s
and
)
.

A formula
\varphi(x,y)
is said to have the
binary tree property
if there is an obstruction
\psi(x,y)
and parameters
(b_{\eta})_{\eta\in 2^{<\omega}}
such that for all
\sigma\in 2^{\omega}
the set

\{\varphi(x,b_{\sigma|_{n}}):\sigma(n)=0\}\cup\{\psi(x,b_{\sigma|_{n}}):\sigma(n)=1\}

is consistent.

Definition A.5.4

(?THM?
)
.

A formula
\varphi(x,y)
has the
order property
(
\mathsf{OP}
) if there are sequences
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
and
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
and an obstruction
\psi(x,y)
of
\varphi(x,y)
such that for all
i,j<\omega
, we have

\displaystyle\models\varphi(a_{i},b_{j})

\displaystyle\text{if }i<j,

\displaystyle\models\psi(a_{i},b_{j})

\displaystyle\text{if }i\geq j.

Theorem A.5.5

(?THM?s
and
)
.

The following are equivalent for a theory
T
:

(i)

T
is stable,

(ii)

no formula has the binary tree property,

(iii)

every type is definable,

(iv)

T
is
\lambda
-stable for every
\lambda
with
\lambda^{|T|}=\lambda
,

(v)

no formula has the order property.

Proof.

The proofs closely follow the standard proofs from full first-order logic, only (v)
\Rightarrow
(i) is much more involved (and is not treated in these notes, see Section
6.5
and
[
DGK23
, Lemma 3.18]
). For the proofs of (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (i)
\Rightarrow
(v) one proves the contrapositive by constructing many different types. For (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) the key insight is that having the binary tree property (with respect to a fixed
\psi
) is type-definable. So by compactness we get a finite bound on the height of such trees. The existence of such a finite binary tree (again, with respect to fixed
\psi
) can be expressed by just a formula. Taking these formulas, while
\psi
ranges over the obstructions of
\varphi
, yields the required definition
\operatorname{d}_{p}\varphi(y)
. Finally, for (iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) we simply count the number of possible definitions over a fixed parameter set, which bounds the number of possible types.
∎

It is also possible to establish the above equivalences on a formula-by-formula basis (e.g., a formula does not have the binary tree property if and only if it does not have the order property), which is done in
[
DGK23
, Theorem 3.11]
.

Theorem A.5.6

(?THM?
)
.

Every stable theory is simple.

Proof.

First show that, roughly, definable types do not divide over the parameter set over which they are definable (see ?THM?
). Then note that there is a bound on the cardinality of the parameters that are needed to define a type, and so we get
local character
for dividing independence.
∎

Definition A.5.7

(?THM?
)
.

A
stationary type
is a type
p(x)=\tp(a/C)
that admits exactly one non-dividing extension to any parameter set. That is, for any
B\supseteq C
, there is a type
p^{\prime}(x)\supseteq p(x)
over
B
such that:

(i)

p^{\prime}(x)
does not divide over
C
;

(ii)

for any type
r(x)\supseteq p(x)
over
B
that does not divide over
C
we have
r(x)=p^{\prime}(x)
.

Theorem A.5.8

(?THM?
)
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is a stable theory then
\tp(a/C)
is stationary if and only if we have for all
a^{\prime}
that
a\equiv_{C}a^{\prime}
implies
a\equiv^{\textup{Ls}}_{C}a^{\prime}
. In particular,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
stationarity
in stable theories.

Proof.

First we note that
T
is simple, and so we can use all the properties of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
as summarised in ?THM?
. For the left to right we extend
\tp(a/C)
to a global type, which can be shown to be
C
-invariant (see ?THM?
), from which the conclusion follows in a standard manner (see ?THM?
). For the converse we assume for a contradiction that there are two distinct non-dividing extensions of
\tp(a/C)
. Then, mainly using the
independence theorem
for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
, we can inductively build a binary tree of non-dividing distinct types, ultimately yielding many distinct types and contradicting stability.
∎

Theorem A.5.9

(?THM?
)
.

Assume thickness. If
T
is simple and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
satisfies
stationarity
then
T
is stable.

Proof.

By
local character
of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
there is a bound
\lambda
, such that any type (in finitely many variables) does not divide over some parameter set of cardinality
\leq\lambda
. At the same time, for any type
p(x)
over a parameter set of cardinality
\leq\lambda
there is a bound on the number of non-dividing extensions, by
stationarity
. Combining this yields the type counting definition of stability.
∎

Theorem A.5.10

(?THM?
)
.

Assume thickness. A theory
T
is stable if and only if there is an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfying
invariance
,

. Furthermore, in this case,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{d}
.

Proof.

This is just piecing together previous results, mainly relying on ?THM?
, with as the only new ingredient that
stationarity
implies
independence theorem
(see ?THM?
).
